#### CB.2:5.3 - Willingness does not supply missing competence

Several people volunteer to help with a case, but none can explain how the proposed diagnostic method is justified. The organiser closes the request with that limitation and seeks a qualified source or method-development contribution. Participants can still help formulate the question and identify missing observations. Their enthusiasm does not make the technical answer reliable.

#### CB.2:5.4 - A first social connection can be enough

At a small neighbourhood reading gathering, two newcomers want conversation rather than critique of their reading ability. The host introduces a shared question, makes room for both to speak and checks whether they want another meeting. They agree to exchange recommendations. No completed review or progression into organising work is required for this exchange to be useful.

### CB.2:6 - Bias-Annotation

Accounts of successful communities can hide the founder's preparation, recruitment and follow-up, while leaving unsuccessful attempts out of view. Keep the actual support demand and missing outcomes visible. Visible, fluent contributors can also dominate early matching; distinguish demonstrated suitability for the specific request from confidence or frequency of posting.

### CB.2:7 - Conformance Checklist

A defensible first exchange has:

- a useful result and recipient appropriate to the purpose;
- contributions chosen for their relevance, competence, willingness and feasible effort;
- sufficiently prepared and permissible material;
- understandable participation and response conditions;
- a result or limitation returned to the people involved;
- a continuation decision that accounts for organising support and the limits of the observations.

A proposed exchange may satisfy the planning conditions while its practical outcome remains unknown. Preserve that distinction when deciding whether to repeat or enlarge it.

### CB.2:8 - Common Anti-Patterns and How to Avoid Them

| Common wrong turn | Practical repair |
| --- | --- |
| Invite a large audience and wait for useful conversation | Arrange one relevant request and the contributions needed to answer it; expand only where the activity can still be supported. |
| Ask volunteers to “engage more” | Name a worthwhile action, the person it helps and its actual effort; make refusal informative and permissible. |
| Select helpers by visible status alone | Establish their suitability, availability and permission for the specific question. |
| Treat every answer as equally valid to maintain harmony | Compare the assumptions and domain basis; retain uncertainty or seek a qualified contribution where needed. |
| The organiser answers, prompts and follows up everything, then declares the community independent | Identify the organising contributions, retain necessary support and test a specific redistribution. |
| Count a useful discussion as successful application or learning | Obtain the relevant later observation before making the stronger claim. |

### CB.2:9 - Consequences

Participants obtain a real opportunity to contribute and receive something useful. The organiser learns which matching, preparation and follow-up work matters, and can adapt it before creating a larger recurring commitment.

The approach requires hands-on attention and can establish that the proposed activity is not currently viable. A small successful exchange leaves larger-scale capacity, durability, learning and causal benefit as separate questions.

### CB.2:10 - Rationale

An early exchange is often blocked by several modest conditions at once: an unclear question, unknown competence, incompatible availability or an absent return. Deliberate matching and preparation make these conditions tractable. A bounded result keeps the commitment understandable and exposes what the interaction supplied.

Closing the return matters because a contributor otherwise may never learn whether the response could be used. Recording organising support matters for a different reason: it shows which work continuation must preserve. Neither requires equal repayment, compulsory activity or a fixed growth path.

### CB.2:11 - SoTA-Echoing

| Source line or developed treatment | Adopted or adapted contribution | Boundary and effect on the method |
| --- | --- | --- |
| David Spinks, The Business of Belonging (2021), physical PDF pp.68–76 and 108–111 | Start with a manageable scope, recruit deliberately and supply early organising work where spontaneous exchange is absent. | The life-cycle stages, activity percentages and growth stories are historical heuristics and cases, not universal thresholds. The method chooses support from the actual exchange. |
| Spinks, physical PDF pp.77–100 | Make the intended participation recognisable and inspect who is missing from the first group. | Identity and belonging can matter without making increasing activity every person's required trajectory. |
| OCE.2's treatment of actual work, supplied results and constraints | Identify the work that makes an exchange possible and retain uncertainty about unavailable contributions. | CB adapts this to independent participants and voluntary contribution; it does not infer positions or authority from participation. |
| HCD.5/6's treatment of helped performance and later observation | Keep receiving help, performing an action and developing capability distinguishable. | Use learning methods when learning is the purpose. A community interaction alone supplies no learning verdict. |

Source return: [David Spinks, The Business of Belonging (2021)](https://www.wiley-vch.de/en/areas-interest/finance-economics-law/the-business-of-belonging-978-1-119-76612-4). The construction retains the useful manual-start proposal while replacing generic activity targets with the participants' result, acceptable burden and actual support conditions. Prefer an adequate resource, professional service or independent-learning route when it supplies the receiving use more directly.

### CB.2:12 - Relations

- **Uses and returns to CB.1:** the contribution, participation terms and serious alternatives. Failure to obtain the central contribution reopens that choice.
- **Uses [OCE.2 (OCE.2:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/ORGANIZATION-CHANGE-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#oce24---solution)** when a relied-on organisational contribution, access or decision relation needs evidence. The participants' actual commitments remain separate from organisation labels.
- **Uses [HCD.5:4 and HCD.6](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Human%20Capability%20Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/HUMAN-CAPABILITY-DEVELOPMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#hcd54---solution)** when the intended contribution includes learning or representative practice.
- **Uses [FPF C.36](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/FPF-Spec.md#c36---cultural-evolution-and-cultural-evolution-engineering)** when the next question concerns how an exchanged practice is transmitted, retained, selected or renewed. Preserving a case is one possible contribution to that larger work.

### CB.2:End

## CB.3 - Make Entry and Changes of Participation Workable

> **Type:** Community-building method pattern
> **Status:** Stable

### CB.3:1 - Problem frame

Use this pattern when a person wants to enter, return to, or change their participation in a community, but the next useful action or its conditions are unclear. A newcomer reads the discussions without knowing whether a question is welcome. An experienced practitioner is invited to host a meeting without knowing the work involved. Someone returning after an absence finds that familiar people, tools and rules have changed.

The organiser helps the person obtain a form of participation that fits their purpose and present conditions. That can mean reading a useful resource, receiving help, making a small contribution, taking responsibility for an activity, or leaving a previous responsibility with a workable handover. Support can begin before any visible contribution.

**First useful result:** the person can choose and attempt an understandable next action, knows where a response or help will come from, and can reduce or decline the proposed involvement. An identified unavailable condition is a useful return when it prevents that action.

The organiser needs a credible account of what the arrangement offers, such as CB.1 supplies, and access to the people who can provide the promised help. The person's relevant domain knowledge may already be sufficient; difficulty entering a space does not establish a need for professional training.

**Ordinary non-use.** If the person already has a suitable way to participate and is using it, leave it available. If the central question is whether the community contribution is worthwhile, use CB.1. If an established organisational contribution has failed and its cause is unclear, OCE.10 supplies the deeper inquiry into the work episode.

### CB.3:2 - Problem

An invitation may be easy to send and difficult to accept. The person may face unfamiliar language, a public-disclosure demand, inaccessible material, uncertain permission, an unsuitable schedule or a commitment larger than they can sustain. An organiser can misread any of these as lack of motivation.

How can the organiser make useful participation obtainable while allowing people to contribute differently, receive help first, change their involvement and return after absence?

### CB.3:3 - Forces

| Force | Decision it changes |
| --- | --- |
| Clear guidance and personal choice | A concrete first action reduces uncertainty; a compulsory public introduction can exclude the person it was meant to welcome. |
| Relevant entry conditions and avoidable barriers | Some actions need competence or permission; unrelated hurdles consume effort without protecting that action. |
| Available help and organising capacity | A welcome creates an expectation of response which someone must be able to meet. |
| Continuity and changing circumstances | Familiar conventions ease return, while changed tools, responsibilities or personal commitments may require a new arrangement. |
| Recognition and actual access | Being told one belongs can matter, but does not provide a missing permission, response or opportunity. |

### CB.3:4 - Solution

Recover the action the person wants to make possible, establish the conditions that affect it, and offer a small workable choice with an available response. Observe the attempted use and change the obstacle or promise that the episode exposes.

**Working reminder:** find the wanted action; make its conditions visible; offer an attainable start; provide the promised response; support a change or return.

#### CB.3:4.1 - Recover where entry becomes difficult

Ask about one recent or imminent occasion: what brought the person here, what they hoped to obtain or contribute, what they could understand, and where the attempt stopped. Walk through the relevant path with their permission. A useful path might run from an invitation to finding a suitable question, preparing permitted material, obtaining help and knowing what to do afterwards. Inspect only the parts that can change this participation.

Compare the person's account with the arrangement they actually encounter. A link can work for the organiser and fail for an outside participant. A newcomer can understand the instructions yet expect that a basic question will be mocked. A returning member can have a valid account but no longer have the time required by an old responsibility.

Keep action-changing distinctions visible. Formal membership may permit access to a space; it does not establish that the person identifies with the group, feels welcome, is recognised by others or has performed the expected work. Find which of these is the live question before changing an entry condition. Often the useful correction is concrete: provide readable material, explain what question is welcome, obtain a response or clarify permission.

Use OCE.10 when an actual work episode needs comparison of rival explanations—for example, lack of access, unavailable time, uncertain role expectations or a punishing response to questions. Its inquiry and qualified return can prevent the organiser from treating a design defect as an individual attitude.

#### CB.3:4.2 - Offer forms of participation that serve the person

Choose alternatives by what the person wants to do and what the activity requires. The following forms can coexist and change over time; they are not ranks.

| Wanted participation | A feasible entry | Conditions to establish |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Find a known answer | Read or search an applicable resource | The material is findable, understandable and accessible under its actual use conditions. |
| Understand what happens here | Observe a permitted activity or inspect a worked example | The observer knows the purpose, sharing limits and how to ask later; the other participants accept the observation where required. |
| Obtain help | Bring a bounded question, privately or with preparation if necessary | Someone can help formulate it and a suitable response route is available. |
| Contribute experience or a result | Respond to one relevant request or offer a small piece of work | The contribution has a recipient, a useful scope and an attainable quality condition. |
| Organise or maintain an activity | Prepare and try the relevant organising work with support | The person understands the work, has the needed capability and resources, and accepts the actual responsibility. |

A person can enter with substantial capability and begin with a demanding contribution. Another can continue benefiting through quiet use. Do not make access to a useful answer depend on a public performance that the answer does not require. Where the arrangement has a legitimate membership or confidentiality condition, explain its purpose and offer the accessible alternative, if one exists.

For each real option, make the requested effort and available response concrete. “You may ask privately; the volunteer host checks requests on the agreed day” is assessable. “Someone will always help” needs capacity the organiser may not have. Let the person ask for a smaller contribution or say that none of the options fits.

#### CB.3:4.3 - Remove the barrier that does not serve the work

Trace an entry demand to the action it protects. A technical intervention may require demonstrated competence and permission. A private case discussion may require an agreed disclosure boundary. A requirement to publish a long biography before reading an ordinary reference may add no useful protection.

Remove or reduce unnecessary effort. Put the first action near the invitation, explain unfamiliar terms with an example, avoid asking for the same information repeatedly, and let people obtain the relevant material in a usable form. Preserve requirements whose removal would make the contribution unfit or impermissible. If the organiser does not control a needed access condition, obtain a decision from its owner or return the limitation.

Test the resulting path from the participant's conditions. Can they find the material, understand the first request and reach the promised help? Include the language, device, schedule, physical access or confidentiality condition that caused the difficulty. An administrator's successful demonstration is insufficient when the participant faces a different condition.

Keep a meaningful refusal available. Explain any consequence of declining a specific responsibility. In an employment or service arrangement, distinguish an existing obligation from optional community participation and obtain the relevant agreement; calling a required act voluntary does not change its terms.

#### CB.3:4.4 - Provide support before demanding visible contribution

Offer help for the actual next action. A newcomer might need to see an example of an answerable question, prepare a permitted description with a peer, or speak with someone who can explain an unfamiliar convention. Match the help to the gap rather than requiring a general orientation programme.

Ask what the person wants to try and what makes it difficult. Give a reason for a necessary condition, real alternatives where they exist, and feedback that helps the person improve the attempt. Make the response usable: who will answer, within which accepted window, and how to get another route if that help is unavailable. The support requires capable, willing people and real time.

Sometimes the obstacle is the person's interpretation of an ordinary transition difficulty. After checking the actual conditions, a relevant, truthful account from a more experienced participant can show how a similar difficulty was overcome and where help was obtained. Invite the newcomer to consider whether that account fits their situation; they may reflect privately or choose to contribute advice for later newcomers. There is no requirement to disclose personal experience.

Use such accounts only when the available opportunities support them. If mistakes are actually ridiculed, correct that practice and its consequences. If no one answers newcomers, obtain the missing response. A reassuring story cannot make rejection or absent support imaginary.

Check both the usefulness of the help and its experienced terms. A completed introduction may have felt coercive or consumed more effort than the person could afford. Silence can also mean that quiet use is sufficient. Ask where the answer can change the next action, and respect a decision not to participate further.

#### CB.3:4.5 - Prepare changes of participation and return

When a person takes a different responsibility, explain the work it adds, the decisions they may make, the help they can obtain and how they can stop or hand it over. Skill in the community's subject does not automatically supply facilitation, moderation or maintenance capability. Obtain the missing preparation through a capable colleague or the relevant HCD contribution.

For a bounded first attempt, agree what the person will do, what support will remain present and what result or difficulty they will return. Choose that attempt from the responsibility itself. Someone preparing to host a case exchange can rehearse opening the question, separating clarification from advice and closing with the recipient's next step. The support provider remains responsible for the help they accepted.

Use OCE.6 when a real organisational appointment, resource allocation, permission or enabling relation must be established. A community invitation can propose the contribution; the appropriate owner establishes the relevant authority and access. For informal voluntary activity, a clear accepted arrangement can suffice where no formal assignment is required.

On return after absence, recover what has changed that affects the next action: the current material, contact, convention, access or responsibility. Supply a short update and a suitable next use. Do not require repeating unaffected orientation or explaining a private reason for absence. If the old contribution is no longer wanted or feasible, agree a smaller one or its end.

When stepping back would leave a relied-on activity unsupported, arrange the handover or change the activity's promise. Permission to leave remains meaningful; continuity requires another feasible contribution, not an indefinite obligation on the departing person.

#### CB.3:4.6 - Close on usable participation and revise locally

Observe whether the person could carry out the selected action under the offered conditions. Distinguish receiving the invitation, entering a space, obtaining help and producing a useful contribution. Ask what remains confusing or costly when that answer can improve the arrangement.

Continue a working option without requiring increased visibility. Change the smallest defeated condition: clarify the request, repair access, change timing, obtain a suitable helper or revise the responsibility. Return to CB.1 when the available offer itself fails to serve the person's purpose. Use CB.2 for an initial exchange, CB.4 for its interaction design and CB.5 for a specific useful contribution.

An available entry can be recognised in an understandable invitation, material and response path. A stronger claim that people actually participate or feel accepted needs observations appropriate to that claim and those people. Registration, repeated attendance and a welcome message establish different things.

### CB.3:5 - Archetypal Grounding

#### CB.3:5.1 - Three repairers need different entries

In this constructed continuation of the independent-repairer example, the association initially asks every newcomer to create a public profile, describe a customer case and attend a live welcome session.

One repairer needs a known procedure and has no wish to discuss it. The relevant reference can lawfully be read without a personal account. The organiser makes it directly accessible and supplies its applicability and correction route. The person obtains the wanted result without becoming visibly active.

A second repairer wants help but cannot publish the customer's material. A willing host offers a short private preparation within an agreed window. Together they determine which observations can be shared; if none suffice, the case uses the service provider's protected route. If a permitted description is possible, the repairer can ask a bounded question. A public introduction would not have supplied this condition.

A third newcomer is experienced in diagnosis and offers to lead a discussion. The organiser establishes that the person has not facilitated a case clinic before. They prepare a small session with an experienced host, practise the clarification/advice boundary and agree which work the host will retain. Subject expertise makes a valuable contribution without proving readiness for every organising responsibility.

The repaired entry is a set of relevant choices. The organiser observes whether each promised route works. Counting three public profiles would conceal the different results.

#### CB.3:5.2 - A first error can lead to another attempt

A newcomer in a voluntary reading group misinterprets an unfamiliar annotation convention and concludes that they are unsuited to the group. The host first checks the event: the convention was unexplained, and a willing reader can help. With that reader's permission, the host offers a brief account of a similar early mistake and shows the corrected example. The newcomer chooses a small second attempt and receives a useful response.

In a changed case, an established member has mocked newcomers' mistakes. The host addresses that behaviour and the protection of questions before using a reassuring story. The two cases require different repairs even though both people express the same worry.

#### CB.3:5.3 - Return and handover preserve a choice

A volunteer who used to welcome newcomers returns after three months with less available time. The old role included both introductions and checking unanswered questions. The group now has another person for introductions, while question checking remains unsupported. They agree a smaller fortnightly contribution and revise the advertised response window. The returning volunteer receives only the changed material and current contact details.

If nobody accepts the remaining work, the organiser reduces the offer or obtains another provider. The previous volunteer's membership does not supply an unlimited continuing commitment.

### CB.3:6 - Bias-Annotation

A growth-oriented account can treat quiet participants as an unfinished version of active contributors. The method instead starts from the person's wanted use and the arrangement's legitimate requirements. Organisers who know the space well can also underestimate unfamiliarity; checking a real entry from the participant's conditions exposes that difference.

### CB.3:7 - Conformance Checklist

A workable entry or change of participation makes it possible to answer:

- What useful action does the person want, and which condition currently matters?
- Which offered form fits their purpose, capability and available effort?
- Which entry demands protect that action, and which can be removed?
- Who will provide the promised response or help?
- What responsibility, permission or support changes when participation changes?
- What did the attempted use establish, and what remains unavailable?

Use the answers to improve the action. A completed orientation checklist alone does not establish access, belonging or capability.

### CB.3:8 - Common Anti-Patterns and How to Avoid Them

| Wrong turn | Practical repair |
| --- | --- |
| Public self-presentation is required before any useful help | Offer the smallest relevant entry and obtain only information needed for it. |
| Quiet use is treated as a motivation defect | Ask whether it meets the person's purpose before prescribing more activity. |
| A reassuring message covers real exclusion or missing support | Change the actual response, access or protection condition. |
| Subject expertise is treated as organising competence | Prepare and observe the new responsibility with appropriate support. |
| Returning members must repeat everything | Supply the changes that affect their next use. |
| A volunteer can leave only after finding their own replacement | Put continuity work with the organiser and revise the unsupported promise. |

### CB.3:9 - Consequences

People can receive value before a large commitment, choose useful forms of participation and change them as circumstances change. The organiser obtains specific return questions about access, help and responsibility instead of one unexplained engagement problem.

The work consumes preparation and response capacity. Offering many options without capable support can make entry less reliable; retain only options the arrangement can supply.

### CB.3:10 - Rationale

Entry is a small use of the actual arrangement. If its first action depends on unavailable material, an unexplained convention or an absent response, persuasion leaves the dependency intact. Following that action reveals which change can help.

Choice and support work together. A bare choice among infeasible options supplies little autonomy; help tied to an unwanted public commitment can undermine it. Making a wanted action understandable, feasible and answerable allows participation to develop without prescribing one trajectory.

### CB.3:11 - SoTA-Echoing

The practical comparison is between a generic induction or escalating participation demand and an entry built around the person's wanted action and its actual conditions.

| Source line or developed treatment | Contribution used | Limit that changes use |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Spinks, The Business of Belonging (2021), physical PDF 102–118 | Proportion the invitation, make small entries intelligible, support quiet use, and prepare each change of responsibility. | The commitment curve is a heuristic. Increased activity and leadership are optional choices; artificial difficulty is not a reason to restrict useful access. |
| Self-determination theory; Slemp, Lee & Mossman (2021) | Provide meaningful choices and reasons, attainable action with useful feedback, and an available interpersonal response. | The workplace intervention review supports bounded design proposals; it does not establish their causal effect in every voluntary community. Observe the recipient's experience and the real work conditions. |
| Walton et al. (2023) | Truthful accounts of ordinary transition difficulties can support a newcomer's interpretation before visible contribution. | The studied student transition and actual opportunities constrain the inference. Use the adapted move only with real help and opportunity; it cannot correct actual rejection by reinterpretation alone. |
| OCE.10 and OCE.6 | Distinguish action-changing explanations; obtain the specific enabling relation where an organisational responsibility is current. | A community invitation supplies neither an employer's allocation nor a permission held by another owner. |

The accepted cost is deliberate inquiry and support in place of cheap uniform induction. Reconsider the entry when the offered help is unavailable, people experience pressure or exclusion, conditions change, or a simpler route supplies the same wanted result.

Source returns: [Spinks](https://www.wiley-vch.de/en/areas-interest/finance-economics-law/the-business-of-belonging-978-1-119-76612-4); [SDT theory](https://selfdeterminationtheory.org/theory/); [Slemp, Lee & Mossman](https://selfdeterminationtheory.org/wp-content/uploads/2021/03/2021_SlempLeeMossman_Interventions.pdf); [Walton et al.](https://www.gregorywalton.com/uploads/4/9/4/4/49448111/waltonetal2023.pdf).

### CB.3:12 - Relations

- **Uses CB.1 and CB.2:** a worthwhile offer and an obtainable first exchange. A failed central offer returns to CB.1.
- **Supplies CB.4 and CB.5:** understood participation conditions and supported contributors; an interaction or contribution can expose a new entry difficulty.
- **Uses [OCE.10 (OCE.10:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/ORGANIZATION-CHANGE-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#oce104---solution):** inquiry and response for a consequential participation gap.
- **Uses [OCE.6 (OCE.6:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/ORGANIZATION-CHANGE-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#oce64---solution):** an actual assignment or enabling relation required by the responsibility.
- **Uses [HCD.3, HCD.5 and HCD.6](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Human%20Capability%20Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/HUMAN-CAPABILITY-DEVELOPMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md):** qualified diagnosis and preparation when learning a contribution is needed.

### CB.3:End

# Part B - Make shared participation useful

## CB.4 - Design a Space and Shared Activity for Its Purpose

> **Type:** Community-building method pattern
> **Status:** Stable

### CB.4:1 - Problem frame

Use this pattern when people have a worthwhile reason to interact, but the setting or activity does not yet let them do the relevant work together. A forum accumulates brief unanswered questions. A large meeting lets a few people speak while others cannot enter the conversation. A private case needs careful clarification, while the proposed format gives everyone only a minute.

The subject is the arrangement of an interaction: its people, purpose, material, place or channel, participation, facilitation, timing and continuation. A useful arrangement can support mutual help, a shared experience, comparison of practice or a wider view of an issue. It may be a single encounter or a continuing space.

**First useful result:** participants can perform the intended shared activity under feasible conditions, and its useful result or unresolved question reaches the next person or action that needs it.

The organiser needs a credible purpose and relevant contributions, such as CB.1/2 supply, and an account of participants' access and preparation. CB.3 helps establish entry conditions. The activity's domain supplies the content and criteria for professional conclusions.

**Ordinary non-use.** Keep an existing interaction that supplies its result at acceptable burden. Use a resource or individual service when it can answer the receiving need without mutual activity. A missing professional method, authority or permission requires its direct supplier; choosing a more engaging meeting does not obtain it.

### CB.4:2 - Problem

A familiar format can determine the result before anyone asks what participants need. A lecture may replace peer inquiry, a constant chat may hide answers that later users must find, or a forced consensus may erase a useful difference of perspective. Adding people, channels or recurring events can increase burden without improving the interaction.

How can an organiser construct and adapt a shared activity so that the right contributions can meet, be understood and lead to a useful continuation?

### CB.4:3 - Forces

| Force | Decision it changes |
| --- | --- |
| A common conversation and individual opportunity | Keeping everyone together preserves shared context; turn-taking capacity can become inadequate. |
| Rich material and feasible preparation | Participants need enough context to contribute, while excessive prework can prevent participation. |
| Familiarity and fit | A known tool or rhythm reduces entry cost; it may not support the required interaction or access. |
| Openness and sensitive material | Broad participation can bring useful perspectives, while some material needs a limited audience and explicit use conditions. |
| Continuity and available attention | A predictable opportunity can help people return; maintaining it consumes real organising and participant effort. |

### CB.4:4 - Solution

Begin with the result and the actions that could produce it. Choose a form, participants and setting that make those actions feasible; prepare their connections, run the activity with the support it needs, and return the outcome to its use.

**Working reminder:** name the result; choose how people will work; fit material, space and time; guide the actual interaction; connect its outcome to the next use.

#### CB.4:4.1 - Name the result and the interaction it requires

Ask what should become possible during or after this activity. A case owner may need a justified next step. Practitioners may need to understand how another group handles an unfamiliar condition. Neighbours may want a valued conversation. These purposes require different closure and different evidence.

Identify what participants must do with one another's contributions. Clarifying a difficult case needs questions and answers. Comparing contexts needs enough common questions to make the differences intelligible. Generating alternatives needs room for contributions that have not yet been evaluated. Producing a shared decision requires an applicable decision arrangement in addition to discussion.

Select the minimum material and relevant people from that action. Explain why each contribution is needed and what the person can use or receive. Include a missing perspective where it can change the result, and obtain the needed domain competence separately from facilitation.

State a limit early when it changes expectations. A forum answer can guide a next inquiry without promising an urgent professional response. A discussion can expose disagreement without settling it. A pleasant shared activity can be useful without demonstrating that anyone learned a skill.

#### CB.4:4.2 - Choose the form, timing and size together

Compare serious forms against the action and participants' conditions.

| Interaction needed | A suitable starting form | Main condition to check |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A question can be understood from prepared material and participants are available at different times | Asynchronous request and responses | The material is interpretable, the return window remains useful, and someone can close the thread or route. |
| The result depends on rapid clarification or joint attention | A bounded live conversation | Relevant people can meet, hear one another and see the needed material. |
| Several contexts must be compared | Prepared displays or a common set of questions, followed by comparison | Entries concern comparable questions while retaining the conditions that explain differences. |
| A larger group needs one developing conversation | A small speaking circle with an accessible way to enter and leave it | Listening, joining and turn-taking are feasible for the whole group. |
| Many perspectives should be connected | Several conversations with deliberate movement and preserved context | Each new group can recover the previous discussion and contribute a distinct perspective. |
| People need experience of an action | Demonstration, joint practice or a bounded role-play | The domain method, material, competent support and applicable protection are available. |

A combination is often useful: an advance written question can reduce live explanation, and a later response can report what happened in practice. Specify what passes between the parts and who returns it. A recording does not automatically supply that connection.

Separate timing from medium. Asynchronous contribution can use paper, shared artefacts or messages as well as an online space. A digital channel can support a live conversation or a delayed response. Choose from the work and access conditions, rather than treating online, remote and asynchronous as interchangeable.

Check the attention budget. Multiply a proposed round by the number of speakers, then allow time for understanding, comparison and closure. If it does not fit, shorten the requested contribution, use advance material, divide work with a meaningful return, select a smaller relevant group or change the scope. Do not merely announce a shorter time while retaining all the work.

Small size alone establishes neither trust nor confidentiality. Large size alone establishes neither diversity nor useful coverage. Inspect who is present, whose contribution is missing and whether the selected form actually lets that contribution matter.

#### CB.4:4.3 - Choose a concrete conversation construction

Use a form because of the result it develops. The variants below are adaptations of practitioner treatments in the Communities of Practice Guidebook. Their timings and group sizes must fit the actual case.

**A focused discussion of a current question.** State the question and obtain contrasting experience or interpretations. Let people clarify the terms, offer a relevant example and respond to a specific difference. Limit recurring monologues that prevent others from entering. If the agenda itself is uncertain, participants can propose and select questions for the available discussion time. Selecting discussion topics gives the group an agenda; it does not grant authority over the matter discussed.

**One conversation in a larger group.** Use a fishbowl-like arrangement when splitting into separate groups would lose the shared conversation. Keep a small speaking group and a wider listening group, with a clear, usable way to change places. People join the speaking group to contribute rather than starting a competing conversation from outside. Allow a newcomer to the speaking group a real chance to speak and remain for responses. Choose a transition signal that fits physical access or the available communication channel. A facilitator maintains audibility and the transition agreement. A fixed panel with an audience supplies a different interaction.

**Conversations with changing groups.** Use a café-like arrangement to explore one question across several contexts. Give each small group the same intelligible question and a way to retain the material. On changing groups, keep a host or other reliable context return; incoming participants add what they encountered elsewhere and inspect differences. At the end, compare themes and unresolved conditions across groups. Choose the number and duration of rounds from the available attention. A display of popular themes can guide further inquiry, but should not erase an important minority condition or be presented as a representative population estimate.

**Arguments that people hesitate to voice.** For a bounded issue, invite participants to explore opposing arguments in explicitly assigned roles. Give them preparation time, an opportunity to answer the other argument, and a closing conversation outside those roles. Ask which assumptions or alternatives now matter to their own judgement. Do not infer participants' personal beliefs from the side they were asked to argue. Keep the debate separate from a formal decision and use a different form if the conflict cannot be explored under acceptable participation conditions.

**A view of different practice settings.** Start with what participants need to know about one another. A gallery can let people present a relevant project, need or offer and then discuss it. For systematic comparison, agree the questions, let each participant describe their own context, and compare answers to the same question across contexts. Preserve a missing answer as missing; distinguish reported practice from demonstrated performance. A practice matrix is useful when it makes those comparisons possible. Its colours or cell counts add no independent quality judgement.

**Personal reflection connected to shared discussion.** Give a quiet period in which people can formulate their own conclusions or questions, then let them compare what they choose to share. Use a common document when seeing emerging contributions helps the purpose. If independent initial responses matter, collect or retain them before showing others' responses. Close with the important differences and the next use of the notes, rather than leaving a large unexamined transcript.

A shared timeline is another useful construction when the question concerns different histories or future connections. Choose the relevant period, let participants place meaningful events or proposals, and discuss where their accounts meet or differ. Keep a remembered event, a hope and an accepted commitment distinguishable. Use the result to identify a present question or agreed next action.

For a specific case clinic, design clinic, story, project review or prepared guest contribution, CB.5 develops how to obtain and return the useful content. The arrangements above can carry those activities where their participation conditions fit.

#### CB.4:4.4 - Prepare the space and the material

Choose a physical or digital setting by the interaction it must support. Check whether people can enter, see or hear the material, take turns, ask for clarification and recover the result. For a continuing question-and-answer space, also check whether later users can find an applicable answer and its conditions. For rapid discussion, the ability to exchange and clarify may matter more than a complex information structure.

Test the actual participant path with a representative task. Open the intended material under the participant's access conditions, try the proposed contribution and obtain the expected return. If a platform's features or cost matter to the decision, inspect its current terms and behaviour for this use; a remembered product category does not settle that comparison.

Keep the number of spaces proportionate to distinct needs and available support. Split a channel when the difference in audience, material, timing or purpose justifies it and the new route can be found. Avoid requiring people to repeat the same question in several places. Where independent groups already use another space, arrange a permitted connection rather than assuming that all useful relations must move to the organiser's platform.

For a meeting, make its purpose, preparation and participation understandable before it starts. Provide only material that participants need for the action, and allow enough preparation time. A case may need a short contextual explanation and a relevant diagram; a complete project review needs a larger body of work and inspection time.

Establish the sharing and participation conditions that the material requires. Explain who will be present, what may be retained or shared, whether recording is planned and how to decline a question or stop participation. Use another setting or material when those conditions cannot be met. A promise of a private conversation requires more than a small room or a closed online group.

#### CB.4:4.5 - Facilitate the work and respond to change

Open by making the purpose, current conditions and next action clear. Give an example when participants cannot cheaply infer how to contribute. Allow a person to pass or take another relevant form of participation where the activity permits it. Do not require personal disclosure or an emotional performance as proof of involvement.

LDR.1 supplies a developed construction for a brief before work, a short reassessment when the situation changes and a debrief afterwards. Use its relevant contribution: confirm what people will do, make an obstacle or change intelligible, and return the needed adjustment to the person who can make it.

During the activity, intervene at the actual difficulty. Ask for the observation behind a conclusion, give a quieter contributor a usable entry, recover a lost question, clarify which configuration an answer concerns or separate clarification from advice. If the selected form is preventing the work, change it openly and preserve the intended result. Breaking a large discussion into smaller groups also requires a way to bring their relevant results back.

For joint practice or role-play, name the representative situation, available materials, participant roles and the action being tried. Observe what happened and compare it with the applicable criteria before trying a variation. A demonstration can include the practitioner's explanation of a choice that would otherwise remain invisible. Use HCD when the intended result is a person's learning or capability; attendance, enthusiasm and seeing an expert perform are insufficient evidence of that result.

Keep emotionally sensitive material within the participants' consent and the organiser's competence. A person may decline to tell a story or continue an exercise. If the activity requires specialist support that is unavailable, change the activity or obtain that support. The purpose and limits of an ordinary practice discussion remain visible even when participants care deeply about it.

#### CB.4:4.6 - Connect the result to use and continuation

Before closing, ask what the activity supplied and who needs it next. The result may be a case owner's next step, a set of contrasting interpretations, an introduction, a material revision or a valued shared experience. Preserve an unresolved question where its deciding condition is still absent.

Return enough context for the next use. Include the question, relevant conditions, remaining disagreement or uncertainty, and the person who has accepted the next action where one is needed. Obtain agreement before sharing attributable or sensitive material. A private experience can remain private when no further record serves its purpose.

If the activity recurs, choose its rhythm from demand, participants' availability and organising capacity. Keep a familiar entry when it helps, but change the content or support that no longer serves the work. Predictability need not mean daily activity, a universal meeting length or a calendar filled at every frequency.

Observe the next use when the claim depends on it. A meeting can establish that participants compared alternatives; later application needs a later observation. Reopen the affected design when people cannot enter, contributions are lost, answers cannot be recovered, burdens exceed capacity or the purpose changes. Use CB.1 if another route now supplies the result more directly.

### CB.4:5 - Archetypal Grounding

#### CB.4:5.1 - Eighteen people compare a recurring difficulty

A constructed repairer meeting has eighteen participants, forty-five minutes and one question: under which workshop conditions does a particular permitted diagnostic observation become unavailable? The purpose is a picture of the differing conditions and contacts for follow-up, rather than agreement on a new diagnostic method.

The first design gives everyone two minutes for an introduction. That consumes thirty-six minutes and leaves nine for the actual comparison and closure. The organiser instead asks for a short advance description and arranges three groups with six people each. Each group has a willing host who can preserve its account.

The forty-five minutes are allocated as five to establish the question and sharing limits, twelve for the first conversation, three to change groups, twelve for a second conversation, ten to compare findings, and three to close the return. These case allowances sum to forty-five. The hosts retain context while the other participants encounter a different group.

One group reports an equipment constraint; another reports permission to use the data; a third finds that an available observation has been misunderstood. The whole-group comparison preserves those different conditions. The relevant access owner receives the permission question, and two participants accept a bounded clarification of the instruction. A majority preference would not make these difficulties the same.

If preparation is unavailable or participants cannot use the changing-group arrangement, the organiser reduces the questions, keeps stable groups with an explicit exchange of findings, or chooses an asynchronous comparison. The result governs the adaptation.

#### CB.4:5.2 - A private case cannot use the public channel

An association's open forum is effective for general reference questions. A member brings a case containing material they may share only with named authorised people. The organiser cannot solve this by moving the discussion to an unexamined private chat.

They establish the permitted audience and material, choose a setting that supports those conditions, and prepare the bounded question with the case owner. If no adequate material can be shared, the case uses its authorised service route. Other members can still receive an independently prepared general explanation if it is useful and permitted.

#### CB.4:5.3 - A common conversation needs a different entry

A large neighbourhood gathering wants to hear different experiences of using a shared garden. A fixed panel would leave most participants as an audience. The host proposes a small speaking circle with an accessible signal for joining, clear turns and audibility for the rest of the room. Someone unable to move between seats can enter through the agreed microphone route.

The closing account distinguishes differing experiences and questions for the people responsible for the garden. Participation in the conversation does not appoint the speaking group to decide for all residents.

#### CB.4:5.4 - A demonstration changes the receiving question

A practitioner demonstrates a familiar repair method while explaining why an observation changes the next action. Viewers recover a previously hidden distinction and ask for suitable practice material. CB.4 has arranged a useful demonstration and a return. The subsequent learning design, representative attempts and capability judgement use HCD. If the demonstrated method itself remains unclear, its description or development uses Method Engineering.

### CB.4:6 - Bias-Annotation

Event accounts often make a memorable experience or a fluent discussion appear sufficient. The method keeps the receiving result and later use visible. Platform-centred accounts can also favour measurable activity over quiet access and off-platform relationships; choose the setting from the contribution and actual participant conditions.

### CB.4:7 - Conformance Checklist

Before relying on the arrangement, establish that:

- the result and necessary interaction are intelligible;
- relevant contributors and domain criteria are available;
- the form, size and timing fit the work;
- material and access conditions allow the intended participation;
- facilitation addresses actual obstacles and permits a workable change;
- the outcome reaches its next use with the needed context and limits.

Recognition can begin with a simple invitation and prepared activity. Claims about actual learning, durable relationships, representative opinion or a fulfilled professional service require their own evidence.

### CB.4:8 - Common Anti-Patterns and How to Avoid Them

| Wrong turn | Practical repair |
| --- | --- |
| Select a favourite format and then search for a purpose | State the wanted result and compare interaction forms that could produce it. |
| Add participants while keeping the same speaking budget | Change the work distribution, scope or timing and preserve the return between groups. |
| Treat every digital channel as asynchronous | Establish actual response timing and its usefulness for the question. |
| A fishbowl becomes a permanent panel | Restore an accessible transition into the speaking conversation or acknowledge the panel purpose. |
| A theme vote is presented as a collective decision | Return the discussion result to the applicable decision arrangement. |
| A stimulating exercise is reported as acquired competence | Obtain representative performance evidence through the relevant learning use. |
| Create more spaces than can be supported | Consolidate the route or supply the missing organising work. |

### CB.4:9 - Consequences

Participants receive an arrangement that lets their contributions meet and produce an understandable result. The organiser can change size, timing, medium or support without losing the reason for gathering.

Preparation and facilitation cost effort. A smaller activity, an adequate reference or an individual service may be preferable when a designed shared experience adds little to the receiving use.

### CB.4:10 - Rationale

A format distributes attention, material and opportunities to respond. Its value therefore depends on the action participants need to perform. The same group can need a private case conversation, a broad comparison and quiet independent access at different times.

Preserving the return is as important as producing contributions. Splitting a group without connecting its findings loses shared context; recording everything without selecting what the next action needs creates another burden. A bounded result and receiving use give the organiser a reasoned choice.

### CB.4:11 - SoTA-Echoing

| Source line or developed treatment | Contribution used | Adaptation and accepted limit |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Spinks, The Business of Belonging (2021), physical PDF 139–168 | Connect people, purpose, setting, participation, rules, invitation and intended result; design guidance and timing instead of waiting for spontaneous interaction. | Select size and rhythm from the actual action. Small size, emotional intensity and platform activity supply no general quality or learning guarantee. |
| Wenger-Trayner et al., Communities of Practice Guidebook, second edition (2023), printed 152–156 and 163–174 | Distinct constructions for a focused discussion, shared conversation, rotating conversations, argument exploration, context comparison and reflection. | These are practitioner methods and cases. Preserve their different results and participation conditions; a forum format or topic selection supplies no independent decision mandate. |
| LDR.1:4.4 | Brief the work, reassess a changed situation and debrief the episode with an actual return. | Reuse the conversation construction while obtaining competence, permission and authority from their direct sources. |
| HCD.5/6 | Select learning activities and representative practice when learning is the receiving use. | An interaction can supply material or help without establishing independent capability. |

Compared with opening a channel or running a familiar event, the construction requires more explicit preparation but can reveal a timing, access or return failure before a recurring commitment is made. Reconsider it when a simpler arrangement supplies the same result, when the support cannot be obtained, or when participant conditions change.

The conversation variants are analytical adaptations of the [Communities of Practice Guidebook](https://www.wenger-trayner.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/03/24-02-21-CoP-guidebook-second-edition-final-2.pdf) by Etienne Wenger-Trayner, Beverly Wenger-Trayner, Phil Reid and Claude Bruderlein, published under [CC BY-NC 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc/4.0/). Further source return: [Spinks](https://www.wiley-vch.de/en/areas-interest/finance-economics-law/the-business-of-belonging-978-1-119-76612-4).

### CB.4:12 - Relations

- **Uses CB.1–3:** the purpose, first exchange and attainable participation conditions.
- **Supplies and uses CB.5:** the interaction carries a useful contribution; its recipient, material and criteria determine the appropriate form.
- **Uses [LDR.1 (LDR.1:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/LEADERSHIP-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#ldr14---solution):** the preparation, change-of-situation and debrief conversations.
- **Uses [HCD.5:4 and HCD.6](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Human%20Capability%20Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/HUMAN-CAPABILITY-DEVELOPMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#hcd54---solution):** a learning design when that is the promised contribution.
- **Returns to CB.1:** a changed purpose or an adequate alternative that no longer needs mutual interaction.

### CB.4:End

## CB.5 - Obtain and Return Useful Contributions from Participants

> **Type:** Community-building method pattern
> **Status:** Stable

### CB.5:1 - Problem frame

Use this pattern when a community has a real question or piece of work that participants could help with, but useful contributions are not yet reaching a recipient who can use or accept them. A support question attracts many replies without a usable answer. Product suggestions accumulate without a response from the product team. A volunteer is asked to maintain a resource without access, criteria or time.

The subject is the arrangement for obtaining a useful contribution from participants and returning its result. Contributions can include advice, practical support, a description of experience, a candidate recommendation, an improved requirement, teaching material or a permissible piece of maintenance work. Their domain methods and receiving responsibilities remain distinct.

**First useful result:** an agreed contribution reaches its recipient in a usable form, with an understandable acceptance, application, revision or limitation returned to the contributor. An unresolved requirement or unavailable supplier can be the warranted result.

The organiser needs access to the receiving question, relevant participants and the person who can judge or accept the contribution. CB.1 helps decide whether this is a worthwhile community contribution; CB.2 obtains an initial exchange; CB.3/4 support participation and the activity's form.

**Ordinary non-use.** If an existing resource or accountable service already supplies the result adequately, use it. If a professional method, mandate or permission is missing, obtain it from the appropriate source. Willing participants cannot make an unsupported technical act fit for use by discussing it.

### CB.5:2 - Problem

“Contribute to the community” can conceal very different work. Answering a question, recommending a person, producing a tested product change and restoring equipment have different criteria and receiving decisions. A large volume of activity can leave the needed result absent. Conversely, useful work may be received without acknowledgement, explanation or a path for correction.

How can an organiser connect a real need, suitable voluntary or agreed work, its receiving use and a returned response without promising contributions that nobody can supply?

### CB.5:3 - Forces

| Force | Decision it changes |
| --- | --- |
| Broad willingness and specific competence | Many people may want to help; a particular contribution needs relevant knowledge, access and effort. |
| Contribution and receiving authority | Participants can offer valuable material while the product, employer or equipment owner retains the relevant decision. |
| Quick help and reusable material | A response fitted to one case can be useful immediately; reuse needs its conditions and a maintained source. |
| Voluntary contribution and dependable commitments | Peer help can remain optional; a service promise requires a provision arrangement able to meet it. |
| Learning from feedback and unpaid repair burden | A returned reason can improve the contribution; repeated revisions consume real time and need renewed agreement. |

### CB.5:4 - Solution

Start with the receiving result. Distinguish the work it needs, obtain a suitable contributor under feasible terms, support its performance, and close the return between the contributor and the recipient. Keep domain correctness, authority and the limits of available evidence attached to the actual work.

**Working reminder:** name the receiving result; agree a feasible contribution; prepare the work; obtain a usable return; explain acceptance or the remaining gap.

#### CB.5:4.1 - Turn a general request into a receiving use

Ask who will use the contribution, for what next action, and under which conditions. Replace “we need more content” with the question a reader cannot yet answer or the action they cannot perform. Replace “we need ideas” with the use difficulty and the kind of proposal the receiving team can consider.

Choose the result before recruiting contributors. For a case, establish the subject and relevant configuration, what has been tried, the uncertainty, permitted material and useful response window. For a shared resource, establish its intended users, applicable content and the person who can maintain it. For a recommendation, establish what the recipient needs to decide and what the recommender actually knows.

Agree how the recipient will recognise a useful contribution. A case owner may need a justified next step; a design team may need a testable use account; a service owner may need evidence that a permissible intervention restored the required function. The criteria come from the receiving work and its domain, not from the number of responses or the contributor's popularity.

Distinguish accepted, usable and applied. A recipient can accept a technically sound explanation but be unable to act because access is missing. A product team can find a suggestion informative while declining its implementation. Preserve the actual outcome so that the contribution can be improved without claiming work that did not occur.

#### CB.5:4.2 - Obtain the contributor and the conditions

Find people whose competence, experience or access matches the needed contribution. Ask for a bounded piece of work, explain its recipient and criteria, and disclose the relevant interests of the organiser or sponsor. Confirm willingness, available effort, preparation and a useful return window.

Make the scope negotiable. A person can provide an observation without writing the whole guide, review one claim without maintaining the product, or answer a question without accepting a permanent service obligation. If the receiving promise requires more than anyone can commit, narrow it, obtain funded provision or use an accountable supplier.

Obtain the material, tools, access and permissions needed for the chosen work. OCE.6 supplies the fuller method when actual organisational assignments or enabling relations are current. Its central distinction matters here: selecting a capable person, agreeing a contribution and establishing permission are different achievements.

Provide preparation for the actual gap. A peer can help formulate a case or inspect an example. A qualified HCD contribution can develop missing capability. If the underlying professional method is unavailable, return that development or inquiry question to Method Engineering or Research Method Practice. More reminders cannot replace it.

Identify the receiving person before the contribution is made. Agree when and how they will inspect it and give a useful response. An open suggestion box without a receiving decision can invite effort the organisation is not prepared to use.

#### CB.5:4.3 - Choose the work and its acceptance conditions

The following branches illustrate different contributions. Select the branch that matches the receiving result and combine branches only with an explicit connection.

**Answer a question or provide peer support.** Prepare the question, connect it with a competent willing respondent and compare the response with the case's conditions. Ask for the basis and applicability of the answer when these change its use. The case owner returns what became possible, what was tried or which condition still prevents action. Use the relevant service route when a response or escalation must be dependable.

**Enable self-service.** Give the user an applicable instruction, necessary access and a recognisable result or stopping point. Try the route under the user's conditions and provide a route for exceptions. A public reference may serve many people who never join the discussion. OPS.6 supplies the action and return discipline when this is an operational case; the applicable domain method supplies the action itself. A confusing or obsolete instruction returns to its owner for correction.

**Help someone enter useful work.** A participant can recommend a possible colleague, introduce a newcomer or help them prepare. Obtain the person's agreement to the relevant introduction and give the recipient a factual account of the contribution offered. The employer or activity owner judges suitability and agrees the work. For onboarding, name the task the newcomer must be able to perform and supply the corresponding material, help and learning where needed. A recommendation is not a hiring decision, and an introduction is not completed preparation.

**Recommend an offer to a potential user.** Connect a real receiving need with an accurately described offer. State the experience or evidence behind the recommendation, its limits and the recommender's relationship to the provider. Let the recipient inspect the terms and decide whether to continue. CB.1 supplies the honest-offer discipline; the provider remains responsible for product claims. A community relationship supplies no obligation to promote a sponsor, and a purchase alone does not demonstrate that the recommendation served the person.

**Co-design a result or product.** Choose what contribution is wanted: a use account, requirement, concept, design alternative or tested part. Give contributors the relevant problem, constraints, available material and acceptance question. SYSE.2 helps connect the use situation to a candidate concept and compare changes on either side of the system boundary. The receiving product specialist must examine the contribution and return an adoption, revision, rejection or unresolved question with a reason. A popular proposal still needs the applicable engineering judgement.

**Produce or improve material.** Establish the reader's intended action, the subject method and correctness basis, and the person who can review and maintain the result. Ask participants for useful cases, explanations, corrections or a bounded authored contribution. Inspect the result through a representative use before presenting it as a reusable answer. For a teaching publication, obtain the relevant HCD learning design and use Learning Product LPF for the publication under the access condition in [CB.Reference:8](#cbreference8---actual-supplier-returns); the subject expert, author, learning designer and provider can be different people. If a necessary contribution cannot be obtained, preserve the gap instead of treating submitted material as a complete learning product.

**Maintain an object or a shared resource.** Name what must remain usable and who can authorise the actual work. For technical maintenance, MNT.1 establishes the unit, configuration, required functioning and the question being decided. Obtain the applicable professional method and permissions before an intervention; return functioning evidence and the separate decision to resume use where required. For a knowledge resource, obtain a capable maintainer, correction criteria and a route for obsolete or disputed content.

When a contribution spans institutions, obtain the actual contribution and authority from each. A university, supplier or association may provide useful people, material or access; its category alone specifies none of those commitments.

#### CB.5:4.4 - Develop useful case work and comparison

Choose a construction that fits the question, available experience and time. The following adaptations from the Communities of Practice Guidebook retain different results.

**Case clinic: obtain a reasoned continuation.** Ask the case owner for a real challenge, a focused question and just enough permitted context. Begin with clarification; let contributors ask about conditions before offering solutions. Then draw out relevant experiences and compare proposed actions with the case. Keep observations, experience-based suggestions and qualified technical conclusions distinguishable. The owner explains what they will take away, what they will not use and what remains undecided. Arrange a later response when actual use can be observed.

For a complex case, the work may require substantial clarification and more time than a short round permits. Divide the question or obtain separate preparation when needed. With a large group, CB.4 can provide a speaking circle or smaller groups with a return. A facilitator protects the question and turn-taking; domain contributors supply technical judgement. If advice conflicts, recover the differing conditions or the missing evidence rather than choosing the most popular answer.

**Design clinic: widen the possible next moves.** Use this when time is short or participants' contexts are too different for a deep case judgement. Give the owner a short opportunity to state the challenge. Follow with concise clarification, relevant experiences, suggestions and the owner's account of what changed their thinking. Keep a record of possibilities useful to the owner. The result is a broader set of options, with further examination still needed; do not present the speed of the exchange as evidence that a solution has been selected or tested.

**Story of practice: make experience available.** Ask the teller to recover what happened, the conditions, actions, difficulty and outcome. Let listeners ask for details that change their interpretation and explain what may be relevant to their own situation. An interviewer can help expose a choice or failure that the teller treats as obvious. Keep a story's context available when drawing a lesson. If someone needs a validated method or general causal explanation, obtain that further work; one compelling account does not provide it.

**Project review: obtain an informed view of a larger body of work.** Agree the question the project owner wants answered and give reviewers access and time to inspect the relevant work. Alternate examination with reflection so that claims can be challenged against the project, rather than against a short presentation alone. A rehearsal can help the owner prepare for an actual review. The project owner's applicable acceptance and release decisions remain separate.

**Demonstration or joint work: expose an action and its reasons.** Choose a representative situation, let a competent practitioner perform or explain the difficult action, and give participants a chance to ask about a consequential choice. When they try the action, supply the relevant practice and feedback through HCD. Their experience can also expose a question for method description or development. Keep the observed action and the result it actually establishes recoverable.

These forms can be combined. A story can clarify a case, a demonstration can test an interpretation, and a project review can expose a smaller question. Explain the change of receiving result when the activity changes, so that participants know which contribution is now wanted.

#### CB.5:4.5 - Prepare an external contribution without losing the question

Invite an external expert or neighbouring practitioner when the group lacks a needed perspective or competence. Give the guest the actual question, relevant material, participant preparation and expected result. Check that the guest can address it and agrees to an interaction that lets participants clarify and examine the contribution.

A prepared presentation can be useful, but leave enough time for the community's questions and their application. The guest may need to observe the work first, respond to selected cases, or join a small group after a general explanation. Choose the arrangement with the guest rather than presuming that a familiar lecture supplies it.

Connect preparation, shared work and continuation. Participants can submit questions in advance; the guest can identify missing context; afterwards, the recipient can return what was used or still needs clarification. Obtain agreement on retained material and attribution. A recording serves later use only when it contains the relevant question and can be accessed under the agreed conditions.

If the desired result is a continuing course, service or research project, obtain its provider and resource arrangement. The community can contribute domain expertise and cases, but a single guest visit does not supply the whole undertaking.

#### CB.5:4.6 - Return, accept, revise or stop

Give the recipient the contribution together with what determines its use: the question, applicable conditions, evidence or domain basis where needed, and unresolved limits. Use the existing discussion or work record when it can carry these facts.

Ask the recipient to return a meaningful disposition. Examples include: used as supplied; accepted for a stated later use; useful after a specified change; declined because a condition or requirement differs; or not yet assessable because a needed basis is missing. Explain the reason that can improve the contribution or prevent inappropriate reuse.

Agree further work rather than silently extending the original commitment. A small correction within the accepted task can proceed with the contributor's available effort. A materially different request, repeated revisions or a new maintenance obligation needs a revised arrangement.

Close a failed contribution honestly. When no suitable helper is available, return the unanswered question and a possible source or service route. When the user cannot act, distinguish missing access from an unusable answer. When the product team will not inspect suggestions, stop inviting that kind of work until a receiving arrangement exists.

For an operational case, OPS.6 separates selection, performance and the resulting state. Use that distinction whenever a message or record update could be mistaken for a completed intervention. A technical answer, performed work and restored functioning have different evidence.

#### CB.5:4.7 - Make useful material reusable and support continuation

Retain a contribution when another receiving use warrants it and the relevant permissions allow it. Preserve the situation, method or explanation, applicability conditions and limits. Give later users a way to find it and report a changed condition or error.

Choose a capable owner for correction and maintenance. For professional method content, use the relevant Method Engineering contribution; for a resource containing existing qualified advice, use its established subject review and publication route. If no one can maintain a changing answer, limit its stated use and avoid making an indefinite currency promise.

Observe how the arrangement works for both recipients and contributors. Are useful questions reaching suitable people? Are answers used or returned with reasons? Does preparation and follow-up exceed the available effort? Repeated reliance on one generous contributor is a provision question even when the contribution is voluntary.

Change the specific failed connection: improve the request, recruit another qualified helper, make a reusable answer accessible, change a response promise or stop an unsupported activity. Return to CB.1 when a service, resource or another contribution better serves the purpose. Keep enough information for the next participant to continue without requiring everyone to repeat the whole exchange.

### CB.5:5 - Archetypal Grounding

#### CB.5:5.1 - A clinic returns a useful next step

In a constructed repairer association, a participant brings an unusual diagnostic observation. The organiser obtains a permitted case description, a focused question and two contributors familiar with the relevant equipment. The case owner retains the decision about the actual repair.

The first contributor offers a solution immediately. The facilitator returns to clarification: the suggested action applies to one configuration, while the case may have another. The group first establishes the configuration and what observations can be obtained under the available permission. Contributors then explain similar cases and compare the resulting options with the current evidence.

The owner takes away one justified information-gathering action and reports the later result. It excludes one interpretation but leaves another condition uncertain. The returned account helps the contributors refine the advice. The clinic has supplied a useful continuation without claiming that the equipment was repaired or the method was proven in every setting.

If nobody can supply the needed technical judgement, the group can still clarify the question and return it to a qualified source. Interest and active discussion do not close that missing contribution.

#### CB.5:5.2 - Twenty minutes broaden a design question

A voluntary community wants to make its shared reference easier to use, but participants work in different settings and cannot inspect the whole resource together. They agree to a twenty-minute design clinic: two minutes for the owner's challenge, four for clarification, five for relevant experiences, six for possible changes and three for the owner's selected takeaways. The allowances sum to twenty.

Participants expose two different difficulties: finding a known answer and recognising whether it applies. The owner retains a clearer index and an applicability example as possibilities for a later trial. No vote selects a final design. A subsequent use test and the resource owner's decision are still needed.

A full case judgement would require more material and examination than this interval permits. The shorter form is useful because it promises a different result.

#### CB.5:5.3 - A product suggestion reaches an actual decision

Independent repairers repeatedly misunderstand a provider's configuration label. The provider asks the association for useful input. Instead of an unrestricted call for ideas, the product contact requests permitted accounts of the use situation and a proposed distinction that would help.

Participants supply two contrasting cases. One suggests changing the label; another suggests improving the accompanying explanation. The receiving specialist uses the relevant Systems Engineering work to compare product and supporting-arrangement changes. The team tests a revised explanation, then tells contributors which part it adopted and why the hardware label is unchanged.

The community obtained usable input and a returned decision. A count of submitted ideas would miss the comparison, and the participants' preference would not by itself establish a product change.

#### CB.5:5.4 - Support, self-service and maintenance have different closure

A user asks how to correct a permitted configuration setting. An applicable instruction and available access let them complete the case independently. They report the resulting condition; a volunteer's additional live session is unnecessary.
