In a changed case, a community participant can explain the likely fault but cannot obtain permission to open the customer's equipment. The result is bounded advice and a return to the responsible service provider. MNT.1 helps establish the actual unit, functioning question and permission needs. No one marks the repair complete from the advice alone.

A third case concerns obsolete advice in the shared reference. The organiser obtains the applicable corrected source, a capable reviewer and a maintainer who can replace the entry and identify affected uses. This is maintenance of knowledge content, with its own criteria, rather than permission to perform an equipment intervention.

#### CB.5:5.5 - A willing mentor receives a bounded task

An association member recommends a possible employee to a workshop. The person agrees to the introduction, and the employer assesses suitability and offers work under its own conditions. A volunteer mentor then agrees to help the new employee prepare one familiar task, using available instructions and a qualified learning arrangement where needed.

The mentor's commitment is one prepared attempt and a return about the remaining difficulty. It does not include hiring authority or unlimited onboarding work. If the employer needs dependable continuing support, it must obtain that provision rather than assuming that a successful first meeting created it.

### CB.5:6 - Bias-Annotation

Sponsor accounts can make participant work appear to be freely available production capacity. The method keeps consent, preparation, receiving responsibility and revision effort explicit. Fluent or highly visible contributors can also crowd out less visible competence; select the contribution by the actual question and required basis.

### CB.5:7 - Conformance Checklist

A usable contribution arrangement establishes:

- the receiving use, result and applicable criteria;
- a suitable contributor and a feasible accepted scope;
- required material, preparation, access and permission;
- a construction that fits the kind of work;
- a recipient able to inspect and return a disposition;
- the actual result, remaining gap and any agreed continuation.

A plausible match is enough to begin a bounded invitation. Reliance on correctness, performed intervention, restored functioning or acquired capability requires the relevant domain evidence.

### CB.5:8 - Common Anti-Patterns and How to Avoid Them

| Wrong turn | Practical repair |
| --- | --- |
| Ask for “engagement” or “content” without a receiving use | Name the question, recipient and usable result. |
| Treat every volunteer as available production capacity | Agree the bounded work and obtain missing resources or another provider. |
| A popular answer becomes the accepted technical result | Inspect its basis and applicability through the domain criteria. |
| A short design clinic is presented as a completed decision | Preserve its alternatives and obtain the remaining comparison or acceptance. |
| A guest delivers a standard lecture unrelated to participants' questions | Prepare the guest around the receiving problem and connect interaction to later use. |
| Suggestions disappear into a receiving team's inbox | Establish inspection and a reasoned response before soliciting more work. |
| A one-case answer becomes a timeless general instruction | Preserve its conditions and obtain a maintained, qualified reusable explanation. |

### CB.5:9 - Consequences

The organiser can obtain different kinds of useful work without collapsing them into conversation or visible activity. Contributors understand what they are accepting and can learn what happened to the result. Recipients receive a usable contribution or an actionable gap.

Matching, preparation, inspection and feedback all consume capacity. Some work therefore belongs with a paid or otherwise accountable provider. A community contribution can complement that provision while retaining its own purpose and voluntary terms.

### CB.5:10 - Rationale

A contribution becomes useful through a relation to receiving work. Without a recipient and criteria, the organiser can collect effort without obtaining the needed result. Without a response, contributors cannot distinguish a useful accepted result from one that was ignored, misapplied or blocked by another condition.

The shared construction is the connection among request, contributor, conditions and returned result. The professional operations remain different. Keeping those differences visible lets a community combine support, learning, design and maintenance while preserving the authority and evidence each requires.

### CB.5:11 - SoTA-Echoing

| Source line or developed treatment | Contribution used | Adaptation and limit |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Spinks, The Business of Belonging (2021), physical PDF 45–54 | Distinguish support, product input, recommendation, contributed material and learning-oriented help; make the relevant answer obtainable, including for quiet users. | Business categories help expose different contributions but do not define every community purpose. The participant's result and agreed burden remain part of the choice. |
| Wenger-Trayner et al., Communities of Practice Guidebook, second edition (2023), printed 145–156 and 175–178 | Develop case clinics, shorter design clinics, stories, project reviews, demonstrations and prepared external contributions with later returns. | Their outcomes differ. A story is contextual experience; broadened options still need examination; a guest visit supplies only its agreed contribution. |
| A. Levenchuk, community-development essay 1741983, 2024 | Distinguish work that participants can contribute in recruitment, support, co-design and maintenance. | Match each contribution to an actual recipient, domain method and permission; membership supplies no general authority to perform it. |
| OCE.6, OPS.6, SYSE.2 and MNT.1 | Obtain enabling relations; distinguish chosen action from performed work; develop a use-and-concept proposal; establish the maintenance question. | Use each supplier for its actual result. CB organises the contributions and returns, while those bodies retain their professional questions. |

The serious alternative is to leave the work with an adequate resource or accountable provider. Community contribution is worthwhile where relevant people can supply useful work under acceptable terms and a recipient can use it. Compared with an unrestricted call for help or ideas, the construction adds preparation and response effort while exposing unavailable contributions and unusable results earlier.

Reconsider the arrangement when the kind of work, receiving criteria, permission, contributor capacity or available alternative changes. A missing method or unaccepted product obligation returns to its direct owner.

The clinic and external-contribution variants are analytical adaptations of the [Communities of Practice Guidebook](https://www.wenger-trayner.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/03/24-02-21-CoP-guidebook-second-edition-final-2.pdf) by Etienne Wenger-Trayner, Beverly Wenger-Trayner, Phil Reid and Claude Bruderlein, under [CC BY-NC 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc/4.0/). Other source returns: [Spinks](https://www.wiley-vch.de/en/areas-interest/finance-economics-law/the-business-of-belonging-978-1-119-76612-4); [Levenchuk](https://ailev.livejournal.com/1741983.html).

### CB.5:12 - Relations

- **Uses CB.1–4:** a worthwhile contribution, first exchange, supported entry and suitable shared activity.
- **Uses [OCE.6 (OCE.6:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/ORGANIZATION-CHANGE-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#oce64---solution):** actual assignments and enabling relations when required.
- **Uses [OPS.6 (OPS.6:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/OPERATIONS-MANAGEMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#ops64---solution):** a permissible next action, its performance evidence and the returned case result.
- **Uses [SYSE.2 (SYSE.2:4.3)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/SYSTEMS-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#syse243---develop-the-use-claim-and-candidate-system-concept-together):** connected use and system-concept proposals for co-design contributions.
- **Uses [MNT.1 (MNT.1:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/MAINTENANCE-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#mnt14---solution):** the bounded technical-maintenance question, operating conditions and relevant control.
- **Uses [HCD](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Human%20Capability%20Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/HUMAN-CAPABILITY-DEVELOPMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md) and Learning Product LPF:** learning and publication contributions when those are needed.
- **Returns to [Method development in Core (FPF C.39)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/FPF-Spec.md#c39---find-and-develop-a-way-to-obtain-a-result) or [Research Method Practice (RMP)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/RESEARCH-METHOD-PRACTICE-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md):** an unavailable professional method or an unresolved inquiry needed by the contribution.

### CB.5:End

## CB.6 - Recognise a Contribution and Choose a Meaningful Response

> **Type:** Community-building method pattern
> **Status:** Stable

### CB.6:1 - Problem frame

Use this pattern when someone has made a useful contribution but cannot see what it helped, or when an organiser must choose how to acknowledge it. An answer quietly prevents wasted work. A person prepares the material that makes a discussion possible, while only the speaker receives credit. A public award attracts more visible activity but leaves the recipient uncomfortable.

The subject is recognition of a contribution: making its value intelligible to the contributor and choosing an appropriate response. This can be a specific thank-you, a returned account of use, agreed public credit, a useful introduction, access to an opportunity or a material benefit. A community-wide reward arrangement is one possible application; ordinary recognition need not become a programme.

**First useful result:** the contributor receives a truthful, understandable response to what they supplied, in a form they can accept, with any offered benefit or opportunity actually obtainable.

The organiser needs an account of the contribution and its use, or a recipient able to explain them. CB.5 supplies the return from receiving work. Where the result remains uncertain, recognition can concern a known contribution such as careful clarification without claiming an unobserved outcome.

**Ordinary non-use.** An adequate, welcomed response needs no additional award. If the problem is unavailable help, access, learning or compensation already owed, obtain that contribution directly. If nothing useful is known about the contribution yet, seek the relevant account before praising an imagined effect.

### CB.6:2 - Problem

Recognition can fail in opposite ways. Useful work disappears without a response, or an organiser attaches praise and prizes to whatever is easiest to count. The recipient may experience the same public gesture as appreciation, exposure or a demand to contribute more. A reward can also consume another person's time or imply an opportunity that nobody has agreed to provide.

How can an organiser recognise a real contribution, choose a response that matters to its recipient, and change the arrangement when it distorts the work or imposes an unwanted burden?

### CB.6:3 - Forces

| Force | Decision it changes |
| --- | --- |
| Timely acknowledgement and warranted claims | A contributor should not wait indefinitely for a response; the organiser must distinguish a known contribution from a later effect that is still unobserved. |
| Public credit and personal preference | Visibility can help a person, while privacy, shared authorship or an unwanted association can make another form preferable. |
| Consistent treatment and different contributions | Common criteria make an award understandable; comparable value cannot always be represented by a count of messages or hours. |
| Appreciation and control | A response can convey useful information and care, or make approval conditional on further unpaid effort. |
| A desirable opportunity and actual provision | An introduction, learning place or special access is useful only when the relevant people, conditions and capacity support it. |

### CB.6:4 - Solution

Recover what the person contributed and whom it helped. Choose the purpose and form of the response with their circumstances in view, make any promised benefit feasible, deliver it, and observe whether it served the person and the work.

**Working reminder:** identify the contribution; recover its use; choose a fitting response; supply what was offered; correct the response when its meaning or effects differ.

#### CB.6:4.1 - Find the useful contribution behind the visible activity

Start from an episode or result. Ask the person receiving the contribution what became easier, clearer or possible, and which part helped. A specific answer, a corrected explanation, preparation, translation, coordination or patient clarification can matter more than the most visible performance.

Use the evidence proportionate to the claim. A case owner's account can support “your clarification helped me identify the missing information”. A claim that the advice prevented a technical failure needs the relevant technical basis. When the later outcome is unknown, thank the person for the contribution you can identify and leave the later question open.

Recover joint work fairly. Ask who prepared the material, made access possible or performed a less visible part. Name the contributions in a way those people accept. Do not assign all credit to the person who presented the result merely because their work is easiest to see.

Distinguish useful effort from a successful result. A careful attempt can expose why a proposed approach will not work. Explain that value without relabelling the attempt as successful implementation. Conversely, extensive effort alone does not make incorrect advice fit for reuse.

For an ordinary exchange, this inquiry can be one question to the recipient. A scarce award or influential public endorsement needs a more inspectable basis because other people's opportunities depend on it.

#### CB.6:4.2 - Choose what the response should accomplish

Decide whether the present need is to make the contribution's effect visible, convey appreciation, give fair credit, offer a useful opportunity or change a recurring recognition arrangement. These aims can combine, but each should have an actual recipient and result.

Begin with the least elaborate response that fits. A private message explaining a concrete use may be more valuable than a generic public accolade. Returning a recipient's permitted account of what changed can give the contributor information that the organiser could not supply alone.

Find out how the person wants to be recognised when the choice matters. They may welcome a named example, prefer collective credit, want an introduction, or decline publicity. Existing preferences and the relationship may be sufficient for a routine thank-you; a new public profile, attribution of sensitive work or substantial opportunity warrants an explicit agreement.

People can contribute because they enjoy the activity, because they value its outcome, because they accept a responsibility or because provision is paid. A fitting response does not require the organiser to declare one motive authentic and the others defective. Ask whether the response respects the person's choice and helps them understand their contribution.

Keep recognition and an agreement for work clear. Pay or reimburse what was agreed. A gift does not settle an outstanding obligation, and a thank-you does not obtain a new commitment. If continued work needs funding, access or protected time, arrange those conditions through their owners.

#### CB.6:4.3 - Construct a response that can actually be received

The options below are examples.

| Response | How to make it useful | Condition that can change the choice |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Specific acknowledgement and returned use | Name the contribution, explain a supported effect and let the recipient respond or correct the account. | The effect is unknown, the wording misattributes joint work, or the person prefers a different audience. |
| Agreed credit or public recognition | Describe what the person supplied, obtain the relevant consent and use an audience for whom the account has meaning. | Visibility exposes protected material, creates an unwanted association or overstates the person's qualification. |
| A useful connection | Establish why both parties might benefit, obtain their agreement and prepare the introduction around a real question. | The other person is unavailable, does not wish to participate or cannot supply the expected help. |
| Access to a useful activity or resource | State what is offered, obtain the provider's commitment and make the participation and selection conditions understandable. | The person lacks needed preparation, the resource is unavailable or the selection would remove access someone is already entitled to. |
| A material benefit | Choose something the person can use, explain any terms and provide it without an unannounced obligation. | Receipt creates expense, unsuitable conditions, disclosure or a burden greater than its value. |

A badge or title can carry an accurate statement, such as contribution to a particular resource. Make its meaning recoverable. If it is likely to be read as assessed professional competence or decision authority, either obtain that separate basis or change the description.

An invitation to speak can recognise useful experience and serve other participants, provided the person wants the work and has time to prepare it. Agree its scope and support. An invitation that becomes an unwanted unpaid assignment defeats the proposed benefit.

For an introduction or mentor opportunity, prepare the receiving question and agree the other person's contribution before promising access. CB.2 and CB.5 supply the exchange and contribution arrangements. A learning opportunity also needs an available learning provider and a suitable HCD contribution.

Check the receiving path for a material benefit. Can the person attend the event, use the resource or accept the item under their circumstances? Offer a reasonable alternative when the original benefit is unsuitable.

#### CB.6:4.4 - Make a recurring arrangement understandable and revisable

Use a recurring arrangement when contributions are repeatedly missed or a shared award needs consistent treatment. Begin with the useful work being recognised and the reason the arrangement is needed. Retain ordinary personal acknowledgement alongside it.

Explain how a contribution becomes known, who examines it and what the recognition means. Use examples of different qualifying contributions so that preparation, correction and quiet help can be noticed. Choose an observation window that fits the work: immediate message counts cannot reveal a result that appears only after later use.

If nominations or peer responses are used, ask for a concrete account of the contribution and its value. Popularity can help locate a candidate contribution, but it does not supply its correctness or receiving effect. Where a decision-maker has a relevant interest, use another suitable judgement or make that interest visible to the people relying on the award.

Make the burden proportionate. A small voluntary group may use a brief account at an existing meeting; a scarce professional opportunity needs clearer criteria and a way to correct an error.

Examine what the arrangement makes attractive to produce. If a count rewards short duplicate replies, replace or remove that count and inspect the useful response. If a ranking conceals collaborative work, recognise the joint contribution or its distinct parts. A peer vote alone does not solve these failures; it can favour well-known people or visible work too.

Provide a practical correction route. A contributor should be able to point out mistaken attribution, decline publicity or question a factual claim. Correct the account and affected benefit where necessary. When changing an established reward, explain the reason and honour commitments already made; do not quietly change the meaning of an earned acknowledgement.

#### CB.6:4.5 - Deliver the response and inspect its meaning

Deliver the recognition close enough to the contribution for the relation to be clear. Use ordinary language: what the person did, why it mattered and who benefited, within the available evidence. Avoid generic superlatives when they obscure that information.

A response can be offered without requiring a grateful public reply. Give the person a usable way to correct the account, change attribution or decline the opportunity. Keep a refusal compatible with continued ordinary participation.

Where the response could affect future participation, ask how it was received. Did the person understand what was valued? Was the opportunity useful? Did the gesture feel like appreciation or pressure to maintain an unwanted role? The organiser's good intention cannot answer these questions for the recipient.

Observe the work as well as the visible reaction. An increase in posts may coexist with less useful help. Continued contribution may reflect an accepted responsibility, a valued outcome or pressure. Use the person's account and relevant receiving results to interpret the change; silence or a click alone leaves that question open.

When the response creates a problem, repair its specific cause. Change the audience, clarify a claim, supply a promised opportunity, remove an unsuitable incentive or revise the expected commitment. Do not try to compensate for absent help with more praise.

#### CB.6:4.6 - Keep recognition distinct from continuing participation

A well-received acknowledgement closes the present recognition question. It need not establish a future obligation or a habit of returning.

Use CB.12 when the person wants help making a useful action easier to repeat. Use CB.3 for a changed role or a reduced commitment, and CB.5 when the needed repair is an absent contribution or receiving response. Learning feedback and capability assessment use their own HCD criteria.

Keep only the account needed for attribution, the offered benefit or later use. Publicly accumulating every acknowledgement can create a ranking that nobody intended to introduce. Retain a simple response when it does the job.

### CB.6:5 - Archetypal Grounding

#### CB.6:5.1 - A useful correction receives a specific return

In a constructed repairer association, one participant corrects a misleading applicability example in a shared reference. Another person confirms that the correction helped them distinguish two equipment configurations. The organiser asks permission to return that account.

The contributor prefers a private message and agrees to a brief credit on the corrected page. The response names the distinction they clarified and the use that the recipient reported. It makes no claim that all later repairs were successful.

A later public summary credits both the person who found the problem and the maintainer who checked and incorporated the correction. If a use report contradicts the correction, the subject review reopens; the acknowledgement of the contributed work does not settle the technical question.

#### CB.6:5.2 - A leaderboard rewards the wrong work

An online support group awards points for every answer. Several participants begin splitting replies and repeating material. A quiet contributor who prepares a reliable explanation receives little attention.

The organiser inspects actual cases with their recipients. Some quick replies are useful, but the count does not distinguish them from duplication. The group ends the per-message competition, explains the change and retains already-earned acknowledgements with their original limited meaning. Useful case returns and corrections can receive specific recognition without a new public ranking.

If a later formal award is wanted, it uses an account of a contribution and a suitable judgement. Popular nominations are considered alongside the receiving use and overlooked preparatory work.

#### CB.6:5.3 - A mentor invitation cannot promise another person's time

An organiser wants to thank a volunteer by offering a meeting with an experienced practitioner. Before announcing it, they learn what the volunteer would like to discuss and ask the practitioner whether they can help.

The practitioner can offer one bounded conversation but no continuing mentorship. The volunteer accepts that scope, receives preparation guidance and has a useful exchange. The organiser does not advertise a mentor programme from this single contribution.

In a changed case, the volunteer has no use for the introduction and prefers access to an existing reference resource. Recognition changes form without reducing their standing in the group.

#### CB.6:5.4 - Appreciation does not settle provision

A garden association publicly thanks someone who has repeatedly prepared shared equipment. The person explains that the work now exceeds the time they can offer. More praise would make the burden more visible without removing it.

The association keeps the acknowledgement, agrees a smaller voluntary contribution and arranges the remaining maintenance through a responsible provider. Any payment already agreed is settled separately. Recognition and provision both have useful outcomes.

### CB.6:6 - Bias-Annotation

Visible speakers, prolific writers and people close to the organiser are easier to notice. Look through the receiving work for less visible preparation, correction and coordination. An organiser can also project their own preferred reward onto another person; inspect the recipient's circumstances and response.

### CB.6:7 - Conformance Checklist

A fitting response establishes:

- the actual contribution and the supported account of its value;
- the purpose and audience of recognition;
- a form the person can accept;
- feasible provision of any offered benefit;
- proportionate criteria and correction where opportunities depend on them;
- a way to change an unwanted, misleading or distorting response.

An ordinary thank-you can rely on a known episode. A public competence claim, scarce award or promised service needs the corresponding assessment, decision or provision.

### CB.6:8 - Common Anti-Patterns and How to Avoid Them

| Wrong turn | Practical repair |
| --- | --- |
| Praise activity without finding its useful contribution | Obtain the relevant receiving account and name what it supports. |
| Credit only the person who presented joint work | Recover preparation and other contributions and agree the attribution. |
| Make every acknowledgement public | Choose the audience with the person's circumstances and preferences in view. |
| Promise an introduction or learning place without its provider | Obtain a bounded commitment and feasible access first. |
| Treat popularity or message volume as contribution quality | Inspect the contribution and its use; revise the misleading incentive. |
| Turn recognition into a new unpaid obligation | Agree new work separately and respect a refusal. |
| Replace owed payment or missing support with appreciation | Fulfil the actual obligation or obtain the missing provision. |

### CB.6:9 - Consequences

The contributor can understand what their work helped, and the organiser can offer a response that has real meaning and feasible terms. Less visible contributions become easier to notice. Recognition can remain small and personal.

Finding the receiving effect and providing an opportunity consume effort. A recurring award also requires criteria, correction and capacity. When those costs add little value, a truthful direct response is preferable.

### CB.6:10 - Rationale

Recognition connects a contribution with a person's understanding of its value and of their relation to others. The same gesture can convey different meanings because its audience, terms and the person's circumstances differ. Those conditions provide reasons for choosing a response; the recipient's account helps establish how it was received.

An incentive also changes what is worth producing under its rules. A measure that is easy to increase can draw effort away from the receiving need. Inspecting the work and the recipient's experience makes that displacement visible and gives the organiser a specific repair.

### CB.6:11 - SoTA-Echoing

| Source line or developed treatment | Contribution used | Adaptation and limit |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Spinks, The Business of Belonging (2021), physical PDF 119–129 | Return the experienced value of contribution and consider different forms of acknowledgement, status, connection, access and benefits. | Treat these as options. Claims that payment always displaces care, that every community needs status growth or that a peer reward system prevents gaming are too broad for choosing the response. |
| Self-Determination Theory, especially CET and OIT | Distinguish interest in an activity, instrumental motivation, internalisation and the perceived autonomy or control of social responses. | These distinctions guide questions about the arrangement; they do not predict one person's reaction to a particular award without evidence. |
| CB.5 and LDR.1:4.4 | Return a contribution's actual result and conduct a task-focused conversation about what helped and what needs changing. | Appreciation has its own purpose; technical acceptance, developmental feedback and an organisational decision retain their criteria. |

Compared with an unexamined prize or leaderboard, this construction requires more attention to the contribution and recipient but can reveal misleading attribution, unsuitable terms or an absent provider. The simpler alternative is often a specific acknowledgement.

Source returns: [Spinks](https://www.wiley-vch.de/en/areas-interest/finance-economics-law/the-business-of-belonging-978-1-119-76612-4); [the official SDT account, including CET and OIT](https://selfdeterminationtheory.org/theory/). Reconsider the response when the contribution, its receiving effect, the person's preferences or the promised opportunity changes.

### CB.6:12 - Relations

- **Uses CB.5:** an account of the contribution and its returned use.
- **Uses CB.2/4:** a useful connection or shared activity when offered as recognition.
- **Uses CB.3:** a changed responsibility, support condition or reduced voluntary commitment.
- **Uses [LDR.1 (LDR.1:4.4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/LEADERSHIP-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#ldr144---perform-the-smallest-sufficient-working-conversation):** a task-focused conversation and return when a concrete working contribution is needed.
- **Uses [HCD](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Human%20Capability%20Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/HUMAN-CAPABILITY-DEVELOPMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md):** the learning contribution or assessment actually promised by an opportunity or qualification claim.
- **Combines with CB.12:** recognition may accompany useful repetition, while the repeatable action and its conditions require their own construction.

### CB.6:End

## CB.7 - Prepare and Sustain Distributed Organising

> **Type:** Community-building method pattern
> **Status:** Stable

### CB.7:1 - Problem frame

Use this pattern when useful participation depends on one organiser, when capable organisers cannot attend to several emerging needs at once, when a local group needs people who can sustain its work, or when an invitation through the most visible members fails to reach the people concerned. A founder still receives every request. A willing host has a title but cannot obtain a room or help with a difficult meeting. A quiet practitioner is the person others actually trust to explain a change.

The governed work is organising participation through capable, willing people and usable relationships. It includes finding those people, preparing a bounded contribution, enabling temporary groups of participants to support the activity, and arranging continuation or handover. Some organisers hold formal positions; others help without one. The source and scope of any authority to decide for others must be established separately.

**First useful result:** a willing person or temporary participant group can perform an agreed organising contribution at the next relevant occasion, with the access, preparation, time, help and decision boundaries it requires. Where continuity matters, another feasible performer or an honest reduced promise is also available.

Start with the useful activity and the contribution currently missing. A general ambition to "develop leaders" is insufficient to choose whom to involve or what to teach. CB.5 can identify the receiving contribution; LDR.1 develops the explanation, working conversation or preparation that enables it.

**Ordinary non-use:** a stable small exchange already has sufficient willing support and a workable absence arrangement. Continue it without creating a leadership programme. If the intended result is wider use of a practice among independent people, CB.1 may instead select an explanation or learning provision. Shared decision rights that are absent or contested require CB.13; assigning more organisers alone will not establish them.

### CB.7:2 - Problem

An organiser's visible work can conceal the relationships and conditions that make participation possible. Copying the role title leaves those conditions behind. Selecting only frequent speakers can omit competent helpers, people trusted by a less visible group and people who perform essential preparation.

Delegation also fails when the initiator continues to settle every exception, or gives away work while retaining every useful decision. The apparent replacement cannot act, participants return to the founder, and a volunteer absorbs an indefinite burden.

A capable host can also miss simultaneous difficulties: some participants have no usable way to contribute, a discussion consumes the time needed for another question, and a recurring practical problem goes unnoticed. Training the host may help a capability gap. A shortage of attention can instead call for participants to share a bounded observation and support task.

The practical problem is to obtain the needed contribution and its continuation while preserving local knowledge, willing participation and an effective way to handle limits.

### CB.7:3 - Forces

| Force | Working consequence |
| --- | --- |
| Reach and trust differ. | A widely known speaker may not be the person a particular group consults about its work. |
| Willingness and preparation differ. | Interest makes an invitation possible; representative work reveals what preparation remains. |
| Local freedom and shared commitments interact. | Local organisers need room to adapt, while participants must be able to rely on the common promises. |
| Continuity takes spare capacity. | A nominal substitute who has no time or access cannot cover an absence. |
| Attention and conference time are limited. | A support group needs a bounded task and time to interpret its observations together; adding duties can consume the participation it should enable. |
| Evidence about relationships is partial. | Missing nominations or messages may reflect the inquiry's boundary, not isolation or lack of contribution. |
| Support can become dependence. | Keep useful assistance while making it possible to perform without the initiator doing the work. |

### CB.7:4 - Solution

Construct the next organising contribution around the people who can actually supply and receive it. Investigate relationships only far enough to choose a useful action; prepare performance around a representative episode; then arrange a sustainable next occasion.

#### CB.7:4.1 - Name the contribution and the failure it should remedy

Describe what participants need to receive. For example, a local session needs an attainable place and a prepared host; new members need an answer from someone who can help; a reference needs corrections returned to its maintainer. Recover what currently happens, including work performed before and after the visible event.

Identify which part depends on a particular person and why. They may hold the only key, know the relevant specialist, settle a disputed responsibility or supply the explanation nobody else can give. These are different replacement problems. Reassigning the meeting chair will not replace an unavailable specialist. When capable organisers cannot notice or support several aspects of the activity at once, consider the variant for temporary participant groups in :4.7.

Make the commitment small enough to understand and large enough to complete. Agree the intended result, next occasion, expected effort, relevant conditions, available support and a point at which the person can reconsider. Name any preparation, follow-up or emotionally demanding incident work; an hour hosting a meeting may require several hours beyond the meeting itself.

Separate the work from the authority it needs. A volunteer may arrange an event within an existing offer but need a venue owner to approve access and a community decision-maker to change membership conditions. Use the effective arrangements supplied by CB.13 or the relevant organisation. OCE.6 can obtain an assignment and enabling contributions where those need to be established.

#### CB.7:4.2 - Find the actual paths of help, explanation and influence

When the relevant people are already known through recent work, approach them directly. When reach or understanding is uncertain, start with the question the relationship evidence must answer. "Who helps new repairers judge an unfamiliar case?" selects different relationships from "Who can book the room?" or "Whose explanation do members of this local group consult?"

Ask people about concrete episodes and whom they approached. Include participants outside the most visible circle and an accessible way to answer privately. Compare their accounts with permitted observations or work traces. Arrange a useful introduction with the introduced person's agreement; leave the connection unresolved when the available accounts are insufficient.

Keep the different relations intelligible. Being named frequently, connecting otherwise separated groups, being trusted on a subject and holding a mandate can belong to different people. OCE.2 supplies recovery of actual work, authority and resource relations when formal descriptions are misleading. A simple account of who supplied which help may be enough.

Use the inquiry to choose an action:

| What the inquiry reveals | Possible next move |
| --- | --- |
| A group already obtains reliable help from a willing local practitioner. | Ask whether that person wants a bounded organising contribution and what support it needs. |
| Two groups have useful knowledge but no workable exchange. | With their agreement, obtain an introduction or a joint case through CB.2/5. |
| The central organiser hears only one group's account. | Obtain the missing group's own account before adapting the offer. |
| Several people rely on one overburdened intermediary. | Reduce the promise, prepare another contributor or provide an additional route that participants can actually use. |
| People understand the proposal but find the practice unsuitable. | Return the substantive problem to its owner; changing the messenger will not repair the practice. |

With a willing participant who understands the receiving group's work, prepare the explanation of the proposed change. Start from a familiar case: what could people do differently, what useful result is expected, and which conditions or commitments would change? Use language and examples the recipients can inspect. Return a substantive objection to the person who can change the practice or offer.

Ask a recipient to work through that case and explain what they would do next and where they would seek help. If the action or consequence is misunderstood, repair the explanation and try the affected part again. If it is understood but infeasible or unwanted, return that condition instead of repeating the message. Later use can show whether the explanation was sufficient for the actual circumstances; agreement with the organiser is not the test of understanding.

Explain why information about relationships is being obtained, who will see it and what decision it will inform. Obtain only what that use needs. A public network drawing can reveal relationships that people did not agree to disclose; use a restricted account or an aggregate explanation when that is sufficient. If participants use a shared drawing to reconsider their cooperation, obtain agreement to that activity as well.

Distinguish evidence used to select an organiser from changes to the relationships themselves. Later, inspect whether the promised exchange occurs and supplies value. Increased connectivity or similar behaviour among connected people alone cannot establish that the organising intervention caused improvement.

#### CB.7:4.3 - Invite and select for the actual work

Make an individual, intelligible offer: the contribution, its recipient, available freedom, expected burden, support and way to leave or reduce it. Ask what the person wants from the work and which conditions would make it feasible. Declining the role can leave their existing participation valuable.

Use evidence appropriate to the contribution. A potential host can work through a representative session; a prospective moderator can explain how they would respond to a difficult case; a maintenance organiser can show how they obtain and return a correction. Assess the needed work, rather than using visible enthusiasm or friendship as its proxy.

Select preparation and support from the gaps revealed. One person may need the local access procedure, another practice in giving a brief, and another a qualified colleague for a domain judgement. A person who can welcome members need not be able to teach the practice or decide a complaint. Obtain those separate contributions where needed.

Make selection understandable when several people seek a role. State the work criteria, who selects, how relevant conflicts are handled and how an unsuccessful applicant can obtain a useful explanation. Keep the inquiry proportionate: a small voluntary hosting task may need one working conversation, while a role with consequential decisions requires the relevant mandate and preparation.

Recognise the contribution through CB.6 in a form the person values. Distinguish that acknowledgement from compensation, expenses, access and time needed to make the work possible. If the community needs dependable service beyond willing voluntary capacity, change the provision or promise.

#### CB.7:4.4 - Prepare the first episode and make support obtainable

Use LDR.1's brief, practice and debrief around the actual next contribution. Explain the purpose and demonstrate the difficult part where that helps. Let the new organiser explain their own next actions and practise a representative difficulty. Return concrete feedback; if a capability must be learned or assessed, obtain the corresponding HCD contribution.

Make the working guidance useful at the point of action. A local host may need a room contact, an accessible invitation example, the session's purpose and shape, and a response to an interruption. Put a frequent action near its cue and a consequential exception near the decision it changes.


Obtain the actual conditions: permission and access, material, time, a person who will answer a difficult question and a feasible response window. A name in a contact list is insufficient if that person is unavailable. Agree what the organiser should pause or reduce while help is absent.

Arrange peer support where organisers have recurring related problems. Use a feasible rhythm for exchanging cases and working examples that help with the next contribution. Keep sensitive incidents within the agreed handling route.

After the first episode, inspect the receiving result and the organiser's work. What could participants do? What required help? Which commitment took more effort than expected? Use the account to repair the specific condition. A completed training session alone does not show that the organising contribution can now be supplied.

#### CB.7:4.5 - Give local organisers usable freedom

For a local group or chapter, agree which shared commitments it carries and which choices it can make locally. Common commitments might concern a promised service, use of a shared name, access to a shared resource or a rule for reporting incidents. Local choice might concern timing, examples or a meeting format. Select these boundaries from the actual common work.

Give each retained central decision a reachable decision-maker and a response time compatible with local work. Give local decisions the means to take effect. An organiser who may choose a meeting time but cannot obtain the venue does not yet have a usable arrangement.

Work through an ordinary variation and a consequential exception. Can the host change a format when attendance is small? Who handles an event that would exceed the agreed budget or exclude a group previously promised access? What happens if two chapters want the same resource? Return unresolved decision rights to CB.13 and resource conditions to their owner or ECO.7.

Let local observations change the shared offer where warranted. Ask organisers what their participants could not use and bring back the outcome of that request. Otherwise local autonomy can become permission to absorb the failures of an inflexible central promise.

#### CB.7:4.6 - Continue, hand over or reduce the promise

Review the next contribution while there is still time to change it. Check willingness, effort and support as well as completion. If work repeatedly exceeds the accepted commitment, reduce the activity, obtain more provision or change the arrangement with its recipient.

Prepare a successor through real work. Introduce the relevant people, transfer permitted working material and obtain their own access. Explain unresolved commitments and consequential exceptions. Agree when responsibility changes and tell the recipients whom to approach.

Where independence from the initiator matters, let another prepared person perform an episode without the initiator doing it for them. Preserve the domain assistance, accessibility support and decision routes that the work legitimately requires. Inspect what the new organiser obtained, where they still needed help and what prevented completion.

A willing, capable organiser may continue under the applicable appointment arrangement; another role may need rotation to share learning or burden. An expiring mandate still needs its proper renewal. CB.13 supplies that decision, while this pattern prepares someone to perform the resulting contribution.

If no feasible successor or support can be obtained, inform participants of the reduced or ending offer. Settle outstanding commitments, provide a usable route for remaining questions and preserve material under its applicable permissions. A declared handover is complete only when the recipient can take up the agreed work; otherwise return the missing condition or end that promise explicitly.

#### CB.7:4.7 - Let temporary participant groups support the activity

Select this variant when sharing attention or follow-up among participants could repair a specific difficulty. A small group might attend to opportunities to contribute, the usefulness of the agenda and time allocation, or what happens when people use a shared aid afterward. Choose the contribution that is missing. An adequate host, one helper or an existing maintenance arrangement may already supply it.

Invite willing participants to one bounded task for a meeting or an agreed period. Explain what they should help the community obtain, the effort and access it needs, and when the task ends. People may continue their ordinary participation without joining a support group. In a small community, combine compatible tasks or select fewer; one person need not carry several simultaneous observation duties.

Agree the group's scope with the people who can authorise it. Distinguish an action it may take during the activity from a suggestion requiring someone else's decision. A group may offer an already permitted way to submit questions, signal an agreed time limit or maintain an authorised summary. Changing membership conditions or settling a complaint still needs its proper decision route. Use CB.13 when the required mandate does not exist.

Provide time for members to confer before, during or just after the activity, or at a feasible interval for a longer task. Include that time when designing the activity through [CB.4](#cb4---design-a-space-and-shared-activity-for-its-purpose). Agree how they will obtain the relevant observations without collecting unnecessary personal information. Silence can mean wanted quiet participation; ask about an apparent obstacle before treating it as exclusion. Incident details belong in CB.8's handling route.

At the agreed occasion, compare what members noticed and what it could mean for their task. Select a feasible response, act within the agreed scope and observe what the response enables. Where the change needs another person's decision, return a concrete proposal to that person and obtain a response. A group with no time to confer has not supplied this shared interpretation merely by collecting individual notes.

Return what happened to the participants or organisers who can use it. A proposed change to the next agenda or participation format goes to CB.4's activity construction; a question about what the activity helped people do goes to [CB.10](#cb10---understand-the-value-of-participation-and-cultural-work). Keep an observation, its interpretation and a claim of benefit distinct. For example, a newly used question route shows access to that exchange; whether it changed later practice needs the recipient's account and appropriate further observations.

Close the temporary assignment at its agreed limit. Settle unfinished suggestions with a willing receiver, retain only permitted useful material, and decide whether a later occasion needs the task again. If so, agree its burden and performers anew or arrange the handover in :4.6. Reduce or stop it when the observation or conference burden prevents useful participation or nobody can receive its result.

### CB.7:5 - Archetypal Grounding

#### CB.7:5.1 - A local explanation reveals the missing condition

In a constructed repairer association, the main meeting host invites members to return tested corrections to a common reference. The invitation reaches regular speakers, but a smaller weekend group rarely responds. The organiser initially suspects that the message needs more emphasis.

They ask people in both groups whom they consult after a difficult repair. Members of the weekend group name a practitioner who rarely attends the main meeting. With that person's agreement, the organiser obtains a conversation about recent cases. The group already understands the request; its members cannot prepare the confidential customer details expected by the current submission form.

The practitioner helps test a permitted account that retains the configuration and observed result while removing unnecessary customer information. A maintainer confirms that this is sufficient for the selected class of correction. One weekend repairer submits such an account and receives a useful return.

The newly found connection helped recover the obstacle. The repair was to the contribution conditions, not simply the volume of promotion. The practitioner agrees to introduce the new route once; they do not accept an indefinite duty to collect every case. The maintainer and organiser provide the continuing route.

#### CB.7:5.2 - Another host carries the next meeting

A garden group relies on its founder to open the room, welcome participants, explain the day's shared work and settle every uncertainty. A volunteer offers to host the next meeting.

They identify two replaceable contributions: room access and the preparation brief. A venue steward supplies authorised access; an experienced gardener remains available for unfamiliar growing questions. The volunteer observes one brief, rehearses the next task with feedback and agrees what to do if attendance or conditions change.

At the next meeting, the founder is absent. The volunteer opens the room, gives the brief and adapts the grouping to the people present. A plant-health question goes to the experienced gardener. The selected hosting contribution works without the founder while subject expertise remains available.

The debrief reveals that returning equipment takes longer than agreed. The group obtains a separate helper for that task. If no helper is available, it reduces the equipment-dependent activity rather than silently extending the host's commitment.

#### CB.7:5.3 - A local chapter reaches the limit of its freedom

A professional chapter may choose the timing and examples for an agreed case session. It may also use a shared name under stated conditions. Its host wants to turn the session into a paid service with a new guarantee.

The host can propose that change but cannot establish the guarantee or spend the central association's funds. The relevant decision-makers consider the new commitment under CB.13; the host continues the already supported session while that question is open. Local initiative has produced a proposal with consequences, not an assumed expansion of authority.

#### CB.7:5.4 - A small exchange needs no leadership programme

Three neighbours already take turns preparing a monthly materials exchange. Each knows the bounded task, one can cover an absence, and unused materials have a clear destination. A proposal to create a leadership ladder would add selection and reporting work without improving the exchange. They retain the arrangement and revisit it only if the work or conditions change.

#### CB.7:5.5 - A capable host cannot notice everything at once

In another constructed repairer session, eighteen people have ninety minutes to compare difficult cases. The host and a successor can both facilitate, but the previous session left an emerging question unheard while one account consumed most of the discussion. Further host training is one option; the group first tries sharing two bounded attention tasks.

Four participants volunteer in two pairs for this session. One pair attends to usable opportunities to contribute. The other attends to time and questions that the agenda is failing to serve. Their scope permits offering the existing written-question route and signalling the agreed time limits. The host retains decisions about changing the agenda. Each pair has five minutes together during a planned pause and ten minutes just after the session; the volunteers accept this burden for one occasion.

One member of the first pair notices that three speakers have taken every turn; the other notices an unused written-question route. During the pause, they compare these observations and ask a quiet newcomer whether the available routes work for them. The newcomer wants to ask a question without interrupting the case. A written question reaches the host, who gives it a place in the remaining discussion. The second pair identifies two related questions that cannot both receive a useful treatment in the remaining time. It proposes a focused next session; the host accepts that proposal through CB.4 and a participant agrees to prepare the shared material. The pairs neither infer everyone's wishes from silence nor grant themselves authority over the community's programme.

At the debrief, the first pair reports that the newcomer received a relevant reference. The second pair confirms the next-session preparation with its willing receiver. Both temporary assignments end; the existing organiser and successor remain responsible for their own agreed contributions. Through CB.10, the organiser later obtains the recipient's account: the reference supplied the previously missing condition needed to select a diagnostic check. This is one bounded account of value, not proof that the group arrangement generally improves repair performance.

The next event is shortened to thirty minutes, and only one participant offers additional help. There is no feasible pair or conference time. The organiser retires the group variant for that event, asks the willing helper to collect unanswered questions for the final three minutes, and defers the wider participation inquiry. The smaller contribution remains useful without claiming that a group performed the task together.

### CB.7:6 - Bias-Annotation

Visible participation is easier to notice than quiet preparation, private help or work by people absent from central meetings. Relationship inquiry can also reproduce the organiser's existing circle. Compare concrete episodes from the affected groups, retain uncertainty about missing connections and allow people to decline disclosure or a proposed role. Company chapter models bring useful support practices but do not make unpaid work or brand growth the purpose of every community.

### CB.7:7 - Conformance Checklist

For an ordinary next contribution, ask:

- What useful result will participants receive, and which organising work is missing?
- Why is the proposed person suitable and willing, and what preparation remains?
- Are their authority, access, time and help actually sufficient for the next occasion?
- Which choices are local, and where does an unresolved exception go?
- What did a performed episode reveal about value, burden and needed support?
- If a temporary participant group is used, can it confer, act within its scope and return a useful response before its assignment ends?
- If continuation matters, can the next person perform the work, or has the promise been reduced honestly?

A stronger claim about professional competence, influence or cultural continuation requires evidence appropriate to that claim. The ordinary result here is the obtained organising contribution and its supported next use.

### CB.7:8 - Common Anti-Patterns and How to Avoid Them

| Misuse | Repair |
| --- | --- |
| Appoint the most visible member and assume they reach everyone. | Recover the relationships relevant to the missing contribution and include the affected group's account. |
| Give a title without access or a usable decision boundary. | Obtain the specific enabling condition and establish the decision right through its proper source. |
| Treat an enthusiastic volunteer as permanently available. | Agree the burden and review point; change the promise when provision becomes insufficient. |
| Supply a large playbook while the difficult action remains unexplained. | Develop that action around a representative episode and feedback. |
| Let the founder rescue every trial of a successor. | Let the prepared successor perform while retaining the support the role actually needs. |
| Centralise every exception under the name of consistency. | State the common commitment and give local organisers the choices and means compatible with it. |
| Give every participant several observation duties and no time to confer. | Select fewer voluntary tasks, provide conference time and a receiving decision, or use a smaller individual contribution. |
| Interpret an unsuitable practice as a communication failure. | Return the substantive defect to the practice or offer owner. |

### CB.7:9 - Consequences

Participants obtain organising help through a broader and more understandable arrangement. The community can preserve useful local knowledge, change an unrealistic promise and continue a selected activity when the initiator is absent.

Preparation and spare capacity cost effort. Relationship inquiry and shared working guidance also need maintenance when people or conditions change. Use them where they alter the next contribution; stable small arrangements can remain simple. Temporary support groups also spend participants' attention and conference time. Their continued use depends on what those contributions enable, not on keeping the groups in existence.

### CB.7:10 - Rationale

Organising is effective when someone can obtain the contributions that let others participate. Those contributions depend on specific relationships and conditions. Recovering them explains why a nominal replacement or a generic leadership course can fail.

A representative episode links preparation to the receiving work. A later episode performed by another person tests the continuity actually claimed. Keeping authority and expertise separate makes it possible to distribute useful work without inventing powers or demanding that one organiser know everything. Temporary participant groups add a different contribution: several people notice, interpret and support an aspect of the activity together. Their return can change the next activity even when the host already knows how to facilitate it.

### CB.7:11 - SoTA-Echoing

The practice question is how to obtain distributed organising when relationships, local conditions and continuity matter. The useful line combines inquiry into actual work with a bounded offer, situated preparation, usable local freedom and observation of the next contribution. Where concurrent attention is missing, a temporary participant group can add shared observation, conference, permitted intervention and a received proposal. It accepts the cost of preparation and support in place of relying on visible enthusiasm or a founder's continuing rescue.

| Source or developed treatment | Contribution adopted or adapted | Limit that changes use |
| --- | --- | --- |
| David Spinks, *The Business of Belonging* (2021), physical PDF 30–32 and 114–118. | Guidance, peer support and local choice help make organising feasible; :4.3–4.6 ties them to a defined contribution. | Historical company programmes illustrate possibilities. Leadership is an optional contribution, and local arrangements need their own resources and authority. |
| [Valente, “Network Interventions” (2012)](https://ctbs.fsu.edu/sites/g/files/upcbnu2651/files/Valente_2012%2BScience.pdf), introduction and individual-selection discussion. | :4.2 distinguishes people frequently consulted, connections between groups and groups left outside the initial inquiry. | These are choices for investigation, not a universal ranking of organisers or proof of an effect in this community. |
| [Maya-Jariego (2025)](https://reference-global.com/download/article/10.2478/connections-2025-0002.pdf), typology and conclusion. | :4.2 separates relationship information used in preparation, changed relationships, shared interpretation and observation of implementation. | The distinction clarifies the work being attempted; effectiveness depends on the actual contribution and conditions. |
| Wenger-Trayner et al., *Communities of Practice Guidebook*, second edition (2023), printed 83–84 / physical PDF93–94, §3.3. | :4.7 adapts temporary participant groups that confer, support an activity and return suggestions; :5.5 varies available attention and time. | This practitioner construction is optional. The authors' experience claim of accelerated development is not a causal effectiveness result; voluntary participation, available time and actual authority determine the local arrangement. |
| OCE.2:4 and LDR.1:4.1–4.6. | Recover actual relations; prepare a working contribution; observe a subsequent episode without making one person indispensable. | Use their concrete recovery and preparation Methods. A roster or an agreement alone does not supply the performed result. |

The temporary-group variant is an analytical adaptation of the [Communities of Practice Guidebook](https://www.wenger-trayner.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/03/24-02-21-CoP-guidebook-second-edition-final-2.pdf) by Etienne Wenger-Trayner, Beverly Wenger-Trayner, Phil Reid and Claude Bruderlein, under [CC BY-NC 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc/4.0/). The repairer cases are constructed explanations.

The alternative of a central organiser remains sufficient when the work is small, reliable and sustainable. Distributed organising is useful when it repairs a real reach, capability, attention or continuity problem; adding roles for its own sake increases the burden.

Reopen the arrangement when a relied-on relationship, participant commitment, access condition, mandate or receiving result changes.

### CB.7:12 - Relations

- **CB.1/2** identifies the useful offer and helps establish a first exchange; **CB.3** enables entry or a change of responsibility.
- **CB.4/5** supplies the activity design and receiving contributions; **CB.6** supplies appropriate recognition. **CB.10** investigates the value a participant group observes; its findings and proposals can change the next CB.4 activity.
- **CB.8** supplies usable participation rules and incident handling. **CB.13** establishes or repairs a missing shared decision mandate.
- [OCE.2:4](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/ORGANIZATION-CHANGE-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#oce24---solution) recovers actual work and relations. [LDR.1:4](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/LEADERSHIP-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#ldr14---solution) develops the concrete enabling conversation, preparation and next episode.
- [OCE.6](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/ORGANIZATION-CHANGE-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#oce6---establish-holder-assignments-and-enabling-relations-for-organization-change) supplies effective assignments and enabling relations when needed; [HCD](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Human%20Capability%20Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/HUMAN-CAPABILITY-DEVELOPMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md) supplies the specific learning or assessment contribution.
- [ECO.7:4](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/ECONOMIC-REASONING-AND-COORDINATION-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#eco74---solution) supplies rules for an actually constrained shared resource or maintenance contribution.

### CB.7:End

## CB.8 - Make Participation Rules Usable and Resolve Incidents

> **Type:** Community-building method pattern
> **Status:** Stable

### CB.8:1 - Problem frame

Use this pattern when participants cannot tell what conduct is expected, when different organisers apply a rule differently, or when an incident requires a response that people can understand and challenge. A useful disagreement is being treated as disloyalty. Advertising crowds out requested help. A participant can no longer access the only channel through which they could appeal their exclusion.

The governed work is making rules of participation usable and handling an incident under a valid decision arrangement. It includes clarification, proportionate protection, fact finding, response, appeal and return. Establishing who may make shared rules or review the decision-makers is the separate CB.13 question.

**First useful result:** the people concerned know the applicable expectation, the action being taken and its reason, and a reachable way to obtain correction or review. Where an incident is open, someone capable and authorised owns its next action.

An organiser needs the relevant activity and its conditions, a person able to receive concerns, and the actual scope of the available decision authority. A simple clarification can start with one unclear expectation. A consequential restriction needs grounds and authority proportionate to that action.

**Ordinary non-use:** a civil disagreement about the practice can continue through subject reasoning and CB.4/5 without becoming a conduct case. Existing understood rules may already be sufficient. Use the relevant qualified service for a danger or allegation beyond the community's handling capability.

### CB.8:2 - Problem

A rule can be visible but hard to apply. "Be respectful" may leave participants unable to distinguish sharp criticism, repeated personal attack and refusal to provide private information. A long code may still omit who can decide or what happens when the moderator is involved.

Incident handling fails in several ways. An organiser can rush to judgement, demand an unsafe confrontation, repeatedly tolerate an action that prevents participation, or hide a correctable mistake behind the rule's authority. A public explanation can itself disclose the information that should have been protected.

The task is to preserve useful participation while resolving the specific conduct question with an intelligible basis, an effective response and available correction.

### CB.8:3 - Forces

| Force | Working consequence |
| --- | --- |
| Immediate protection and a considered judgement have different timing. | A temporary measure can be needed before the facts supporting a final restriction are settled. |
| Common expectations meet different circumstances. | Language, accessibility, role and prior conduct can change what a fair response requires. |
| Explanation and privacy interact. | People need reasons without unnecessary exposure of reports or personal details. |
| Familiarity helps and can bias judgement. | A moderator's relationship to the people or dispute can require another decision-maker. |
| A rule needs real handling capacity. | An appeal promise fails when the same unavailable or conflicted person receives every route. |
| Criticism can be uncomfortable and useful. | Distinguish the subject claim from conduct that prevents others participating. |

### CB.8:4 - Solution

Connect each needed rule to the activity it supports and an action people can perform. Make the ordinary route usable before an incident, then handle a particular incident through its actual facts, available protection and applicable decision arrangement.

#### CB.8:4.1 - Explain the expectations needed for the activity

Begin with the useful participation and a concrete way it can fail. In a case exchange, participants need to challenge the proposed explanation and protect confidential case details. In a shared feed, repeated promotional posts may make requests hard to find. At a workshop, access or equipment rules may be necessary to carry out the activity.

State what a participant should do, the relevant boundary and why it matters. Include a short example where the distinction is easy to misread. For instance: disclose an affiliation when recommending one's own tool, explain its relevance to the question, and use the designated offer space for unrelated promotion. A blanket restriction may still be appropriate for a particular activity; choose it for that activity's conditions.

Separate subject standards from conduct rules. A technical claim can be wrong and need correction; a person can challenge it strongly while following the participation rules. Conversely, a correct claim does not justify repeated personal intimidation. Domain judgement and incident handling may both be needed, with different recipients.

Make expectations available where people enter and act. Explain the few consequential conditions before an event, link the relevant guidance at a posting point and provide an accessible way to ask. A participant should be able to understand an expectation before making a conspicuous contribution. Explain real limits on privacy instead of promising that anything said in a group will remain confidential.

Retain adequate existing rules. Revise an unclear or ineffective rule through its actual decision arrangement, with affected participants able to explain the practical consequences. State when the revision takes effect. Clarifying future conduct and judging past conduct are different decisions; do not silently treat a newly invented expectation as an earlier agreement.

#### CB.8:4.2 - Provide a workable receiving and decision route

Identify who receives a concern, what that person can do immediately and who may make a consequential decision. State how someone can report a problem privately and how urgent problems are routed while the usual receiver is absent. Give a realistic response window.

Test access from the position of a person who has been excluded, has left the venue or does not use the main platform. A route outside the restricted space can be essential. State who sees a report and the practical limits of confidentiality before inviting sensitive details.

Choose people able to understand the context and make the relevant judgement. Provide preparation, time, backup and permission to step away through CB.7. A person receiving hostility should be able to transfer the case.

Provide a way to disclose and handle a consequential conflict of interest. The challenged moderator, their close collaborator or an opposing party in the dispute may have useful evidence while being unsuitable to decide the case. Identify a replacement or an agreed external route under CB.13.

Keep the process small enough to operate. A small group may need two named contacts and an agreed alternate. A large or high-consequence setting may require trained staff and a more formal arrangement. If no adequate route exists for the claimed level of protection or review, change that promise and obtain the missing provision before relying on it.

#### CB.8:4.3 - Establish what happened and what needs action now

Receive the concern without deciding its truth from the reporter's status or tone. Recover the relevant action, context, time, affected participation and requested help. Obtain the smallest evidence that can settle the next decision: a complete exchange rather than one isolated sentence, an account from the people present, or a relevant observation.

Separate what was observed, what someone reports, their interpretation and what remains disputed. Retain enough context for later review. Restrict access to sensitive material and avoid reproducing it in a public discussion of the case.

Ask what must be protected while the issue is examined. Where the available authority permits it, a temporary pause, removal of exposed private information, separation of an interaction or alternate contact can preserve participation. State the scope, reason, responsible person and next review point. A temporary protection measure does not by itself establish responsibility for the alleged conduct.

Choose the next contribution from the question that needs to be settled:

| What remains to be settled | Appropriate next contribution |
| --- | --- |
| People understood different meanings or lacked relevant information. | Clarify the expectation and context, then check whether the difficulty remains. |
| People disagree about the practice or a proposed result. | Obtain the subject discussion or qualified judgement through CB.4/5. |
| An observed action may breach an applicable participation rule. | Compare the action and context with that rule, hear the relevant response and decide within the available authority. |
| People dispute the rule, decision-maker or mandate itself. | Preserve any currently justified protection and return the governance question to CB.13. |
| The matter exceeds the community's capability or authority. | Obtain the appropriate service or institutional response and limit the community's own promise. |

Several questions can coexist. A useful criticism can contain an impermissible disclosure; preserve the criticism's subject while acting on the disclosure.

#### CB.8:4.4 - Select and carry out a proportionate response

Give the person whose conduct is in question enough of the concern and relevant basis to respond, subject to the protection needed for others. Ask for missing context and correct factual mistakes. Where an immediate action had to precede this hearing, provide the opportunity as soon as the conditions permit.

Choose the response from the actual difficulty, seriousness, recurrence and foreseeable effect. Useful responses include clarification, a request to change or repair the action, a warning with an understandable next expectation, a bounded restriction or ending access to the activity. Follow the applicable decision rule. A serious incident may justify a stronger permitted response immediately; a fixed ladder of warnings cannot decide every case.

Consider whether a support or design defect contributed. An inaccessible posting rule, an unclear boundary between critique and personal comment, or an overloaded channel may need a repair alongside any individual response. A communication difficulty alone is not evidence of hostile intent.

Explain the decision to the people concerned. State the conduct or uncertainty on which it rests, the rule and scope, what changes now, how long a restriction lasts or what condition ends it, and how review can be requested. Give practical instructions for continuing permitted participation where that remains possible.

Use direct conversation when it can help and the people concerned can take part freely. Do not make a person confront the subject of their complaint as the price of receiving a response. A written exchange, an intermediary or separate conversations may be more workable. Choose the form that preserves the needed account and usable participation.

Make the selected response effective. Confirm the actual change to access, event arrangements or material, including the owner needed to perform it. For an in-person event, that may be the venue's responsible host; on a platform, it may be an administrator whose scope differs from the community moderator's. An announcement alone does not alter the operating condition.

#### CB.8:4.5 - Make appeal and correction possible

Offer a review route proportionate to the decision's consequence. Explain the grounds that can change it, such as relevant new facts, mistaken application of a rule, an excessive response or a conflict in the original judgement. Provide the person with enough reason to formulate the request and a route they can still reach.

Have a suitably authorised person who can reconsider the case examine the challenge. For a consequential disputed judgement, obtain someone other than the challenged decision-maker. The original moderator can supply their reasoning and evidence while the reviewer makes the reconsideration. CB.13 establishes who can fulfil that function if the arrangement is missing or contested.

Compare the decision with the relevant rule, facts and protected interests. The outcome may retain, change or reverse it, or return a missing inquiry. Explain the result and any further route that actually exists. Repeated identical submissions need not reopen settled facts indefinitely; a material new fact or a newly established conflict can reopen the affected question.

Correct what the mistake changed. Restore appropriate access, remove an unsupported public implication, revise an incorrect record and inform the people who relied on it. Restoring a post does not require redisclosing private material; provide a corrected permitted version when necessary.

Where return after a justified restriction is offered, agree the next expected conduct and support from the participation problem that led to it.
