Obtain the authorised access or activity change and inspect the next participation against that expectation. Retain the original reason where it still holds; a useful return need not reverse the earlier judgement. If access ends, state the scope and settle remaining practical matters through the applicable arrangements.

#### CB.8:4.6 - Learn from cases without making the case public

Keep a proportionate account sufficient to explain consequential decisions, support review and recognise recurrence. Use the normal permitted record and its access and retention conditions. An informal clarification may need no separate incident file; a consequential restriction needs a recoverable basis.

Inspect whether the response restored the wanted participation and whether it displaced the burden. A quieter feed may reflect restored usefulness, people leaving, or inability to report. Ask affected people through a workable route; absence of public complaints is not enough to distinguish those outcomes.

When cases reveal an unclear rule, incompatible decisions or missing support, repair that condition through its owner. Compare similar relevant cases while preserving their consequential differences. Prepare moderators on the revised expectation and confirm that participants can discover and use it.

Explain a community-wide change at the level people need: the working difficulty, the changed expectation, when it applies and how to ask or challenge. Protect case details that are unnecessary for that explanation.

Close the incident when the selected actions and any review are complete, the people concerned have the usable result, and outstanding work has a recipient. Reopen for material new evidence, failed protection, an inaccessible review route or recurrence that defeats the earlier remedy.

### CB.8:5 - Archetypal Grounding

#### CB.8:5.1 - Correct a mistaken removal without losing the rule

In a constructed online repairer group, a rule permits a relevant answer about one's own tool when the affiliation is disclosed; unrelated offers belong in a separate space. A moderator removes a member's linked answer as self-promotion and temporarily restricts posting from the member's account. The member can still reach the independent appeal contact by email.

The appeal includes the original question and the complete answer. The member disclosed their connection, explained the tool's relevant limitation and answered the requested comparison. The moderator who removed it also sells a competing tool and supplies their account without judging the appeal.

The alternate reviewer finds that the removal misapplied the rule. The answer and the member's posting access are restored, the moderator's public implication of an undisclosed interest is corrected, and the member receives the reason. The group keeps the rule that separates relevant disclosure from unrelated promotion.

A later post from the same member advertises a discount without answering a current question. Its different content supports moving it to the offer space. Correcting the first decision did not make every later post exempt.

#### CB.8:5.2 - Receive an offline complaint after the event

At a volunteer workshop, the host repeatedly interrupts one participant's account of a scheduling problem. The participant leaves and later reports through the alternate contact printed on the invitation. They do not have to attend another meeting chaired by the same host to be heard.

The alternate obtains accounts from the participant, host and a willing witness. The scheduling concern is real and returns to the organiser responsible for access. The interruption also prevented the participant from making that concern intelligible. Under the group's existing decision arrangement, the alternate obtains an authorised organiser's assignment of another facilitator for the next discussion. The host receives concrete feedback on receiving concerns.

The participant can use the new route without a private reconciliation with the host. The contact explains what will change and checks whether that route is usable. If the authorised organiser cannot obtain a replacement, the contact explains the remaining gap and obtains a different workable arrangement before promising the change.

#### CB.8:5.3 - Protect first, then settle a disputed judgement

A member posts a screenshot containing another person's private contact details. Under a previously established moderation rule, the on-duty moderator can hide that material immediately and preserve a restricted copy for review.

The screenshot is hidden and the affected person receives an explanation and a contact. The moderator then hears the poster's account. It is still disputed whether the exposure was deliberate. The immediate protection is justified by the exposed information; a further sanction is decided from the applicable rule and evidence, without treating the unresolved intention as a proven fact.

If the moderator accidentally hid an accompanying technical correction as well, they restore a permitted version of that correction. The useful subject contribution and the privacy problem receive distinct outcomes.

#### CB.8:5.4 - A disagreement needs a subject answer

Two members disagree about which maintenance interval applies to a particular device. Both state their reasons and leave room for a response. The organiser asks for the relevant configuration and source, then obtains a qualified comparison through CB.5. There is no conduct restriction to impose merely because the discussion is uncomfortable or ends without agreement.

#### CB.8:5.5 - Offer return while retaining a justified restriction's reason

In another constructed repairer group, a member repeatedly inserts advertisements into requests for technical help. A moderator explains the rule and shows the separate offer space. The member repeats the advertising, displacing the requested comparisons, and posting is suspended under the group's stated rule. The decision gives its reason and an email contact for review.

The member later asks to return. An authorised alternate confirms the original basis and offers a supported return within the existing moderation mandate. The member wants to answer questions about work they also sell. Together they examine a proposed answer: it needs to address the question, disclose that interest and keep unrelated offers in the offer space. A willing moderator can check the next two technical answers within an agreed response time. They agree to resume ordinary posting once the member has prepared those two answers to meet the stated expectations.

The administrator enables submission for approval. In the first answer, the member explains which repair their service cannot perform and supplies a relevant alternative; the moderator approves it. For the second answer, the member removes an unrelated discount before submission; the moderator approves the technical comparison. The questions receive usable answers. After these two exchanges, the moderator confirms the agreed condition and the administrator restores ordinary posting. The earlier advertising and its justified restriction remain in the proportionate account of the case.

This result shows that the offered arrangement worked for those exchanges. Further unrelated advertising would reopen the response from the new conduct and its effect. If the promised moderation support becomes unavailable, the reviewer and member need another workable arrangement before relying on it.

### CB.8:6 - Bias-Annotation

Public posts and articulate complaints are easier to inspect than private exclusion or the experience of someone who has already left. Moderators can also identify with familiar contributors or mistake an unfamiliar communication style for misconduct. Recover context and affected participation, make alternative reporting possible and separate consequential conduct from agreement with the organiser. The amount of public approval is evidence about expressed reactions, not a complete measure of fair or effective handling.

### CB.8:7 - Conformance Checklist

For the present rule or case, ask:

- Can a participant understand what to do and obtain the relevant guidance at the point of participation?
- Does the concern have an accessible receiver with the capability and authority for the next action?
- Are observed conduct, reported experience, interpretation and unresolved facts kept distinct?
- Is any temporary protection limited and subject to a real next review?
- Does the selected response address the actual difficulty, and has it taken effect?
- Can the affected person obtain an intelligible reason and a reachable, suitably independent review?
- Has an error been corrected in the access, material or understanding it changed?

Routine recognition can be brief. The evidence, hearing and review needed for a consequential restriction depend on its actual consequences and governing arrangement.

### CB.8:8 - Common Anti-Patterns and How to Avoid Them

| Misuse | Repair |
| --- | --- |
| Treat disagreement with an organiser as a conduct breach. | Preserve the subject question and identify any separate action that actually violates a rule. |
| Add rules after every discomfort without checking their use. | Recover the practical failure and test whether clarification or a direct repair is sufficient. |
| Apply a new rule as though everyone had already accepted it. | Explain its basis and effective time; judge earlier conduct under the applicable conditions. |
| Publish the complaint in order to demonstrate transparency. | Explain the decision while restricting unnecessary case details. |
| Require the reporter to negotiate directly with the person complained about. | Offer a feasible receiving and handling route with the needed protection. |
| Place the appeal inside the space from which the person is excluded. | Supply an accessible route outside that restriction. |
| Let a conflicted moderator decide the challenge to their own action. | Obtain the authorised alternate and retain the original moderator's account for consideration. |
| Call an announced restriction or correction complete. | Confirm the actual change and return it to the people who rely on it. |

### CB.8:9 - Consequences

Rules become instructions people can use, and an incident can end with a specific correction or supported restriction. A mistaken decision can be repaired without abandoning a useful expectation. Useful disagreement can continue while harmful conduct receives an effective response.

Handling and review require time, judgement and protection of sensitive information. A community that cannot supply them must narrow its promise or obtain the missing service. More elaborate procedure is worthwhile only where it improves the relevant decision and participation.

### CB.8:10 - Rationale

An expectation helps when a participant can recognise its application and act on it. Enforcement adds interpretation and consequences, so the decision-maker's actual authority, the evidence and the possibility of correction matter.

Temporary protection and final judgement answer different questions. Separating them permits timely action without turning uncertainty into guilt. Separating rule quality, actual handling and public reaction makes subsequent improvement more informative than adding another rule or counting approval.

### CB.8:11 - SoTA-Echoing

The practice question is how to maintain workable participation and correct mistakes in incident handling. The useful line connects clear expectations with accessible reporting, contextual judgement, proportionate response and effective review. It accepts the cost of hearing and correction where a mistaken response could materially exclude people or damage the activity.

| Source or developed treatment | Contribution adopted or adapted | Limit that changes use |
| --- | --- | --- |
| David Spinks, *The Business of Belonging* (2021), physical PDF 168–172 and 186–197. | Make expectations visible, sustain useful disagreement, explain enforcement and correct an organiser's mistake; :4.1–4.6 gives these actions explicit recipients and returns. | A rule or a friendly tone does not settle a contested fact. A direct call and public transparency are options whose privacy, access and power conditions must fit. |
| [Leibmann et al., “Reddit Rules and Rulers” (2025, v2)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.14163v2), longitudinal rule-change analysis and limitations. | :4.6 distinguishes a written rule, its execution and expressed perceptions of governance. | The study uses public discussion in sampled English-language Reddit communities; it does not observe enforcement or establish the right rule for this case. |
| [Lee et al., “Mapping Community Appeals Systems” (2025)](https://arxiv.org/html/2509.06557v1), moderator collaboration and offered reintegration. | :4.5 and :5.5 distinguish correcting an error from offering a supported return while retaining a justified restriction's basis. | Accounts from 17 Discord moderators with appeals systems do not demonstrate user-perceived fairness or rehabilitation. Evaluate the next conduct and available support; apology, tone and elapsed time do not supply that evidence. |
| [Community Canvas Guidebook (2017)](https://github.com/communitycanvas/documents/blob/master/CommunityCanvas-Guidebook.pdf), pp.47–49. | Anticipate conflicts involving members, staff and decision-makers; :4.2/:4.5 requires a real route beyond a conflicted original decision. | This historical repertoire does not itself establish the community's mandate; CB.13 supplies that construction. |
| OCE.14:4.1–4.6 and ECO.7:4. | Change a failing arrangement with its actual owner, conditions and returned effect; for a constrained common resource, combine use and upkeep rules. | Use OCE.14 for an existing organisation's revision and ECO.7 for the actual resource difficulty, rather than treating every disagreement as either problem. |

The alternative of informal clarification remains sufficient for an ordinary misunderstanding with limited consequences. A standing incident process becomes useful when consequences, recurrence or multiple decision-makers make that informal response insufficient.

Reopen the method's local use when reporting becomes inaccessible, an appeal lacks an independent decision-maker, a rule defeats useful participation or later evidence contradicts a consequential decision.

### CB.8:12 - Relations

- **CB.3/4** makes entry and shared activity usable; **CB.5** supplies subject contributions that a conduct process cannot judge merely by agreement or tone.
- **CB.7** prepares and supports organisers. **CB.13** establishes the authority to make rules, assign decisions and review challenges to decision-makers.
- [LDR.1:4.4](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/LEADERSHIP-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#ldr144---perform-the-smallest-sufficient-working-conversation) supplies a task-focused conversation when that is the needed contribution.
- [OCE.14:4](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/ORGANIZATION-CHANGE-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#oce144---solution) supplies a bounded revision of an established organisation; its owner and mandate must already be available.
- [ECO.7:4](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/ECONOMIC-REASONING-AND-COORDINATION-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#eco74---solution) supplies combinations of use, upkeep, observation and dispute rules for an actually constrained common resource.

### CB.8:End

# Part C - Provide, sustain and govern the arrangement

## CB.9 - Provide for Community Work and Agree Its Terms

> **Type:** Community-building method pattern
> **Status:** Stable

### CB.9:1 - Problem frame

Use this pattern when a useful community activity depends on contributions that have not been secured, when organisers are carrying work beyond what they agreed, or when a sponsor's conditions change what participants can expect. A room is paid for but nobody can prepare the session; several departments expect different results from one organiser; a voluntary contribution has become a service with fixed deadlines.

The subject is the arrangement that provides labour, money, access and other resources for cultural or community work. The move is to connect a bounded promise to willing contributions, effective authority and terms that the parties can sustain. The same provision construction can support a community's contribution to independent carriers: maintaining a public guide can need this arrangement even when its users never meet. Their cultural continuation does not thereby become a community-participation process.

The first useful result is an offer that can be delivered for a stated period: what will be provided, by whom, under which conditions, what participants and supporters receive, and what will change if support falls short. A smaller activity, a paid service, a different source of support or a decision to end an unsupported promise can be the result.

The intended user organises the activity and can obtain decisions from resource owners and affected contributors. The pattern uses accounting and operating results where calculation or scheduling needs specialist work.

**Ordinary non-use.** An already authorised, adequately provided activity can use its existing arrangements. If the remaining question is the value of the activity, use CB.10. If nobody can yet explain what contribution is worth supporting, return to CB.1 before seeking a larger budget.

### CB.9:2 - Problem

Community work consumes resources even when participants do not pay. Invitation, preparation, moderation, responding to difficult cases, maintaining material and handing over to a successor can remain hidden behind a successful meeting. Organisers may conceal that deficit by donating more time until an absence exposes it.

Support also has conditions. A sponsor may expect recruitment while participants expect confidential help. A platform provider can control access to material that the group depends on. Several beneficiaries can each assume another party is paying. Increasing income alone leaves these conflicting promises unresolved.

How can the organiser provide the useful work, agree who bears its burdens and benefits, and revise the promise before a shortfall becomes an involuntary obligation?

### CB.9:3 - Forces

| Force | Decision it changes |
| --- | --- |
| Useful promise and feasible supply | A more generous offer can help participants while exceeding the available capability or time. |
| Voluntary contribution and dependable service | People can willingly help within limits; a service promise may require firmer provision and compensation. |
| Several beneficiaries | Participants, employers, sponsors and independent users can gain different things and accept different costs. |
| Visibility and hidden work | Public sessions are easy to count; preparation, response and maintenance can determine whether they continue. |
| Resource ownership and community judgement | A funding decision can enable work without granting its owner every decision about participation or content. |
| Immediate support and future dependence | A convenient grant or platform can create a later renewal, withdrawal or transfer problem. |

### CB.9:4 - Solution

Build the arrangement from the promised activity and its resource dependencies. Compare support options with the affected people, obtain the actual commitments and test an ordinary use together with a plausible shortfall.

**Working reminder:** bound the promise; recover the work; compare support and terms; obtain commitments; try delivery; revise before the burden becomes hidden.

#### CB.9:4.1 - Bound the work that support is supposed to enable

State the receiving result and period. “Keep the community active” is too broad to show what must be provided. “Offer one monthly case discussion with a qualified facilitator and return the agreed resources” exposes a different demand from “answer every member's question within one working day.”

Recover each material expectation with the party that holds it. Participants may need dependable access; an employer may permit staff time to develop practice; a sponsor may want evidence of useful reach. Ask what each party offers, needs and has authority to decide. A staff member's enthusiasm does not allocate their employer's time.

Resolve incompatible promises before costing them as one programme. A confidential peer conversation and an identifiable sales-lead report require different permissions and participant expectations. Change the proposed terms, separate the activities or decline one promise when the conflict remains. CB.1 helps reconsider the contribution; CB.13 supplies a missing collective decision mandate.

For a group with several supporters, identify the person who can reconcile the demands on the same organiser. Retain the distinct results and receiving parties. Adding every possible sponsor benefit to one activity can multiply preparation, data collection and reporting beyond the activity's useful work.

#### CB.9:4.2 - Recover the resource demand, including quiet work

Walk through an actual or proposed occurrence with those who will perform it. Recover preparation, delivery and the follow-up needed for its result. Include access administration, conflict response and material maintenance where the promise depends on them. Ask what happens during absence or an unusually difficult case.

Express consequential requirements in usable units and time windows. Qualified facilitator-hours, a room booking and the organiser's time are different resources. An available room does not supply a facilitator, and eight hours available next month cannot meet a promise due tomorrow. Preserve the capability and timing that constrain the work.

Use [MA.1 (MA.1:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/MANAGEMENT-ACCOUNTING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#ma14---solution) to obtain a missing resource-consumption and cost account. It connects outputs, preparation, attempts and rework to resources and to the applicable payments. Use [MA.2 (MA.2:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/MANAGEMENT-ACCOUNTING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#ma24---solution) when supplied, used, reserved and unavailable capacity have been confused. Keep donated time visible as a constrained contribution. Express its monetary value only when the receiving comparison needs and can justify that valuation.

Recover what is already committed elsewhere. [OCE.11 (OCE.11:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/ORGANIZATION-CHANGE-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#oce114---solution) helps arrange overlap with continuing work; [OPS.5 (OPS.5:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/OPERATIONS-MANAGEMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#ops54---solution) supplies admission against the provider's actual commitments. Obtain those results from the people who can change the work or its provision. A community schedule by itself cannot release a contributor from their other duties.

Use enough detail to decide. A small group may need only the next session's tasks and contributors. A programme with staff, several locations or uncertain demand may need alternative schedules and a fuller account. Deepen a quantity when its plausible range changes the promise or the support decision.

#### CB.9:4.3 - Compare ways to provide the contribution

Construct support alternatives from the actual gap. Members may share a task, an employer may provide time, a sponsor may provide space, or a qualified supplier may perform paid work. A smaller or less frequent activity can remove a demand; changing a payment source alone cannot.

For each feasible alternative, compare the received contribution, the burden on each party and the consequential conditions. Ask who bears preparation and interruption, who controls the resource, which uses are allowed and what happens at renewal. A free platform may cost migration work or impose limits that matter more than its current price. Obtain its applicable terms when choosing it.

Separate the resource comparison from the funding mechanism. Membership dues, voluntary contributions, grants, paid services and commercial sponsorship can fund similar work while changing who can enter and whose interests receive attention. Explain these effects to affected participants. A group can choose mutual provision without becoming a business, and a commercial programme can pay for work while retaining a voluntary discussion space.

Use [OPS.14 (OPS.14:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/OPERATIONS-MANAGEMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#ops144---solution) for an unresolved operating and financial comparison. It follows the alternatives through actual resource use, displaced work, payment timing and funding. MA supplies the cost relations. A favourable total at the end of a quarter does not pay an earlier room deposit.

Choose with the authorised parties. State the accepted loss: fewer sessions, paid coordination, a narrower response promise or less sponsor reporting, for example. Keep the alternative or missing decision that would reopen the choice. Participation in an activity alone does not authorise the organiser to accept a new obligation on members' behalf.

#### CB.9:4.4 - Agree contributions, benefits and control

Make each consequential commitment understandable to its contributor and receiver. State the work or resource, period, limits, support, compensation or reimbursement where applicable, and how an absence or withdrawal will be handled. Obtain agreement from the person or owner who can actually provide it.

Distinguish an invitation to contribute from an accepted assignment. Ask what a volunteer is willing and able to do; leave a reachable way to decline, reduce or end the contribution. If continuity requires fixed delivery, provide an arrangement that can carry that obligation, such as paid work, a confirmed rota with backup or a smaller promise. Appreciation through CB.6 can support a relationship; it does not supply missing labour.

Agree how benefits and costs are shared. Explain who can use the result, whether someone pays for privileged access, what a sponsor receives and how contributors are credited. Bring a contested distribution to those with the relevant mandate. Agreement can include an accepted asymmetry, such as an employer paying while independent practitioners receive open guidance; equal contributions are not a prerequisite.

When use and renewal of a shared resource are the difficulty, use [ECO.7 (ECO.7:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/ECONOMIC-REASONING-AND-COORDINATION-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#eco74---solution). Its arrangement connects access, use, upkeep, observation, response and adaptation. CB.13 supplies a missing community mandate; resource owners retain their actual rights. A majority vote among users cannot grant access that the owner has not provided.

Make sponsor influence legible. Agree who selects activities, who controls contributed material and what information may be returned. Provide a way to raise a conflict without risking loss of ordinary participation. If a proposed condition changes the original offer, return it to affected people before relying on their earlier agreement.

#### CB.9:4.5 - Make the arrangement operable and try a shortfall

Obtain the allocations, access and practical support needed for the next activity. OCE.6 helps establish assignments and enabling relations; the receiving provider must accept work that depends on its resources. Check that a person can perform their part, rather than treating a named owner as provision.

Run one ordinary occurrence through the arrangement. Can the host enter the venue, can the facilitator prepare, and can the promised result reach its user? Inspect the first use and obtain contributors' accounts of work that the plan omitted. Correct the promise or the provision where they differ.

Also examine the shortfall most likely to defeat continuation: a key person's absence, a delayed payment, a loss of access or demand above the agreed limit. Decide who will reduce, postpone or cancel the affected promise and how participants will learn that decision. A simple backup may be enough. If no backup is feasible, make the limit part of the offer.

Keep the response proportionate to the reliance. A casual discussion can be postponed; a promised professional service may need a qualified replacement and explicit settlement with its users. Ask the relevant owner for the needed arrangement before accepting that reliance.

#### CB.9:4.6 - Reconcile support with actual work

At a useful interval, compare the accepted promise, delivered result, resource use and remaining support. Ask contributors privately where public gratitude could make a burden difficult to report. Look for extra preparation, repeated requests outside the agreed scope, deferred rest and work silently displaced elsewhere.

Distinguish a shortfall in supply from a change in the contribution itself. More applicants may require admission or a smaller promise. A sponsor's new sales objective may require a new agreement. An activity with little practical value needs CB.10 and possibly CB.1; simply funding more of it may preserve the wrong offer.

Revise the affected commitment with its holder and receiver. Obtain the replacement allocation, payment or reduced expectation and communicate it to those who rely on the old one. Record only what people need to act and later settle the arrangement; a small group need not create a permanent reporting system.

Before an important support period ends, establish whether renewal is real, conditional or absent. Use CB.11 for continuation, transfer, separation or ending that changes the arrangement as a whole. Preserve useful resources and settle current commitments even when the future programme is reduced.

### CB.9:5 - Archetypal Grounding

#### CB.9:5.1 - A funded room with an unfunded organiser

In a constructed repair community, two clinics each month require twelve expert-hours and six coordination hours. Two experts each offer six hours, but the organiser offers only four. Room and consumable payments total 120 currency units per month. Paying those 120 units leaves the two-hour coordination gap.

The group compares two supported options for the next three months. One monthly clinic requires six expert-hours, three coordination hours and 60 units. An available unrestricted grant can fund it. Keeping two clinics requires the original 120 units plus two paid coordination hours at 30 units each, or 180 units per month. A sponsor offers 540 units for the period with recognition on the invitation and a report of the number of completed clinics.

The organiser verifies that the additional coordinator can handle booking confirmations within the paid two hours, that the experts still agree to their six hours, and that the sponsor pays before bookings fall due. Members on alternating work schedules can reach only one of the two dates. The community's authorised decision therefore keeps both clinics, accepting the additional payment and sponsor recognition to preserve that access. The coordinator accepts the work and receives the booking access; the sponsor receives the agreed aggregate report.

The first clinic reveals another hour spent returning tools and resolving an unfinished booking. The group places that work with a willing contributor who has access and time. It also narrows the invitation: specialist advice is available at the clinics, with no promise of continuous replies between them.

When one expert becomes unavailable for a later date, the other cannot safely cover both tables. The organiser uses the agreed response: reduce that clinic to one table and tell booked participants what can be served. The funded venue does not justify preserving the original capacity claim.

#### CB.9:5.2 - When a voluntary contribution becomes scheduled production

A professional association invites members to contribute examples when they wish. A new customer contract would require revised examples every Friday in a prescribed format. Before promising that service, the association separates the open contribution space from the scheduled production.

A willing qualified member accepts paid editorial work with allocated time and a replacement arrangement. Others can continue offering examples under the original voluntary terms. The customer receives only the production promise that has been provided. If the paid role cannot be filled, the association defers that contract instead of converting the contributors' earlier enthusiasm into a weekly duty.

#### CB.9:5.3 - Keeping access without compulsory payment

A neighbourhood group wants newcomers with little money to use a shared room. Some members can fund bookings; others offer preparation and cleanup. The group obtains the owner's permission, compares the upkeep burden and agrees supported open sessions under an existing mandate.

At the first session, the paid booking and cleanup both occur. Later, an additional session has no willing cleanup contributor. The group postpones it rather than automatically charging the newcomers or leaving the task to the usual host. If the same conflict persists, ECO.7 helps revise access and upkeep together.

#### CB.9:5.4 - Independent users and sufficient existing provision

A small public guide has a willing maintainer, authorised hosting and a clear way to report corrections. Its users apply the practice independently. When the next update fits that established provision, the maintainer makes it under the existing arrangement; no membership programme or new funding exercise is needed. A future loss of hosting or maintenance would reopen the provision question.

### CB.9:6 - Bias-Annotation

The organiser may underestimate their own time, while a sponsor can make its visible benefits easier to report than participants' quiet gains. People with less money or less control over their work time can be excluded by an apparently equal contribution rule. Ask whose burden is missing from the arrangement and whether they can decline without losing unrelated access. A favourable account from continuing contributors leaves departed or silent people unexamined.

### CB.9:7 - Conformance Checklist

For an arrangement on which people will rely, establish the following at the detail needed for that reliance:

- The promised result and support period are understandable to the receiving parties.
- Material labour, capability, access and payment requirements have willing, authorised provision.
- Other commitments and the timing of payments have been considered where they affect feasibility.
- Contribution terms, benefits and sponsor influence are understandable to the affected people.
- Volunteers can decline or revise their contribution, and any dependable service has appropriate provision.
- An ordinary use and a consequential shortfall have a workable response.
- Actual burden and the end of the support period can change the promise before it becomes unsupported.

The arrangement can support reliance when the contributions, permissions and terms actually hold. Its description makes those conditions inspectable. Delivery and later value require their own observations.

### CB.9:8 - Common Anti-Patterns and How to Avoid Them

| Failure | Better move |
| --- | --- |
| Count a grant as complete provision | Recover the qualified work, access and timing that the money still needs to obtain. |
| Treat donated time as unlimited | Agree a contribution and its limit with the person who will perform it. |
| Promise every department a different benefit from one organiser | Reconcile the receiving results and provide the work each requires. |
| Use gratitude to carry a continuing service deficit | Provide the missing work, reduce the service or end the unsupported promise. |
| Count an allocated cost reduction as spendable cash | Obtain the actual capacity and payment consequences through MA/OPS. |
| Accept a sponsor's new condition under members' old agreement | Return the changed offer and obtain the affected decisions. |
| Keep a schedule by silently shifting the burden to the remaining volunteer | Apply the agreed reduction, replacement or postponement and revise the arrangement. |

### CB.9:9 - Consequences

Participants receive a more credible offer, contributors can see and question their obligations, and supporters can understand what their provision enables. A small activity can continue with mutual support; a service that requires dependable production can receive a different arrangement.

Making hidden work visible can reduce the apparent scale of the programme or increase its stated cost. Obtaining agreement and testing a shortfall also take effort. The gain is a choice made while alternatives remain available, rather than an unexplained cancellation or an exhausted organiser.

### CB.9:10 - Rationale

A community promise is realised through several people's work and through resources they may not control in common. Connecting those contributions makes a support decision actionable. Separating the benefit, contribution and authority of each party also exposes conflicts that a single budget cannot resolve.

Voluntary and paid contributions can coexist when their terms are clear and workable. The decisive question is what reliance the arrangement can support. Reopening that question when work or conditions change protects both the useful contribution and people's ability to choose their participation.

### CB.9:11 - SoTA-Echoing

This pattern compares community programme design with the actual operating and accounting methods needed to provide it. The alternatives are compared at the effort of arranging one supported activity or programme period, rather than at the scale of an organisation-wide transformation.

| Source or approach | Adopt, adapt or reject by value | Trade-off and limit |
| --- | --- | --- |
| David Spinks, *The Business of Belonging* (2021), physical PDF pp.59, 62–76 and 191–197 | Adapt the connection between objectives, programme work, support and organiser effort. Retain explaining consequential changes to participants. | The business framing makes sponsor support concrete but does not make revenue the purpose of every community. Its lifecycle and numerical anecdotes are historical heuristics, not universal staffing rules. |
| Duolingo, [announcement ending volunteer course creation](https://blog.duolingo.com/ending-honoring-our-volunteer-contributor-program-2/) | Use the company's explanation of its move to paid production as a bounded contrast: commercial conditions and standardised delivery can change an earlier voluntary arrangement. | This is a historical company account, not an independent finding about contributors' outcomes or a rule that every voluntary contribution must become paid. |
| MA.1/2 and OPS.5/14 | Adopt actual resource dependencies, available capacity, commitment admission and incremental payment timing when these questions are unresolved. | This adds calculation where it can change the choice; a small already supported activity can use a simpler account. The calculation does not grant authority. |
| OCE.6/11 and ECO.7 | Adopt actual assignments and enabling relations, compatibility with continuing work, and shared-resource access/upkeep arrangements. | These suppliers develop the organisational and economic work; CB.9 connects it to the participants' offer and the supporters' terms. A common purpose alone supplies none of those permissions. |

The current useful answer combines a community contribution with effective provision and revisable terms. It retains independent access and non-commercial support as positive options. Current provider terms, payment conditions and applicable rules must be obtained for the particular arrangement; a historical example cannot establish them.

### CB.9:12 - Relations

- **CB.1** supplies the worthwhile contribution; **CB.2–5** supply the participation and work that consume resources.
- **CB.6/7** support recognition, willing organisers and succession. CB.9 provides the work without treating recognition as compensation for an unagreed obligation.
- **CB.8/13** supply workable expectations, complaints and community decision authority.
- **CB.10** investigates value and burden; **CB.11** changes or ends an arrangement when support or purpose changes.
- **MA.1/2**, **OPS.5/14**, **OCE.6/11** and **ECO.7** supply the dependent cost, capacity, assignment and shared-resource results described in the Solution.
- **CB.14** can require a separately provided development project. Independent carriers can use the resulting practice without joining the funding or discussion group.

### CB.9:End

## CB.10 - Understand the Value of Participation and Cultural Work

> **Type:** Community-building method pattern
> **Status:** Stable

### CB.10:1 - Problem frame

Use this pattern when people need to decide whether a cultural or community arrangement is useful, what to change, or what support to continue, and visible activity does not answer that question. Meetings may be well attended while the intended help remains absent. A quiet reader may use a guide successfully without posting. An employer may ask what changed in practice after staff spent time together.

The subject is the value and burden of the arrangement for its participants, contributors, independent users and supporters. The move is to recover particular experiences and uses, connect them with relevant observations, and obtain an account that informs a practical decision. This includes cultural work whose carriers do not communicate with one another.

The first useful result states what people received or did, what changed, which contribution the evidence supports, what remains uncertain and which condition should be continued or changed. A useful conversation or sense of welcome can be a result in its own right. A promise of improved work also needs evidence from that work.

The intended user can talk with affected people and obtain or commission the observations needed for the decision. The pattern develops practical inquiry and feedback. A causal estimate, specialist capability judgement or formal method trial requires the corresponding method and evidence.

**Ordinary non-use.** When the result is already established at the needed scope and the only difficulty is allocating the agreed support, use CB.9. A routine attendance count needed to book a suitable room can remain a routine count. Return here when its interpretation or the activity's usefulness is in question.

### CB.10:2 - Problem

The easiest observations often concern the organiser's activity: events held, messages posted or resources published. The benefit may occur later, elsewhere and through a combination of contributions. People can enjoy a session without using its advice, use a resource without telling its author, or obtain an important result from one brief exchange.

Stories and numbers have complementary limits. A general success story can omit the action that supposedly helped. An indicator can change because the people, work or conditions changed. Listening only to those who continue participating can hide exclusion, unsuccessful use and the cost of leaving.

How can an organiser investigate the contribution and its limits well enough to improve the arrangement, while keeping the inquiry proportionate and useful to those who supply its evidence?

### CB.10:3 - Forces

| Force | Decision it changes |
| --- | --- |
| Visible activity and value elsewhere | Easy counts help locate work but can miss later independent use. |
| Participants' meanings and comparable observations | An account must retain what matters to people while supporting the comparison needed for the decision. |
| A convincing story and a warranted contribution claim | A detailed sequence can make a contribution plausible without identifying its causal effect. |
| Learning and accountability | Supporters need an account, while excessive reporting can consume the participation being supported. |
| Positive use and missing voices | Available successes can conceal non-use, harm, burden or people who could not enter. |
| Stable observation and newly discovered value | A consistent indicator supports comparison; an unexpected use may require a different question. |

### CB.10:4 - Solution

Begin with the practical decision, follow specific experiences into their receiving use, and compare the account with observations that could confirm, limit or contradict it. Return the result to participants as a reason for a concrete next move.

**Working reminder:** choose the decision; recover an occurrence; follow its use; compare evidence and missing cases; change a condition; return and observe the result.

#### CB.10:4.1 - Name whose decision and whose value matter

Recover the question with the person who will use the result. Continuing a monthly exchange, correcting an invitation, funding a guide and claiming reduced service cost require different evidence. Name the period and the change being considered, including leaving an adequate arrangement alone.

Ask affected people what would count as useful and what would count as an unacceptable burden. Keep differences visible. A participant may value a dependable answer, an organiser a manageable workload, and a sponsor a competent workforce. A gain for one party can coexist with a loss for another.

Distinguish the claims before selecting observations. Useful questions can concern experience and inclusion, relationships, access to a resource, ability to perform a practice, actual use, results in that use, or the provision and governance of the arrangement. Choose the question that matters to the receiving decision.

For a cultural result, use the distinctions in CB.1 and [FPF C.36](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/FPF-Spec.md#c36---cultural-evolution-and-cultural-evolution-engineering): people carrying a practice, their opportunities to enact it and the continuation of the practice are related but different subjects. A registration or download count alone does not establish any person's capability or later use.

Choose the smallest inquiry that could change the decision. An account of the last failed entry may suffice to repair a confusing invitation. A claim about programme-wide value needs a broader and appropriately selected evidence base. State the scope before a vivid case becomes a statement about everyone.

#### CB.10:4.2 - Choose observations and people who can answer

Find where the promised result would become visible. A participant can report whether they felt welcome; a recipient can show whether a handover let them act; an operating record can show later completion or repeated help requests. Use an existing adequate observation when its meaning, period and access fit.

An indicator needs a recoverable unit and population. For returning participation, distinguish people from visits and say who could have returned during the period. For response, distinguish an initial acknowledgement from a useful answer. If the observation method changes, retain that difference before interpreting a trend.

Use activity patterns to find informative accounts. Include a relevant contrast: someone who returned, someone who left after one attempt, a quiet user, or a contributor whose work stopped. Select the contrast because it can change the explanation. A few purposeful conversations can expose a repair without representing the whole population.

Provide an appropriate way to answer. People may need a private conversation, an accessible format or someone other than their organiser to receive criticism. Explain how the account will be used, obtain permission for attributed reuse and retain only detail needed for the inquiry. A refusal or missing response remains missing evidence.

Keep collection proportionate. Start with a small number of useful observations and cases; expand when plausible alternatives still change the decision. If the question needs specialist sampling or causal identification, obtain that contribution instead of treating more informal interviews as an equivalent.

#### CB.10:4.3 - Recover the path from experience to use

Ask for one particular occasion, preferably in the person's own account. Recover what happened, what the person received, what they subsequently did with it and what difference that made. A story can begin with a later result and work backwards. Check time order and retain where the account ends.

For social learning, the Guidebook's four basic value cycles help ask different questions:

| Part of the account | Useful recovery question | What the answer can establish |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Immediate experience | What happened in the exchange, and what did it mean to you then? | A reported experience, such as useful discussion or being able to ask a difficult question. |
| Potential resource | What idea, contact, explanation or other resource became available? | Something that could support later work, with its actual content or availability. |
| Applied use | What did you do differently, where and under which conditions? | An account of adoption or adaptation, including failed use. |
| Realised difference | What changed in the receiving situation, and what was that worth to those affected? | A reported or observed result and burden, qualified by the other evidence. |

Follow each consequential connection. If someone says the meeting improved a repair, ask which contribution they used and how it changed the action. If they received an idea but have not had an occasion to use it, keep that as potential value. Agree a later return only when it can answer the decision.

Recover the conditions that enabled or altered the path. The Guidebook distinguishes enabling value, such as obtaining time and support for practice, from strategic value, such as a conversation that connects the community's contribution with a stakeholder's decision. Understanding a relevant external development can provide orienting value. Transformative value concerns a change in participants' identity or the systems and culture around their practice, such as an adopted change to an organisation's rules. Follow these additional contributions when they help explain the use or its limits.

Ask about cost, unwanted effects and failed attempts with the same specificity. What extra preparation was needed? Who could not use the resource? Which expected result did not occur? A useful negative account can show where the proposed method or support fails.

Keep the person's experience and the investigator's interpretation distinguishable. Let the contributor correct an attributed account before it is circulated. If the purpose is simply a valued social experience, the immediate account can answer it; inventing a later productivity gain would change that purpose.

#### CB.10:4.4 - Compare the account with other observations

Seek the observation most capable of changing the account's meaning. It may be a recipient's response, an actual work product, an existing activity record or a relevant outcome indicator maintained for another purpose. Recover how it was obtained and whether it concerns the same people, work and period.

Compare the parts that can conflict. A participant may report that an answer resolved the issue while a later request shows that a necessary step remained unclear. A reported improvement may coincide with a different workload or new tool. Ask what the discrepancy changes; do not discard it to preserve the preferred story.

Use stories to interpret indicators and indicators to challenge stories. An unexpected rise in repeat requests can identify a case worth following. An unexpected successful use may reveal a benefit that the current indicator misses. Revise the question or indicator when the receiving decision needs that new meaning, retaining enough continuity to interpret the change.

Treat absent links explicitly. When a result is known but the supposed community contribution cannot be recovered, report the result without attributing it. When a contribution was used but no later outcome can yet be observed, return the use and the remaining question. Lack of a record is not automatically lack of value; it limits what the account can support.

[A.3.1.WT (FPF A.3.1.WT:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/FPF-Spec.md#a31wt4---solution) supplies a formal method-trial contribution when the question requires it, distinguishing intended work, what occurred, conditions, burdens and evidence reach. Its result can feed the community inquiry; retain the evidence scope of any ordinary accounts used alongside it.

#### CB.10:4.5 - Decide what the evidence warrants

State the strongest claim supported at the relevant scope. A report establishes what a person says they experienced. A checked occurrence can establish the action and result observed there. A connected and corroborated account can show which resource a participant used and what the recipient could then do. If that account is used to explain why an outcome changed, examine its causal support.

Recover the causal question: the effect of an intervention, an explanation of an observed difference or a counterfactual. [FPF C.28](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/FPF-Spec.md#c28---causaluse-cal-causal-use-questions-identification-and-realizability) helps determine the supported statement, its limits and the next useful evidence question. [RMP.1 (RMP.1:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/RESEARCH-METHOD-PRACTICE-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#rmp14---solution) first tests whether an adequate existing source answers that question and then whether the remaining inquiry warrants research. A persuasive story or a correlation between membership and performance does not provide missing identification.

Consider competing explanations that matter here. New participants, seasonal demand, another service, changed incentives or easier cases can alter the interpretation. Compare the conditions that could change the present explanation.

Keep the population claim within the evidence. A useful case can justify trying a repair without establishing how often the problem occurs. Several positive cases selected by an enthusiastic host still leave unsuccessful and nonresponding people outside the conclusion. Name a missing observation only when it affects reliance or the next decision.

Include burdens and distribution in the judgement. More answers can coexist with exhausted experts; a helpful resource can be inaccessible to newcomers. MA can supply a missing cost account, and HCD can supply a needed capability judgement. Do not collapse these unlike consequences into an overall “community health” number unless a warranted decision rule actually requires and supports that aggregation.

#### CB.10:4.6 - Change the condition that the inquiry exposes

Turn the account into a proposed action with a responsible receiver. A person who cannot find the first task may need a clearer entry through CB.3. A useful discussion that never reaches its intended work may need a recipient or application opportunity through CB.5. Missing time or access returns to CB.9; an inadequate practice returns to CB.14 or its actual method supplier.

Compare a change with a credible alternative, including retaining the current arrangement. Ask what useful result should appear, what burden it adds and what would make the change unsuitable. Provide the permissions, people and resources before treating the proposal as a commitment.

Try the change at the scale appropriate to the decision. Observe the particular use that prompted it and a consequential contrasting use when that distinction is still unresolved. Record what was changed and what actually happened; a simultaneous change elsewhere may prevent attributing a later difference to this one.

If the inquiry exposes a different purpose, return to CB.1 with the affected participants. If the arrangement no longer merits or can obtain support, CB.11 develops continuation, transfer, separation or ending. A decline in activity by itself does not choose among those responses.

#### CB.10:4.7 - Return the result and keep the learning loop useful

Return what was learned to those who supplied the experience and those who can act. Explain the supported benefit or failure, the limit on the conclusion and the next change. Use the detail needed by each receiver while preserving consequential uncertainty and permission for reuse.

Let participants use the account to improve their own practice. A failed application can become a correction to shared guidance; a successful adaptation can become a candidate for another context. CB.14 and Core's Method-development family qualify any wider method claim. A sponsor's summary can draw on that same grounded account without replacing it with an unsupported impact total.

Choose a next observation only when the question remains live. Follow an unfinished story when application becomes possible, or check the result of the chosen repair. Stop or simplify collection when it no longer affects decisions or learning. Preserve meaningful longitudinal comparisons when they are still used.

### CB.10:5 - Archetypal Grounding

#### CB.10:5.1 - Follow a useful exchange into a changed handover

In a constructed network of repair workshops, a sponsor asks whether monthly discussions justify protected staff time. Participants report useful conversations, but the current dashboard shows only attendance. The organiser selects a bounded inquiry: did the discussion help recipients act on difficult handovers, and what additional work did it require?

A participant recounts one exchange in which a colleague explained why the next workshop needed the actions already tried. The participant uses the shared handover form and adds those actions to a later handover. The receiving technician identifies the next inquiry without repeating that request. With permission, the organiser compares the account with the handover and the recipient's recorded first response. These observations support the claimed use and immediate result in that case.

A second participant copied the same handover form but filled its “prior actions” field with “usual checks.” The recipient still had to ask what had actually been done. A third participant obtained the explanation but has had no relevant case yet. The inquiry retains failed use and potential use alongside the first success.

The participants revise the prompt to request the actual actions and results and try it with the second workshop. Comparing preparation of two similar handovers, they observe that the revised account takes about two more minutes. Its recipient can select the next inquiry from the information supplied. The observed repair concerns handover information; equipment differences and other workshop changes still affect total repair time.

The organiser returns these cases, the preparation burden and the unresolved wider effect to participants and sponsor. They continue the protected discussion time for another limited period and observe repeated clarification requests for the relevant handover class. A later trend can challenge or extend the account; the present evidence does not establish that meetings caused a general reduction in repair time.

#### CB.10:5.2 - A quiet user and a useful practice

A person rarely posts in an association's forum but uses its public planning guide. An optional conversation recovers a particular plan they prepared and the parts of the guide they used. The receiving team confirms that the plan supplied the information needed for its next decision.

This is useful independent use with a local receiving result. The association keeps the guide accessible and improves its correction route. It does not require the person to post in order to count as a successful user, nor infer that every download produced the same capability or result.

#### CB.10:5.3 - More activity and a less useful answer

After a points programme begins, more short replies appear. Participants explain that any reply earns points. Recipients report that many replies repeat the question, and experienced contributors spend additional time correcting them. Comparing a few received answers with their follow-up requests confirms that visible response volume has risen without the hoped-for help in those cases.

The organisers use CB.6 to change what receives recognition and CB.5 to improve the answer-and-return practice. They compare the next answers for usefulness and correction burden. They retain the activity count as an observation, but it no longer stands for successful support.

#### CB.10:5.4 - An experience that does not need a productivity claim

A walking group exists partly for companionship. Members describe feeling welcome, name occasions when they could join a conversation and report that a changed meeting point excluded two regular participants. The organiser checks access with those participants and restores a reachable meeting arrangement.

The inquiry has changed the condition that matters. No claim about workplace performance is needed. If the group later promises a health effect, that new claim needs an appropriate evidence basis.

#### CB.10:5.5 - Sufficient evidence for an ordinary decision

A host needs a room for the next meeting. Confirmed attendance and the venue's capacity answer the booking question. The host uses them without starting a value investigation. If attendance remains high while people report that the meeting is unhelpful, that different decision reopens this pattern.

### CB.10:6 - Bias-Annotation

Positive stories are easier to elicit when the collector controls access, funding or recognition. Departed people, quiet users and unsuccessful applicants may remain invisible. Give criticism an appropriate receiving route, preserve nonresponse as a limit and seek a relevant contrary case. A numerical indicator can also favour what is easy to record; inspect whose work and experience its definition excludes.

### CB.10:7 - Conformance Checklist

For the intended decision, check that:

- The receiving decision, relevant people, period and claimed value are clear.
- Observations have meanings and populations suitable for that claim.
- Particular accounts recover the consequential connections from experience or resource to use and result, retaining missing links.
- Useful contrary observations, burdens and unsuccessful uses are included where they can change the judgement.
- Reports, observed occurrences, contribution explanations and causal claims retain their different evidence requirements.
- The proposed change has a receiver and the provision needed to try it.
- Participants receive a usable return, and continued collection has a reason.

A coherent local account can support a bounded improvement. A broader claim needs evidence with the corresponding reach.

### CB.10:8 - Common Anti-Patterns and How to Avoid Them

| Failure | Better move |
| --- | --- |
| Treat more posts or attendance as the promised benefit | Follow the activity into the experience or receiving work that matters. |
| Collect only promotional success stories | Recover specific use, conditions, cost and negative or incomplete outcomes. |
| Call an available guide an acquired capability | Obtain the relevant capability or use evidence, or retain the weaker access claim. |
| Attribute an outcome because the person belongs to the community | Recover the contribution and use a causal method when the causal difference is the question. |
| Explain a trend after the population or definition changed | Compare like observations or retain the change as a limit. |
| Make every participant file a complete value story after every event | Collect what can change the live decision and use voluntary, proportionate returns. |
| Measure indefinitely without changing anything | Make the receiving decision, revise the inquiry or stop collection that no longer helps. |

### CB.10:9 - Consequences

The organiser obtains a more useful account of contribution, failure and burden. Participants can learn from actual uses, supporters can judge a bounded continuation, and quiet independent use can remain visible without being converted into compulsory participation.

The method takes follow-up effort and can expose an unwelcome limit on an attractive claim. Some benefits remain uncertain or unobserved. Preserving that limit allows a proportionate decision while leaving the stronger unanswered question available for appropriate inquiry.

### CB.10:10 - Rationale

A contribution often matters through a sequence of changes in different places. Recovering the connections explains how the community could have helped; comparing them with other observations makes that explanation more answerable. Returning the account to practice turns evaluation into a useful contribution of its own.

Stories and indicators can correct each other's omissions. Neither makes causal identification automatic. Keeping the claim at its warranted scope also permits small practical repairs without requiring every community to become a research programme.

### CB.10:11 - SoTA-Echoing

The practical comparison is between activity reporting, a bounded inquiry that combines particular accounts with other observations, and a more demanding evaluation or causal study when its answer is needed.

| Source or approach | Adopt, adapt or reject by value | Trade-off and limit |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Wenger-Trayner, Wenger-Trayner, Reid and Bruderlein, *Communities of Practice within and across organizations*, second edition (2023), printed pp.209–222 | Adapt the four basic value cycles, the additional strategic/enabling/orienting/transformative contributions, and the reciprocal use of stories and independently monitored indicators. Preserve negative value, incomplete stories, learning loops and proportionate collection. | Following a specific use costs more than counting attendance but can explain a contribution. A plausible contribution account is kept distinct from an identified causal effect; the source's reservations about control in complex settings do not establish that causal identification is impossible in every case. |
| Spinks, *The Business of Belonging* (2021), physical PDF pp.130–137 and 173–175 | Adapt interviews and observations across different participation histories, including quiet users, to diagnose a concrete condition. | Reject universal engagement thresholds and the use of membership or activity correlations as evidence of causal business return. Local observations need their population, period and meaning. |
| A.3.1.WT with its receiving A.3.1.CC, A.3.1.FT and C.11.MV questions | Adopt the distinction between planned activity, actual occurrence, conditions, burden and evidence reach when a method trial supplies the inquiry. | A formal trial is justified by its receiving question. Ordinary feedback is useful at its own scope and is not silently relabelled as such a trial. |
| C.36, HCD, MA and C.28/RMP | Retain the distinct cultural, capability, cost and causal questions and obtain the actual supplier result when it is needed. | More specialised evidence can resolve a consequential uncertainty; demanding all of it for a simple local repair would burden participation without changing that decision. |

Guidebook source: [second-edition text](https://www.wenger-trayner.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/03/24-02-21-CoP-guidebook-second-edition-final-2.pdf), published at the 2024 URL, under [CC BY-NC 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc/4.0/). The account here is an analytical adaptation; examples are constructed and do not imply the source authors' endorsement.

The useful contemporary combination preserves the source's positive inquiry method while qualifying its claims through the actual evidence question. New collection tools can reduce effort, but their convenience does not establish what the observations mean or whose experience is missing.

### CB.10:12 - Relations

- **CB.1** distinguishes the cultural, participant and supporter results whose value is in question.
- **CB.3–8/12/13** provide entry, activity, recognition, organiser support, expectations, repetition and decision arrangements that an inquiry may improve.
- **CB.9** supplies resource and burden arrangements; **CB.11** uses the warranted result when choosing continuation or ending.
- **CB.14** receives useful adaptations and failures for qualification and maintenance of the repertoire; **CB.15/16** can supply contributions whose returns need following.
- **C.36** supplies cultural distinctions, **HCD** a needed capability judgement, and **MA** a needed resource or cost account.
- **A.3.1.WT** supplies formal trial evidence when selected; **C.28/RMP** govern an unresolved causal or research question.

### CB.10:End

## CB.11 - Continue, Change, Transfer or End a Community Arrangement

> **Type:** Community-building method pattern
> **Status:** Stable

### CB.11:1 - Problem frame

Use this pattern when the purpose, people or support of a cultural or community arrangement changes enough that its continuation needs a decision. A founder is leaving, a sponsor withdraws a platform, participants need different forms of participation, or the useful work has largely been completed.

The subject is the arrangement that people can actually use: its activities, relationships, organising work, resources and commitments. The move is to choose a feasible continuation or ending, carry the useful contributions into the chosen arrangement, and settle what will no longer be provided. The wider cultural practice can continue among independent carriers even when an organised activity ends.

The first useful result is an agreed continuation or ending, with the provision and remaining actions needed to carry it out. Where implementation is part of the work, the result includes receiving use under the new arrangement and settlement of the old commitments. An announcement or copied archive alone establishes neither.

The intended user is an organiser, a person taking over a contribution, or someone who can obtain the relevant decisions from participants and resource owners. They need access to the commitments and means of work that the change affects.

**Ordinary non-use.** A continuing arrangement that remains useful and adequately provided needs no reinvention because a lifecycle diagram labels it mature. A routine rota change within effective existing terms can use those terms. Use this pattern when the change affects what people can obtain, who can provide it or which commitments remain valid.

### CB.11:2 - Problem

People often identify a community with its current host, founder or meeting format. That makes loss of one component look like the end of everything, or lets a claim that “the relationships will continue” hide the loss of access, support and practical opportunity.

Continuing can also be the wrong response. The original task may be complete, organisers may be depleted, or a single arrangement may no longer serve different groups. Holding everyone together can consume resources and prevent useful work elsewhere. Conversely, ending too quickly can lose an explanation, a capable contributor or an opportunity that people still need.

How can participants choose and carry out a continuation or ending that preserves worthwhile possibilities without carrying unsupported promises into the future?

### CB.11:3 - Forces

| Force | Decision it changes |
| --- | --- |
| Continuity and changed purpose | A familiar arrangement can retain value while a different form better serves the next work. |
| Useful relationships and controlled infrastructure | People may wish to stay connected but still need access, contact routes and resources. |
| Succession and voluntary limits | A departing organiser can help a transition without owing indefinite support. |
| Common provision and local independence | Separation may serve different purposes while leaving a shared resource to coordinate. |
| Preservation and maintenance burden | Saving everything can retain unusable or sensitive material and create work nobody accepted. |
| Timely closure and unfinished obligations | A firm ending can release people while current users still need a receiving answer. |

### CB.11:4 - Solution

Recover what people still need and what has changed, compare feasible arrangements, obtain the decisions and provision, then perform the transfer or ending at the level of actual use.

**Working reminder:** locate the change; recover what remains worthwhile; choose and provide the next arrangement; test receiving use; settle the old promise.

#### CB.11:4.1 - Identify what is changing and who can decide it

Name the concrete trigger and its timing. A sponsor may withdraw funding at the end of a period; a host may stop volunteering next month; members may want different activities. Separate what is already decided from a proposal, concern or unexplained drop in participation.

Recover the affected arrangement. Which work will stop or change? Who relies on it? Which resource, access or authority will disappear? Ask both the people doing visible activity and those obtaining quieter benefits, including independent users of maintained material.

Distinguish control over components. A platform owner can decide what it provides; an organiser can end their own contribution; an authorised community body can decide matters within its mandate. Independent participants still decide what they are willing and able to do together. Use CB.13 when the collective decision arrangement itself is missing or disputed.

Respond to an immediate harmful or unsupported condition through the applicable owner and CB.8/9. A necessary short-term suspension can protect participants while the larger choice remains open. State what is suspended and where people can obtain the remaining response.

#### CB.11:4.2 - Recover the useful work and the means of obtaining it

Use CB.10's current account or a bounded inquiry to identify what is worth continuing, what has failed and what is no longer needed. Recover the received benefit and the burden of providing it. A founder's preferred form and a sponsor's preferred metric are inputs to the choice, not a substitute for the affected uses.

Follow one consequential use through its actual dependencies. A monthly case discussion can depend on a qualified facilitator, permitted case material, access to the venue and a way to return the result. A public guide can depend on a maintainer and a correction route while requiring no continuing meeting.

Use [C.36.RP (FPF C.36.RP:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/FPF-Spec.md#c36rp4---solution) when the missing contribution concerns obtaining and using a shared way of working. It locates the gap and compares access, capable help, learning, tools and method development. Retaining a finished answer can leave its interpretation or adaptation unavailable. Obtain the contribution that the later use actually needs.

Include relationships whose continuation requires willing participation. Ask who wants to remain in contact and through what arrangement. Preserve an available way to reconnect where people agree; do not treat a copied membership list as a renewed relationship.

Retain a worthwhile but not yet used possibility when there is a reason and proportionate means to keep it available. The choice need not be between funding a full programme and discarding every trace of its work. An accessible explanation with a clear maintenance limit may be sufficient.

#### CB.11:4.3 - Compare feasible continuations and endings

Construct alternatives that answer the actual change. The following forms have different practical consequences:

| Alternative | What must become true for it to work |
| --- | --- |
| Continue the present arrangement | Its useful purpose and the threatened provision remain or are restored. |
| Reduce or change activities | A smaller or different promise has willing contributors, adequate support and understandable effects on current users. |
| Pause for a stated period or condition | People know which services stop, who handles current obligations and what decision will restart or end the pause. |
| Transfer to another host | A willing capable receiver obtains the necessary access, authority, resources and means of work. |
| Separate into different arrangements | Each group can operate its chosen contribution, and any remaining shared resource or mandate has workable terms. |
| End the organised activity | Current obligations are settled and worthwhile reusable contributions have an appropriate destination or a clear ending. |

Compare benefit, burden, timing and transition loss with the people affected. Include retaining the arrangement when its difficulty can be repaired. Include ending when no worthwhile and feasible continuation is available. Growth, retention of every participant and a larger institution are not universal completion conditions.

Separate a promising alternative from a provided one. A person interested in taking over may still lack time, capability or resource permission. Use CB.7 to develop willing organising work and CB.9 to obtain support. If the needed contribution cannot be provided before the old one ends, reduce or suspend the dependent promise.

[ECO.9 (ECO.9:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/ECONOMIC-REASONING-AND-COORDINATION-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#eco94---solution) develops a change to economic rules through the practices people can actually perform, including entry, exit, transition burdens and wider dependencies. Use it when those rules are the obstacle. [OCE.14 (OCE.14:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/ORGANIZATION-CHANGE-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#oce144---solution) supplies a needed organisational revision.

When the effect is uncertain and reversal is practical, try a bounded continuation before committing the whole arrangement. Name the result and burden that will inform the decision, who can stop the trial and what remains available if it fails. A trial must fit the remaining support period.

#### CB.11:4.4 - Obtain the decision and prepare its practical consequences

Take each required decision through its actual authority. Obtain the resource owner's access decision, the successor's agreement and the community decision where one is required. Keep objections and accepted losses visible through CB.13. A departing volunteer may state the end of their own contribution while others decide how to replace it.

Translate the chosen alternative into what affected people need to do. Name the ending and starting dates, the remaining activities, how to reach the new receiver, which promises cease and where unfinished matters will go. Explain a change before users continue relying on the old offer.

Agree the handover work and its limit with both the departing and receiving contributors. Include the time needed to explain an unusual case, obtain access and practise a consequential operation. CB.7 and C.36.RP help obtain capability where it is missing; a title or a folder transfer cannot supply it.

Recover rights and permissions for the material that will move. Public instructions, contributed examples, private discussions and contact details can have different permitted uses.
