Obtain the relevant permission or leave the restricted material with an appropriate existing receiver. A useful summary can sometimes carry a lesson without carrying the personal account.

If a common resource remains after separation, agree its access, upkeep, decision and dispute arrangements through ECO.7 and the applicable mandate. If those terms cannot be made workable, choose a different resource or narrow what the groups share.

#### CB.11:4.5 - Transfer through receiving use

Provide the chosen receiver with the contributions their work requires. This can include explanations, tools, access, contact with a qualified practitioner and a way to obtain corrections. Preserve the conditions under which a method or resource can be used; the recipient needs to recognise when it is unsuitable.

Have the receiver perform the next consequential work under the new conditions. For an organiser, that could be preparing and running a meeting, responding to an access problem and returning its result. For an independent user, it could be finding the guide, applying the relevant instruction and reaching its maintained correction route.

Observe the gap that the attempt reveals. A missed permission calls for an access decision; an uninterpretable method calls for explanation or learning; a defective method calls for its actual development through Core's Method-development family or the domain practice. Repair the gap and try the affected receiving use again. Keep other usable contributions available.

For a platform move, obtain the applicable export, hosting and access conditions, then inspect the transferred content and a real receiving task. Navigation, links, search, contribution permissions and private material can behave differently. The practical test concerns the functions people rely on, not just the number of files copied.

Finish the departing contributor's responsibility at the agreed boundary. State which work has transferred, what remains unsupported and who has accepted any remaining handover. If the new arrangement cannot carry an essential promise, tell its users and reduce or stop that promise before declaring transfer complete.

#### CB.11:4.6 - Settle the arrangement that is ending

Review the actual remaining commitments with their receivers. Complete, transfer, cancel or renegotiate each one through the applicable arrangement. Paid bookings, promised feedback, borrowed equipment and unresolved participant matters can need different actions. A general closure announcement does not settle them.

Decide what remains accessible and who, if anyone, maintains it. Preserve useful methods, explanations and contact routes only with the permissions and provision needed for that use. Identify obsolete guidance as such where a reader could otherwise rely on it. A maintained public resource and an unmaintained historical archive serve different purposes.

Settle funds, resource access and credentials with the people who hold the corresponding authority. Follow the existing terms for unused contributions or shared property. Obtain a qualified decision where those terms are unresolved; the organiser should not invent a right to distribute or retain them.

Tell affected people what has ended, what remains usable and where the last unresolved work belongs. A final gathering or acknowledgement can help people recognise their shared contribution when they want it. It is not a condition that someone must satisfy before leaving.

Close the promised work at the scope actually achieved. An activity can end successfully after supplying its useful result. Relationships or cultural practice may continue elsewhere; say what has been arranged or observed and leave unobserved continuation open.

#### CB.11:4.7 - Observe the chosen continuation and revise its limits

After a transition, use a small number of relevant returns through CB.10. Can the next host obtain the necessary help? Can independent users still find and use the practice? Did separation remove the conflict or merely move an unresolved shared-resource burden?

Compare the result and cost with the choice. Revise the activity, provide a missing contribution or end the trial when its grounds fail. Preserve the distinction between a successful first use and continuation across later people or time; C.36 supplies the broader cultural question and its evidence requirements.

Revisit the arrangement when purpose, support or context changes again. The Guidebook's context-oriented development can reveal a new external constraint, partner or way to contribute. It can justify collaboration, challenge to an external condition or a different local agenda. It does not require the community to approximate a universal ideal of maturity.

### CB.11:5 - Archetypal Grounding

#### CB.11:5.1 - A departing founder and a closing platform

In a constructed volunteer repair circle, the founder will stop organising in two months and end the privately funded discussion platform. Participants still value a monthly case exchange and the public guide to preparing a useful case request. Some only use the guide and do not want to join another discussion service.

The group compares retaining two monthly meetings through paid coordination, reducing to one meeting with two willing organisers, and ending meetings while keeping the guide. The paid option lacks confirmed funding. Two members can provide the reduced schedule, and a local workshop offers an authorised room booking. Members choose that continuation through their existing decision arrangement, accepting the loss of the second monthly meeting.

The new organisers receive the booking route, the preparation method and access to a qualified facilitator. The founder agrees two handover sessions and no continuing response duty afterward. A willing maintainer accepts the public guide under its existing reuse terms and obtains hosting that will remain available after the old platform closes. Private case discussions remain with their permitted recipients; members receive an invitation to choose the new contact route.

In the first preparation attempt, the new organiser cannot obtain the room key at the required time. The workshop owner provides an actual keyholder arrangement. The organiser then prepares and runs the first reduced meeting with the facilitator, returns the discussed actions and confirms the next booking. An independent reader retrieves the transferred guide and includes the observations and prior actions in a case request. The receiving practitioner can identify the next inquiry. The reader also reaches the maintainer about a missing reference, and the maintainer corrects it.

The founder settles the old platform's remaining payment and returns borrowed equipment through their owners. An unfinished participant complaint retains its agreed receiver and response date, with a contact that remains reachable after closure. Before the platform closes, its entry page and a notice to participants give the ending date, the guide's independent address and the optional new group's contact route.

The result is a smaller activity with working support and continued access to the guide. It does not show that every former member moved or that the relationships will persist indefinitely. The new organisers review the next two meetings against their available time.

#### CB.11:5.2 - Separate purposes with a remaining shared resource

A music group contains people who want informal playing and others preparing public performances. Repeated attempts to combine both in one session leave each dissatisfied. The members choose two independently organised sessions rather than treating one preference as a failure of commitment.

Both groups obtain capable hosts and times. They still use a shared set of instruments, so the owners and authorised users agree regular slots, responsibility for returning the set and priority to the first confirmed booking for additional dates. The informal group uses its first session to help two newcomers join familiar pieces; the performance group rehearses its agreed concert programme without shortening it for that introduction.

Both later request the set for a Saturday. The informal group's booking was confirmed first, so the performance group takes an available Sunday slot. Each returns the set in time for the next user. The groups retain their different activities while using the shared resource under an arrangement both accepted.

#### CB.11:5.3 - End the forum and retain independently usable practice

A professional discussion forum has few new questions, while a qualified guide continues to be used. A bounded CB.10 inquiry finds that several users can perform their intended work from the guide and existing professional help; they do not need regular discussion.

The organiser confirms maintenance and correction support for the guide, settles open forum questions and announces the end of new posts. One later independent user retrieves the guide and obtains a correction through the retained contact. The organised discussion has ended, while that means of using the practice remains available. Independent users continue through the guide and the professional help they can already obtain.

#### CB.11:5.4 - A pause that does not promise an unavailable service

A seasonal field group cannot obtain the authorised site access needed for its next activity. It pauses field meetings until a named access decision or a stated review date. A willing receiver handles current enquiries and tells participants which alternatives are available.

At the review date, access is still unavailable and no capable host can provide the alternative site. The group ends the field programme for that season, returns the borrowed equipment and leaves permitted guidance accessible. The pause did not become an indefinite promise that nobody could fulfil.

#### CB.11:5.5 - Ordinary continuation

A reading circle still serves its purpose, its host remains willing, and the room and materials remain available. It continues under the existing terms. A label such as “mature community” supplies no reason to split, professionalise or replace it.

### CB.11:6 - Bias-Annotation

Founders can overvalue the form they created; successors can undervalue the work that made it usable. Active participants can overlook independent users, and a sponsor can equate the end of its platform with the end of the practice. Recover actual receiving uses and voluntary limits before choosing. People who prefer to leave need an effective exit, while those continuing need more than an assumption that somebody will maintain the shared work.

### CB.11:7 - Conformance Checklist

For the intended transition or ending, establish that:

- The changed condition, affected promises and decision authorities are clear.
- Worthwhile uses and the contributions needed to obtain them have been recovered.
- The chosen continuation, pause, transfer, separation or ending has feasible provision and stated accepted losses.
- Successors and other contributors have accepted their work and its limits.
- Material, access and shared resources move only through applicable permissions and arrangements.
- Receiving use has occurred where an achieved transfer is claimed, and unresolved gaps change the promise.
- Current obligations and the old arrangement's remaining resources have an effective disposition.
- People can discover what remains available, and later observation is bounded to a live question.

A successful first use establishes that transfer at its observed scope. Continued cultural availability or changed population practice requires further evidence.

### CB.11:8 - Common Anti-Patterns and How to Avoid Them

| Failure | Better move |
| --- | --- |
| Keep the founder's preferred format because it once worked | Recover current uses and compare feasible arrangements with affected people. |
| Claim that relationships will survive loss of the platform | Obtain willing contact and activity arrangements and observe their use. |
| Name a successor without providing time, access or capability | Establish and try the contributions their receiving work needs. |
| Copy every discussion and contact into a new space | Recover permitted uses and transfer only what the receiving arrangement may use. |
| Treat separation as failure while keeping an unresolved common resource | Allow different purposes and provide terms for the dependency that remains shared. |
| Preserve every old document as current guidance | Retain useful explanations with their conditions and distinguish an archive from maintained support. |
| Announce closure while leaving promised responses ownerless | Settle or transfer the actual obligations and give their receivers a reachable answer. |
| Maintain a pause with no next decision | Name the resumption condition or review date and decide again when it arrives. |

### CB.11:9 - Consequences

Useful work can continue through a smaller arrangement, another host, independent access or different groups. People can leave organising roles without pretending that their former work will perform itself, and an ending can release resources while preserving worthwhile contributions.

A transition consumes time and can reduce participation or lose a valued form. Some permissions or capabilities cannot be transferred. Making these limits explicit allows the remaining activity to be credible and gives people a real choice among what is still possible.

### CB.11:10 - Rationale

A community arrangement combines contributions that can change independently. Following the useful work exposes which of those contributions must move, be replaced or end. Receiving use tests the new combination where its value is supposed to appear.

Cultural continuation and organisational continuation therefore require related but distinct judgements. A practice can remain obtainable without the old group, and a group can remain active while losing a needed way of working. Preserving the means of use and settling actual commitments makes either a continuation or an ending useful.

### CB.11:11 - SoTA-Echoing

The comparison concerns the work needed to carry a useful contribution through changed conditions, including the option to end it. A simple rotation, a supported transfer and an institutional change require different effort.

| Source or approach | Adopt, adapt or reject by value | Trade-off and limit |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Spinks, *The Business of Belonging* (2021), physical PDF pp.68–76 | Adapt the recognition that growing diversity can justify local groups, distributed responsibility and changed support. | Treat the tree/lifecycle account as a historical organising heuristic. Reject mandatory growth, a fixed progression or the assumption that every group should remain under one umbrella. |
| Wenger-Trayner et al., *Communities of Practice within and across organizations*, second edition (2023), printed pp.118–119 and 125–128 | Adapt attention to relationships and the wider context, and the choice of development that members actually need. | Stronger identity or external alignment can help particular uses while creating burdens or exclusion. Neither is an obligatory maturity destination. |
| C.36 and C.36.RP | Adopt the distinction between the availability of a result and the means to obtain, interpret and adapt a practice; use a receiving task to expose a missing contribution. | This can require explanation, learning or a different provider. A copied archive is cheaper but may leave the needed work unavailable. Later cultural persistence remains a separate evidence question. |
| ECO.7/9, OCE.14 and CB.7/9/13 | Use actual resource rules, organisational revision, willing succession, provision and mandate where the transition depends on them. | A supported handover can be small; a change of shared economic rules needs its wider dependencies addressed. |

Guidebook source: [second-edition text](https://www.wenger-trayner.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/03/24-02-21-CoP-guidebook-second-edition-final-2.pdf), published at the 2024 URL, under [CC BY-NC 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc/4.0/). The treatment here analytically adapts its distinctions; the cases are constructed and do not imply endorsement by the source authors.

The useful answer is a choice tied to present work and viable transition, supported by observation of what receivers can obtain. Historical programme stories can suggest alternatives; the current permissions, provider conditions and willing contributions determine whether a particular continuation is available.

### CB.11:12 - Relations

- **CB.1** reopens a changed purpose; **CB.10** supplies a warranted account of benefit, burden and missing evidence.
- **CB.7/9** provide willing successors, support and terms; **CB.8/13** supply response to participant matters and legitimate collective decisions.
- **CB.14** maintains a shared repertoire; **CB.15** can help establish a useful relation with a successor group; **CB.16** can carry a needed external request.
- **C.36.RP** develops the means of obtaining and using a shared method; **C.36** supplies the broader cultural-continuation question.
- **HCD and Core Method-development patterns** supply missing acquisition or method development when a handover exposes that need.
- **ECO.7/9** and **OCE.14** develop changes to shared resources, economic rules or organisational arrangements.

### CB.11:End

## CB.12 - Support a Useful Repeated Action of Participation

> **Type:** Community-building method pattern
> **Status:** Stable

### CB.12:1 - Problem frame

Use this pattern when a person wants to repeat a useful action of participation, but the intended action does not reliably happen in the situations where it would help. A practitioner forgets to return the result of a peer consultation. A volunteer wants to check a shared resource after using it but cannot fit the step into their working conditions. Reminders increase visits while the wanted contribution remains unfinished.

The wider subject of habit change includes many personal and professional behaviours. This pattern addresses the narrower construction of a voluntarily supported, useful repeated action in community participation. It connects a recognisable occasion, a feasible action, its useful return and the conditions that let the person continue or stop. The professional correctness of the action and the development of missing capability come from their direct suppliers.

**First useful result:** the person and organiser have a feasible arrangement for one wanted action and can examine what happens at subsequent relevant opportunities, including an unmet condition or a decision to discontinue the arrangement.

A habit involves a learned tendency to respond in a particular context with less need for a fresh deliberation to begin or perform the action. Repeated attendance alone does not establish that tendency. A dependable deliberate practice or a retained reminder can be an adequate result when automaticity is unnecessary.

**Ordinary non-use.** Keep an already workable routine. Use a simple accessible route for a rare, irregular need when the person can find it at the right time. Do not create repeated participation to compensate for an absent benefit, unavailable helper or unsuitable obligation. A different behavioural subject calls for its appropriate professional method.

### CB.12:2 - Problem

A successful first encounter does not necessarily make the useful action easy to repeat. The person may fail to notice the relevant situation, know what to do but lack material or time, begin but not finish, or discover that the expected result no longer matters.

An organiser who sees only a participation count can respond to all of these with more reminders or rewards. That can increase visible activity while leaving the actual obstacle unchanged.

How can the organiser and participant build and revise a useful repeated action, distinguish what prevents it, and retain the person's ability to choose another way or stop?

### CB.12:3 - Forces

| Force | Decision it changes |
| --- | --- |
| An easy return and a worthwhile result | Lowering effort helps only if the action still produces something the person or receiving work values. |
| A stable occasion and changing circumstances | A familiar context can support repetition; a changed schedule, tool or responsibility may need another arrangement. |
| Support and dependence | A reminder or helper can be appropriate continuing support; withdrawing it tests a different condition and may impair the result. |
| Visible repetition and uncertain mechanism | Counts show occurrences, while initiation, completion, usefulness and automaticity answer different questions. |
| Continued participation and voluntary choice | A person may value a practice now and reasonably reduce or end it later. |

### CB.12:4 - Solution

Choose one action worth repeating. Examine its actual opportunities, beginning, completion and return; repair the relevant conditions; try a proportionate arrangement with the participant; and use later episodes to retain, revise or end it.

**Working reminder:** choose the useful action; locate the failed connection; make a feasible occasion and response; try it; inspect useful repetitions and burden; change or stop what no longer serves the person.

#### CB.12:4.1 - Agree what is worth repeating

Begin with the participant's receiving need and a concrete action. “Visit the community more” gives no reason to prefer one visit to another. “When a peer's answer has been tried, return the result so that the advice can be corrected” names useful work and a relevant occasion.

Establish what would make one episode complete. The result may be an applicable answer found, a permitted question sent, a contribution returned to its recipient or a bounded period of valued shared activity. It need not be a public post.

Ask whether repetition is appropriate. A daily work handover and an occasional unusual case have different opportunities. The frequency of a platform notification is not the frequency of the person's need. If an address or bookmark is enough for a rare event, preserve that simpler arrangement.

Agree what support the person wants, the burden they can accept and when the arrangement should be reconsidered. Distinguish their voluntary participation from a duty already accepted through employment, membership or another agreement. Explain such a duty through its actual basis; adding a community reminder does not create or remove it.

For a shared activity, obtain the other participants' contributions too. One person's intention to seek help does not guarantee that a qualified respondent will be available. CB.5 supplies the contribution and return arrangement.

#### CB.12:4.2 - Reconstruct what happens at an actual opportunity

Use a recent episode, or a short observation of the next relevant one. Recover the situation in which the action would have helped, what the person noticed, what they began, what they could complete and what they received.

Keep four questions distinct:

| Question | An observation that can change the next move |
| --- | --- |
| Did a relevant opportunity occur and become noticeable? | The intended situation never occurred, was not recognised, or a notification arrived at an unusable time. |
| Could the person begin the wanted action? | The entry was hard to find, the request was unclear, or required material and permission were absent. |
| Could they carry the action through? | They started but lost time, access, assistance or an applicable method before completion. |
| Did the episode supply its intended value? | The answer arrived too late, the activity failed its purpose, or the person no longer wanted that result. |

Ask for the participant's account rather than inferring a motive from silence. A person who reads an applicable answer without posting may have completed the wanted action. A daily visitor may still be unable to obtain help.

Where an explanation remains uncertain, choose a small change or observation that can distinguish the live alternatives. For example, making the return link available at case closure can test a findability problem; sending more reminders while the response team is absent tests no useful repair. Preserve the other relevant conditions so that the observation remains interpretable, and keep its conclusion local.

If the expected benefit is unclear, ask what the person hoped the episode would settle and what remained afterwards. Compare plausible results rather than selecting a reward in advance. A maintained explanation may settle one question; another needs a peer to inspect unfamiliar conditions; a desired social encounter can have a different useful result. Agree a small trial of the relevant alternative through CB.1/5, then use the person's account and the receiving result to decide whether the original action is still worth repeating.

Compare episodes that led to useful action with ones that did not. Look for the event that made the need apparent and the conditions in which the person could respond. Distinguish a potentially useful cue from a merely coincident feature: a notification may have arrived every day, while the actual occasion was completion of a case. Choose the next observation to separate the plausible explanations; treat an unresolved pattern as uncertainty rather than a personal defect.

HCD.3 supplies a fuller comparison when a possible human capability limitation is the receiving question. It compares that explanation with method, access, support, organisation and measurement problems. Use its result rather than diagnosing a lack of motivation whenever participation falls.

#### CB.12:4.3 - Build an occasion, action and return that fit together

Choose an occasion the participant can recognise in their actual work. It may be an existing event, a relevant state of the case or a selected point in the day. Explain the action in terms that remain intelligible there. A person finishing a diagnostic comparison needs the route for returning that comparison, not an unrelated invitation to browse.

Make the beginning easy without deleting the work needed for a useful result. Put the relevant reference, permitted question route or return location where it is needed. Pre-fill stable information when that is appropriate, while leaving the person to inspect and supply the case-specific content. A one-click response is inadequate if the receiving use needs a distinction it omits.

Prepare completion conditions as well as the starting cue. The person may need a quiet interval, available material, usable access or a respondent within a certain window. Walk through one episode under those conditions. If the expected response cannot be supplied, change the promise or obtain provision before inviting repetition.

Specify a realistic fallback. When the planned time disappears, use another feasible occasion if that still serves the result. When the case contains protected information, use an authorised route. When the wanted result is no longer relevant, omit the action. A fallback should preserve the purpose rather than preserve a participation count.

A short personal plan can connect the recognised situation, chosen action, expected result and fallback. Put it where the participant will use it.

The useful return should be dependable enough for the promise being made. An answer, clarified case, valued exchange or maintained reference can supply that return. An added prize is not required when the activity already gives the person the result they want.

#### CB.12:4.4 - Choose support and useful accumulation deliberately

Use a reminder when remembering or noticing is the actual difficulty and the person wants that support. Agree its channel and timing, what action it should make intelligible, and how to change or end it. Combine or remove messages that compete for the same attention.

Begin with enough support for a feasible episode. Help can include a prepared example, a companion for an unfamiliar first attempt or a reliable route back to the relevant material. CB.3 supplies attainable entry and preparation; HCD supplies learning where the required action has not yet been learned.

Do not equate success without a reminder with success without every kind of support. An accessible instruction, an accommodation or an available professional can remain necessary. If independence from a particular prompt is worth testing, agree a safe, useful opportunity to reduce that prompt while preserving the other conditions. Keep the support if its continuing value justifies it.

Let repeated use leave something helpful when that serves a later need: a corrected explanation, a relevant saved example, an agreed connection or a useful personal setting. Make that accumulated result obtainable for its receiving use and preserve the relevant permissions. Its purpose is to improve the next episode; sunk effort alone is no reason to make leaving harder.

An organiser may also recognise a contribution through CB.6. Keep that response tied to the contribution and the person's preferences. A recognition programme is neither a necessary condition for repetition nor evidence that a habit has formed.

#### CB.12:4.5 - Observe the next episodes and distinguish the result

Choose an observation opportunity from the action's real occurrence and the decision being made. A daily task can produce several observations soon; an uncommon fault cannot. Do not manufacture irrelevant activity to fill an observation calendar.

Retain only information needed to decide what to change. Ask whether the relevant occasion occurred, whether the person noticed and acted, what support was present, whether the intended result was obtained, and what burden the episode imposed. A missed occasion, a justified decision not to act and an unfinished attempt should not become the same failure count.

Compare the episodes with the expected connection. If the person notices the occasion but cannot finish, repair completion conditions. If they complete the action but receive no useful result, repair the contribution or reconsider the action. If they do not notice an otherwise feasible occasion, change the cue or agreed support. If the person no longer wants the result, reconsider the arrangement with them.

A person's report that the action now comes to mind readily can inform a local adjustment. Ask separately how readily the occasion brings the action to mind and how much deliberate effort is still needed to carry it through. Compare these accounts across relevant episodes, keeping the actual support visible. When it matters, combine them with an agreed observation under reduced prompting. This can guide the next local trial; it is not a numerical habit-strength assessment. A stronger automaticity claim needs an appropriate measurement method and qualification for the intended use. Frequency alone cannot supply it.

Likewise, a favourable change after introducing the arrangement is useful local evidence but does not by itself establish a causal effect or transfer to every participant. Preserve the actual conditions when deciding what can be reused.

#### CB.12:4.6 - Retain, revise, transfer or stop

Retain the arrangement when it supplies a wanted result at acceptable burden. Fewer reminders, fewer visits or a simpler action can be an improvement if the receiving result is preserved.

Revise the part that failed. A new work schedule may need another occasion; a changed platform may need another route; a different question may need another respondent. Keep the parts that still work. If a change couples several conditions, avoid claiming that the observation isolates one cause.

After absence or a role change, recover the current need and conditions before restarting the old routine. Use CB.3 when the person is returning or changing their contribution. A former commitment can be reduced or replaced; the person need not recover an old activity level merely to count as returned.

Where continued professional development is the purpose, HCD.15 supplies the fuller arrangement for later-work triggers, contribution ownership, evidence and reassessment. Its professional criteria govern that result. A repeated community action can support the arrangement without establishing capability by itself.

Stop the support when the person declines it, the result no longer matters, the burden is unacceptable or the necessary contribution cannot be provided. Turn off the agreed reminders, clarify any remaining accepted commitment and preserve access to useful material where its terms allow. If another person has relied on a continuing contribution, arrange a handover or revise that promise through its owner.

On closing the arrangement, explain what remains available, what has ended and how the person can return if a new need arises.

### CB.12:5 - Archetypal Grounding

#### CB.12:5.1 - Returning the result of peer advice

In a constructed repairer association, participants want to return what happened after using peer advice. The returned cases help respondents correct the shared explanation. Useful case outcomes often remain unreported after the immediate repair work ends.

The organiser and one willing participant examine recent cases. Some advice has not yet been tried, so those cases have no result to return. In completed cases, the person remembers the discussion but must search for its location. The repair is to keep a link to the permitted discussion with the case material and place the return at the point where the case outcome is recorded.

One episode can be complete with a short account of what was tried, the resulting condition and any unresolved limit. Confidential details stay in the authorised route. The organiser confirms that a respondent will inspect the return and revise the explanation if necessary.

At later relevant closures, the participant can use the link without a separate platform reminder. This supports retaining the simpler arrangement. It shows useful returns under those conditions; it does not establish a general habit-strength value or a community-wide causal effect.

If the participant begins the return but lacks a confirmed outcome, the remedy is to wait for or obtain that information. Another notification cannot supply it.

#### CB.12:5.2 - A rare need requires access, not daily participation

An independent repairer occasionally encounters an unfamiliar configuration. They want to obtain a bounded review when the available instruction does not settle the case. The organiser initially proposes a daily digest to keep the community prominent.

The repairer instead needs an easy-to-find request route, a permitted way to prepare the case and a realistic response window. CB.5 helps obtain the relevant respondent. The route is saved with the working reference.

The repairer does not visit on days when no such question occurs. When a suitable case does arise, they use the route. A later case needing an urgent response uses the accountable service instead. The arrangement has improved access to help without adopting a daily habit as its objective.

If no one can answer the relevant class of question, the organiser changes the promised scope. Increased reminders would make the unsupported promise more intrusive.

#### CB.12:5.3 - A changed schedule interrupts completion

A volunteer has been checking a shared reference for one obsolete link after a regular weekly session. A change of schedule removes the quiet interval in which that work was feasible. A reminder still arrives, and the volunteer often opens the reference, but the correction remains unfinished.

They distinguish remembering from completion. The volunteer chooses a different available interval and keeps the bounded task. A maintainer remains available for corrections that the volunteer cannot judge or publish. The next episode supplies a checked correction without increasing the number of reminders.

If the task itself has changed and the person cannot assess the material, the community obtains the needed subject judgement or learning contribution. Repeating the old action more frequently would not restore its adequacy.

#### CB.12:5.4 - End a reminder while preserving the useful result

A member uses a general daily notification to find occasional answers. It increasingly interrupts their work and draws them into unrelated conversations. They prefer to consult the maintained reference when their own question arises.

The organiser helps remove the notification and makes the reference route easy to recover. The member retains permitted access and can submit a question if the reference is insufficient. Their visible activity decreases while the wanted use remains available.

In another case, the person has also agreed to inspect a shared resource on a recurring basis. They still want to perform that task and choose a different workable reminder. If they also decide to stop the contribution, they agree a revised promise or handover with its recipient.

#### CB.12:5.5 - Find out what makes a recurring exchange worth using

In a constructed group of workshop volunteers, a member says that the weekly exchange helps them "keep up", yet often skips it. The organiser has two plausible explanations: the member wants a concise update on changed practices, or wants a second person's judgement before using a practice in their own work. These suggest different recurring actions: reading a digest or bringing an unsettled case.

They compare recent episodes. The member read an update at home and missed the meeting after finding what they needed. On another occasion, they attended after receiving the same update, asked about an unfamiliar material and stayed until someone helped them decide the next step. A third visit was enjoyable but left the working question unresolved. The member's account favours getting judgement on an actual case; the episodes also leave room for valued social contact.

For the next suitable weekly exchange, the member agrees to try a short inspection of their pending case with a competent respondent. The usual invitation, time and place remain; the first part of the meeting is reserved for that case instead of the general round of updates. They judge the trial by whether the member leaves with a usable next step and its limits. If the reference alone settles the question beforehand, they can report that and release the slot.

The trial produces a bounded next step that the member can use. They choose to retain the digest for updates and, at their regular preparation of workshop work, check whether an unsettled case merits a request for the next exchange. The organiser keeps a respondent available for that promised use. They do not make attendance every week the target.

This supplies a practical reason to separate the two actions and occasions. It does not establish that case inspection caused attendance: the participant expected special attention, and personal contact may still matter. A later episode in which a case remains unresolved, no respondent is available or the member wants company rather than advice changes the next arrangement.

### CB.12:6 - Bias-Annotation

An organiser sees visits and messages more easily than usefulness, unwanted interruption or effort outside the platform. The method starts from the person's action and its receiving result. A successful return by a keen participant also says little about people with different opportunities, access or support; keep those conditions visible when considering another application.

### CB.12:7 - Conformance Checklist

A useful repeated-action arrangement establishes:

- an action the person wants or has accepted, with an intelligible result;
- real opportunities for that action;
- the current difficulty in noticing, beginning, completing or obtaining value;
- feasible conditions, wanted support and a useful fallback;
- observations that can change the arrangement without equating frequency with habit;
- an understandable way to retain, revise, reduce or stop the support and any affected commitment.

A feasible first episode is enough to try the arrangement. A claim about automaticity, durable capability, causal benefit or continued performance under different support requires evidence suited to that claim.

### CB.12:8 - Common Anti-Patterns and How to Avoid Them

| Wrong turn | Practical repair |
| --- | --- |
| Choose daily participation before identifying the recurring need | Establish the real occasion and consider a simple accessible route. |
| Treat every missed action as weak motivation | Distinguish absent opportunity, unnoticed occasion, unavailable conditions and an unwanted result. |
| Make starting easy but leave completion unsupported | Obtain the necessary material, time, method and receiving response. |
| Increase reminders when nobody can supply the promised help | Repair provision or change the promise. |
| Report visits or a streak as a formed habit | Preserve the occurrence claim and obtain an appropriate basis for automaticity if it matters. |
| Withdraw a useful support merely to prove independence | Test only the condition the receiving use needs, with agreement and an adequate fallback. |
| Keep a person returning because they have already invested effort | Preserve useful access and make a new choice from current value and burden. |
| Treat stopping as failure despite a better alternative | End the support and settle any remaining accepted contribution. |

### CB.12:9 - Consequences

The organiser and participant can locate a failed connection and change it without escalating all reminders, rewards or activity targets. Useful repetition can coexist with quiet use, continued support and a reduced frequency of visits.

Observation and support still require effort. Rare opportunities may leave the mechanism uncertain for a long time. An adequate deliberate practice can therefore be a better objective than proving automaticity, and a simpler access arrangement can be a better result than a habit programme.

### CB.12:10 - Rationale

Repeating an action requires more than recalling its beginning. Material, time, access, other people's contributions and the returned value can differ from one occasion to the next. Separating these conditions gives the organiser a useful repair even when the psychological mechanism is not established.

The person also retains a reason to choose whether the action should continue. An arrangement that is easy to repeat can outlast the need it once served. Inspecting value and burden alongside repetition prevents continuation from becoming its own objective.

### CB.12:11 - SoTA-Echoing

| Source line or developed treatment | Contribution used | Adaptation and limit |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Spinks, The Business of Belonging (2021), physical PDF 119–124 | Connect a participation occasion, action and experienced return; inspect why someone would come back. | The historical participation-loop account is a starting heuristic. A topic-focused community does not guarantee a beneficial habit, and an external reward is not required for every useful return. |
| Duhigg, The Power of Habit, reader's appendix, printed 275–286 | A practical historical approach to identifying an existing routine, examining its expected result and occasion, then trying a changed plan. | Use inquiry and revisable trials; the account supplies neither a universal change formula nor a fixed repetition deadline. |
| Eyal, Hooked for the Enterprise, Trigger–Investment | Connect accessible action with value accumulated through use. | Retain useful material or connections for the person. Commercial retention, induced pressure and compulsory variable rewards are not the criteria for this voluntary practice. |
| Stojanovic, Grund and Fries (2022), context-stability studies | Distinguish repetition, automaticity, starting context and conditions during performance. | Self-reported intentional habits in the studied populations inform a bounded design question. They do not establish the effect of a community intervention or make attendance an automaticity measure. |
| HCD.3:4.1–4.3 and HCD.15:4.1–4.7 | Compare human and non-training explanations; connect continuing development with meaningful triggers, support and evidence. | Apply these suppliers when capability or professional development is the receiving question. They do not replace the particular participation arrangement. |

Compared with a general reminder or reward programme, the method asks for more explanation of the wanted action and the failed condition. It can then remove unnecessary prompts or stop an unsupported activity. An existing routine or an accessible one-time route is preferable when it already serves the person adequately.

Source returns: [Spinks](https://www.wiley-vch.de/en/areas-interest/finance-economics-law/the-business-of-belonging-978-1-119-76612-4); [Duhigg's reader's appendix](https://charlesduhigg.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/01/Duhigg-Readers-Guide-to-Changing-Habits.pdf); [Eyal's enterprise account](https://www.nirandfar.com/hooked-for-the-enterprise/); [Stojanovic, Grund and Fries](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/psychology/articles/10.3389/fpsyg.2022.883795/full). Reopen the arrangement when its action, context, useful return, support or accepted burden changes.

### CB.12:12 - Relations

- **Uses CB.1/5:** a worthwhile result, an available contribution and its returned use.
- **Uses CB.3/4:** attainable participation and a setting in which the action can be completed.
- **Combines with CB.6:** a meaningful acknowledgement where useful, without making recognition the whole repeated-action construction.
- **Uses [HCD.3 (HCD.3:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Human%20Capability%20Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/HUMAN-CAPABILITY-DEVELOPMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#hcd34---solution):** diagnosis when a possible human limitation must be compared with conditions and non-training explanations.
- **Uses [HCD.15 (HCD.15:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Human%20Capability%20Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/HUMAN-CAPABILITY-DEVELOPMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#hcd154---solution):** a continuing professional-development arrangement when later capability is the result.
- **Returns to CB.1:** a different contribution or simpler route now serves the person better.

### CB.12:End

## CB.13 - Establish a Workable Mandate for Shared Decisions

> **Type:** Community-building method pattern
> **Status:** Stable

### CB.13:1 - Problem frame

Use this pattern when people need decisions about a common activity but have not established who may make them, or when the mandate of an existing decision-maker is disputed or has expired. Several local groups want a joint event. A founder asks for comments and participants believe they are voting. A moderator's decision is challenged, but every appeal returns to that same moderator.

The governed object is the arrangement that makes specified shared decisions possible: their subjects, participants, source of authority, decision method, limits and means of reconsideration. This is one branch of community governance. Performing an already assigned organising task belongs to CB.7; applying an established participation rule to an incident belongs to CB.8.

**First useful result:** the people who can establish the arrangement have made a usable mandate effective for the named questions, with its limits and challenge route understood. If they cannot do so, the result is the exact unresolved authority or agreement question and the work that may still proceed under existing conditions.

Start with an actual shared decision and the people or organisations whose contributions and rights it would change.

**Ordinary non-use:** independent carriers of a practice can continue using it without a common governing body. A single activity owner may already have adequate authority and consultation arrangements. Keep that basis; use this pattern only for the missing or contested shared decision arrangement. A legally constituted body or regulated decision requires its applicable institutional and professional basis.

### CB.13:2 - Problem

Discussion, influence, ownership and a right to decide are different. A popular contributor can understand participants' needs without representing them. A sponsor can control the use of its room without being entitled to decide every question of the group. An elected representative can have a narrower mandate than their title suggests.

A proposed governance form can also omit the work that makes it effective. Representatives may have no way to obtain views or return reasons. A vote may have no defined electorate or result rule. Local groups may be called autonomous while every useful exception requires central permission. A challenge to the decision-makers may have no recipient.

The problem is to construct enough shared authority and procedure for the actual work, make it effective through the people entitled to do so, and preserve a workable way to correct or end it.

### CB.13:3 - Forces

| Force | Working consequence |
| --- | --- |
| Common work and local work overlap. | Allocate the shared question without absorbing choices that can remain local. |
| Participation and decision effort cost time. | Choose consultation, representation or direct participation to fit the consequence and available effort. |
| Formal authority and practical control can diverge. | Recover both the valid mandate and the means to carry a decision out. |
| Affected people may be quiet or absent. | Give them a usable way to contribute before treating the visible discussion as sufficient. |
| Stable decisions need correction. | Keep ordinary work possible while providing bounded amendment, recall and challenge. |
| A new arrangement must begin somewhere. | Its adoption needs an actual source of agreement or authority; the proposer cannot confer it by description. |

### CB.13:4 - Solution

Construct the arrangement around a few real questions, compare forms that could settle them, test their ordinary and disputed use, then obtain the acts and conditions that make the selected mandate effective. Keep the constitution of authority separate from each later decision made under it.

#### CB.13:4.1 - Separate the shared questions and affected parties

Name the intended common result and decisions needed to obtain it. For a joint event, these may concern its programme, common spending and use of a shared name. A local club's recurring schedule can remain outside that common subject.

Identify whose work, resources, access or accepted commitments each decision would change. Include people who supply upkeep or bear an effect without attending the central meeting. Ask what their participation can change: providing facts, proposing an option, consenting to use of their resource or making the shared choice.

Distinguish a community preference from a separate permission or professional judgement. A group can prefer a venue while its owner determines whether it is available. Members can choose a service aim while a qualified provider determines whether a particular promised performance is supportable. Obtain each result through its actual holder.

Make the smallest useful boundary explicit. State which questions the proposed arrangement can decide, which remain local and which must return elsewhere. If there is no common decision after that separation, use direct agreements or the existing activity owner rather than creating a general governing body.

#### CB.13:4.2 - Recover the authority that already exists

Obtain the current basis for relevant decision rights: an applicable agreement, an appointment under an established rule, members' adopted constitution, a resource owner's permission or an institutional decision. Check the subject, scope, holder and effective term against the proposed use. A former office holder may still have knowledge while lacking a current decision right.

Compare the stated arrangement with what people actually do and can obtain. OCE.2 supplies that recovery where the formal account is inadequate. Separate missing authority from missing access, an unavailable executor or disagreement with an otherwise valid decision.

Where no common mandate exists, identify who can enter a limited agreement about their own participation and contributions. Invite them to construct that agreement, stating who is included and what it will govern. A founding group can organise the activity of those who join on that basis; it cannot bind a wider population merely by claiming to speak for it.

Retain separate external conditions. If the proposed arrangement uses an organisation's assets, employees, name or statutory role, obtain the corresponding permission or professional result. The community's preferred rule cannot create that external authority. Return the specific unresolved permission or professional question to its holder.

#### CB.13:4.3 - Compare forms against the actual decision work

Compare workable alternatives, including an adequate existing arrangement. Choose the form for the subject, consequences, local knowledge, number of participants and effort people can sustain.

| Form | Construction needed | Useful boundary |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Consultation followed by an authorised organiser's decision. | State what input is wanted, when it can affect the choice, who decides and how the result will be explained. | Suitable where that organiser already has the decision right. Participants are giving input, not casting a binding ballot. |
| Direct decision by the eligible participants. | Define eligibility, access to the proposal, discussion and the rule for reaching a result. | Useful when the relevant people can take part and bear the decision work; a busy meeting alone does not define the electorate. |
| Representatives with bounded mandates. | Establish who selects each representative, what they may decide, how they obtain views and return reasons, and how they can be replaced. | Useful where direct participation is burdensome or distinct groups need a continuing voice. |
| Local autonomy with a limited common body. | Assign local subjects, common subjects and the route for questions affecting more than one group. | Useful when local groups can act independently except for named shared commitments or constraints. |

These forms can be combined. For example, local groups may select representatives for a joint event, while the event organiser consults participants about the programme within an already approved budget. Keep the basis of each decision understandable when moving between forms.

For each serious option, follow one ordinary question from proposal through information, discussion, decision, execution and return. Then follow a dispute, including a dispute involving the decision-maker. Identify the person or body performing each move, the time and support it needs, and what happens if it cannot act.

Compare the costs of the complete alternatives. Broad participation may improve the available account but require time and accessible preparation. Delegation can reduce repeated decision work while requiring explanation and a way to replace the delegate. Select these trade-offs from the local question; neither concentration nor distribution has a universal advantage.

#### CB.13:4.4 - Make representation and local autonomy usable

For a representative arrangement, define the represented constituency in terms participants can verify. State who may nominate, stand, select and challenge the selection, using the current governing basis. Make the opportunity accessible to eligible people who cannot attend the central meeting. Explain how vacancies are filled.

Specify the mandate's subject, limits and term. A representative may have discretion within a defined aim, be instructed on a particular proposal or need to return a materially different proposal to the constituency. Choose which applies before the consequential decision.

Give representatives a feasible way to obtain concerns and return reasons. Ask for relevant differences and minority positions. Where people decline to share a view or cannot be reached, preserve that limitation. Being affected and being represented through the established arrangement remain different facts.

Provide a means to replace or recall the representative under an intelligible rule. State who can initiate it, who decides, how the person can respond and what happens to current work during a vacancy. A recall procedure that no eligible person can reach adds little practical control.

For local autonomy, state the decisions retained by each group and the specific matters delegated to the common body. Provide a route for overlapping effects: a shared resource, a claim made in all groups' names or an activity that changes another group's commitments. Bring the affected groups into that question without requiring central approval of every unrelated local choice.

#### CB.13:4.5 - Define a decision and challenge method people can perform

For the named class of decisions, make the following working questions answerable:

- Who can propose a matter and receive the relevant information?
- Who must have a chance to contribute before the choice, and through what accessible route?
- Who decides, under which result rule and with what required participation?
- Who records and explains the result, and who makes it effective?
- Who receives a challenge or a proposed amendment, including one against the decision-makers?

Choose the result rule explicitly. An authorised person may decide after consultation; a group may use an agreed voting threshold; another group may seek agreement and have a defined way to handle a remaining objection. State what happens when required participation is absent, the result is tied or agreement is not reached. Delay, a further proposal, a limited interim action and referral are different outcomes.

Make the decision period realistic. Give affected participants the information and time needed for the consequential choice, and define a smaller route for genuinely urgent matters. Any interim authority needs a subject, limits and review point. Obtain that authority before relying on the shorter route.

Separate disagreement with the result from a challenge to its basis or conduct. A review may examine relevant new facts, failure to follow the adopted rule, a conflict of interest or a decision beyond the mandate. Identify a recipient capable of changing the affected decision. If the deciding body itself is implicated, provide the agreed alternate, appointing constituency or outside arrangement.

Plan what happens when no qualified or independent reviewer is available. Obtain one by agreement, limit or pause the dependent decision where the applicable arrangements permit it, or return the unresolved dispute to the body that can establish a valid route. Do not advertise independent appeal while relying on the same challenged decision-maker.

Use ECO.7 when the decisions concern a resource whose availability or condition is limited by use or maintenance. It supplies the combination of access, use, upkeep, observation and adaptation rules. Keep those resource facts separate from the source of the right to adopt the rules.

##### CB.13:4.5.1 - An optional decision method for a small team

An existing small team can consider the Core Protocols' [Decider](https://thecoreprotocols.org/protocols/decider) together with [Resolution](https://thecoreprotocols.org/protocols/resolution) for its own authorised action proposals. Its members must first adopt the [Core Commitments](https://thecoreprotocols.org/corecommitments) and the protocols' full obligations. These include staying for the decision, constrained discussion, no [Pass](https://thecoreprotocols.org/protocols/pass) during Decider, and responsibility for carrying out Decider commitments made in one's absence. Joining a community or volunteering for an activity does not by itself adopt those terms.

Use the complete primary instructions when performing the method. One action proposal receives simultaneous yes, no or support responses. An absolute refusal requires the proposer to withdraw the proposal. Resolution addresses a small remaining dissent when the effort is worthwhile. The proposer asks each dissenter for the precise change needed, adopts it or withdraws the proposal. A simple amendment requires checking that everyone is still in; accepting a complex amendment requires withdrawal and a new proposal containing the change. This supplies a concrete way to handle agreement on an action. The team's mandate, resource permissions and challenge route remain necessary.

Select this option only where those participation terms and limits on discussion fit the decision. If people need to abstain from individual votes, explain their reasons during the vote or avoid obligations for decisions made while absent, choose another agreed method under :4.5. Do not remove such source conditions while claiming to use unchanged Decider. People can decline this arrangement before adopting it without losing their wider community participation.

#### CB.13:4.6 - Adopt the arrangement and make it effective

Test the proposed arrangement before adoption with an ordinary case and a consequential dispute. Check whether the named participants can actually perform it with available time, information, access and support. Repair a missing contribution or revise the option.

Present the resulting proposal and its consequences to the people who can adopt it under the existing basis. Show the scope of the proposed authority, what remains with participants or local groups, the commitment required and how it can change or end. Obtain the required agreement or decision through the applicable procedure.

If this is the first limited voluntary agreement, make acceptance and its boundary explicit. Provide a way to decline. Record whose activity and contributions it governs and what happens to a person who leaves. Leave external obligations with their actual holders. A discussion of a proposal is not its adoption.

Make the mandates and conditions effective: complete the required appointment or acceptance, provide access, obtain the promised resource contribution and tell participants where decisions and challenges go. Use OCE.6 for assignments and enabling relations when needed. A person can be selected while an essential condition remains unavailable; retain that distinction and reduce the dependent promise.

Keep the usable rule and current appointments discoverable. A short agreement can suffice for a small joint activity. A larger association may need its established constitutional and decision records. Use the existing form that preserves the needed scope, term, result and amendment route.

#### CB.13:4.7 - Use, reassess and end the mandate deliberately

Follow the first actual decisions. Could participants offer relevant information? Did the authorised people decide within scope? Could the recipients carry the decision out? Did an affected local group or contributor discover a commitment they had never made?

Return the observed difficulty to its proper question. CB.7 repairs preparation or support for the organising work. CB.8 handles an incident under a valid rule. OCE.14 compares and implements a bounded revision of an established organisation when its current owner and decision rule are available. A defective or expired mandate returns here.

Review the arrangement when the activity, affected parties, resource conditions or scale of consequences changes. Use the agreed amendment method; do not treat an informal practice that drifted beyond the mandate as its automatic renewal.

At expiry, obtain renewal, replace the holder, reduce the subject or end the mandate through the applicable arrangement. Settle pending decisions and continuing commitments. Explain which ordinary work can continue under its own conditions and which must wait for a new mandate.

Close with an effective arrangement usable for its declared subjects, or with the specific missing authority question returned to the people who can resolve it. Evidence from its later use can justify keeping or revising it; adoption alone establishes no general claim about community trust or effectiveness.

### CB.13:5 - Archetypal Grounding

#### CB.13:5.1 - Three clubs authorise a joint conference

In a constructed case, three repair clubs want a one-day conference. Each club can commit its own contribution under its existing rules. They agree that local schedules, membership and regular fees remain with the individual clubs.

Six programme and purchasing decisions are expected over the next six weeks. A booking can be held for five days. Each club can run an accessible member ballot within four days, with a volunteer distributing proposals and returning results. Alternatively, each can appoint a representative who has agreed to two hours of weekly preparation and response. The clubs can authorise either form within the same spending limits.

Under direct club decisions, each club's eligible members would receive the programme options, costs and explanation, discuss them and return their choice through the club's stated ballot rule. Agreement by at least two clubs on the same option would authorise the event organiser to book and report it. Members would retain a binding choice on each question, at the cost of reading and deciding six successive proposals. Under representation, the three delegates would obtain their clubs' initial priorities, compare the proposals and return decisions and reasons. Members would surrender a ballot on each in-scope choice and need to rely on their representative's judgement and reports.

For either form, the clubs agree a reachable contact for challenges to scope or procedure. An experienced organiser from a fourth club accepts that bounded review task. Their agreement empowers this person to suspend or reverse an out-of-scope conference decision and return the new question to the clubs. A challenge to an endorsement hidden in a venue offer can therefore reach the same authorised reviewer whether member ballots or delegates approved it.

Both forms fit the booking window. The clubs choose representation because their members prefer to use the repeated decision time to prepare demonstrations, while the willing representatives can sustain the stated weekly work. They accept the loss of a separate member ballot on each programme choice. If representatives cannot obtain affected members' views or return intelligible reasons, the clubs will reconsider the form; direct club ballots remain feasible.

Each club selects its representative and supplies a spending limit. The three clubs adopt the limited arrangement: programme and common purchases require at least two of three votes, with all three given the proposal and a reasonable opportunity to respond. A proposal increasing a club's commitment beyond its agreed limit returns to that club. Each retains its own procedure for replacing its representative. The mandate ends after the conference's accounts and outstanding commitments are settled.

For an ordinary programme question, all three representatives receive two feasible options and their costs. Two prefer an afternoon of case sessions. The result falls within the limits; the organiser books the sessions and returns the programme and reason to the clubs.

A sponsor then offers a room on condition that the network endorse its repair product. Two representatives approve the offer as a venue choice. The third club challenges the added endorsement through the agreed contact. The reviewer obtains the event mandate and proposed announcement, hears the representatives' accounts and finds that the endorsement exceeds the agreed subject. The approval is reversed; the clubs receive the reason and the separate endorsement question. A feasible alternative venue remains available.

The sponsor can decide whether to provide its room, while the clubs decide whether to grant a new endorsement mandate. If the reviewer becomes unavailable or conflicted, the dependent joint decision waits for the clubs to obtain a replacement under their agreement. Local club meetings continue under their own arrangements.

#### CB.13:5.2 - Consultation does not turn into a ballot

The owner of a small discussion venue is authorised to choose its opening hours within a funded staffing arrangement. They invite regular users to explain which of two schedules supported by the available staffing they can use, stating the decision-maker, closing date for input and the need to preserve accepted access commitments.

Most replies favour the later schedule. An affected group's response shows that it would exclude them despite a separately accepted access commitment. The owner chooses the earlier schedule, explains the constraint and arranges a review when staffing changes. The consultation returned useful information and an intelligible decision; it did not promise that the most popular reply would bind the owner.

If the owner had announced a binding vote under a valid member decision rule, the same unilateral choice would need a different disposition. The declared and effective arrangement changes what counts as a proper decision.

#### CB.13:5.3 - Local autonomy has an interface for shared effects

Two local volunteer groups independently schedule their own case meetings. They share a limited pool of mentor time. Their common arrangement governs only allocation of that time and how unmet requests return; ECO.7 helps construct the feasible use and upkeep rules.

One group can change its meeting format locally. It cannot reserve all mentor time merely because it needs more sessions. That request goes through the common allocation and its actual capacity limits. Free copying of the groups' reference notes is not restricted by the same rule: the constrained contribution is mentor time.

If the common body begins directing unrelated local topics, either group can challenge that expansion through the adopted route. A new common subject requires the appropriate amendment, not merely repeated practice.

#### CB.13:5.4 - An expired mandate leaves a proposal, not an effective decision

An association's coordinator has prepared a useful revision to its moderation appeal route, but their term has expired. No applicable interim rule permits them to appoint the new appeal reviewer.

The proposal and its reasons remain available. The current authorised member body can consider it and establish the appointment. Existing incident protection continues only under the rules and holders whose authority remains valid. OCE.14 can help implement the later approved revision; it cannot renew the former coordinator's term.

#### CB.13:5.5 - Shared practice without shared government

Independent repairers use the same published technique and send occasional corrections to its maintainer. They have no joint budget, common service promise or need for decisions binding the users. The maintainer controls the publication under its actual arrangements; each repairer controls their own practice.

Creating a council of all users would add a representational claim and decision work that the present use does not require. They keep the explanation, correction and learning routes from CB.1/5. A later proposal for a joint service would create a new, bounded governance question.

#### CB.13:5.6 - A small team selects the protocol, a wider forum does not

Five volunteer organisers have adopted the Core Commitments and the full Decider and Resolution instructions for decisions within their session-planning mandate. They have authority over their own preparation and the allotted room time. Other community members have made no such commitment.

One organiser proposes using the next forty-five-minute session for two prepared cases. Four vote yes and one votes no. Asked for the needed change, the dissenter requests the material a day beforehand. The case author agrees to supply it. The proposer accepts this simple amendment, checks that all five remain in, and returns the decision and preparation responsibility. The material arrives as agreed; the session can use it. The protocol has helped this team settle one actionable proposal without creating a community-wide mandate.

On a later proposal, a requested amendment adds a separate paid workshop, changes the available time and needs funding that has not been obtained. The proposer withdraws it; the team must obtain the missing contributions before offering a new feasible proposal. The previous assent cannot authorise the expanded undertaking.

A subsequent forum includes volunteers who want to decide separately which activities they will help with. They do not accept compulsory voting or commitments made in their absence. The organisers use consultation followed by explicit agreement to each person's own contribution. They retain the small team's chosen procedure only within its actual membership and mandate.

### CB.13:6 - Bias-Annotation

Governance templates often begin from a company, association or online platform whose decision rights are already partly supplied. A voluntary common activity may lack that basis. Start with the participants' actual contributions, commitments and rights. Visible discussion can also hide people who lack time or access; provide a usable route for their relevant input without claiming that all affected people agree or are represented.

### CB.13:7 - Conformance Checklist

For the shared decision arrangement, ask:

- Are its common subjects, affected parties and retained local decisions intelligible?
- Does the authority to adopt it come from an actual agreement or applicable institution?
- Is consultation distinguishable from the procedure that produces a decision?
- Can representatives obtain views, act within a bounded term and return reasons, and can their constituents replace them?
- Can an ordinary question and a dispute pass through proposal, decision, execution and challenge with available people and resources?
- If a named external procedure is selected, have these participants accepted its actual obligations, including its treatment of abstention, absence and amendment?
- Has adoption occurred, and have the appointment, access and support conditions become effective?
- Is there a workable amendment, expiry and challenge route, including a challenge involving the decision-makers?

A small voluntary agreement can answer these questions briefly. A stronger institutional or legal claim needs its own applicable evidence and qualified result.

### CB.13:8 - Common Anti-Patterns and How to Avoid Them

| Misuse | Repair |
| --- | --- |
| Infer a community-wide mandate from founding the group, moderating it or being widely trusted. | Recover the actual source, subject and term of the decision right. |
| Ask for comments but present the result as a member vote. | State whether participants advise, consent or decide, and apply that actual arrangement. |
| Call a representative's personal preference the unanimous view of a group. | Obtain and return the relevant differences and limits of representation. |
| Give local groups autonomy while retaining every usable decision centrally. | Allocate specific subjects and provide the means to act on them. |
| Adopt a vote rule without eligibility, notice or a way to handle no result. | Complete the procedure around a concrete ordinary case and a dispute. |
| Extend a small team's decision protocol to everyone attending a community activity. | Obtain the actual adoption and mandate, or choose a procedure whose participation terms the affected people can accept. |
| Let the deciding body be the sole judge of every challenge to its own mandate. | Establish an alternate or return to the people who can constitute the mandate. |
| Treat an attractive revision as effective after a term has expired. | Preserve the proposal and obtain a current authorised disposition. |
| Require a governing council for all carriers of a practice. | Identify the actual common decision; retain independent use where no such decision is needed. |

### CB.13:9 - Consequences

Shared decisions can become both possible and bounded. Participants can understand who decides, supply relevant information, challenge an overreach and keep useful local freedom. Organisers and moderators receive an actual decision basis for their work.

Participation, representation and review consume effort. A limited agreement for one activity can be preferable to a general constitution. Where authority or a necessary contribution cannot be obtained, the method makes the unresolved question visible rather than inventing a collective power.

### CB.13:10 - Rationale

A shared decision needs more than discussion: it needs a rule that gives a result force for the specified participants and subject. That rule itself must be established through a valid basis. Starting from the actual common work exposes both the needed mandate and the questions that can remain elsewhere.

Testing ordinary and disputed use reveals omissions that an organisation diagram hides. Making adoption, appointment and provision distinct prevents a selected arrangement from being mistaken for an operating one. Bounded amendment and expiry then allow useful continuity without silently enlarging the original authority.

### CB.13:11 - SoTA-Echoing

The practice question is how to establish a decision arrangement for common work where its mandate cannot be assumed. The useful line compares consultation, direct decision, representation and local autonomy against actual subjects; supplies adoption and operating conditions; and tests a challenge as well as an ordinary choice. It accepts the effort of obtaining agreement and maintaining representation where shared commitments justify it.

| Source or developed treatment | Contribution adopted or adapted | Limit that changes use |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [Community Canvas Guidebook (2017)](https://github.com/communitycanvas/documents/blob/master/CommunityCanvas-Guidebook.pdf), pp.47–49. | Use its historical repertoire of decision bodies, sources of mandate, mixed local/common decisions and conflicts involving decision-makers to develop :4.1–4.6. | Founder status, election and employment require their actual basis. Claims of universally faster central decisions or more trusted decentralisation do not select the form. |
| Core Protocols: Decider, Resolution, Pass and the Core Commitments, linked in :4.5.1. | A qualified external option for a small adopting team; :5.6 contrasts it with a wider voluntary forum. | The procedure supplies neither a mandate over non-adopters nor a universal advantage. Its full source obligations remain selection conditions. |
| OCE.14:4.1–4.6 and its expired-chair case. | Recover a current owner, compare complete alternatives and make an authorised revision effective; :4.2/:4.6–4.7 preserves that boundary. | OCE.14 revises an established organisation. The missing initial or renewed mandate must be established here or through the relevant institution. |
| ECO.7:4–5. | Test resource-use and upkeep rules with a normal allocation and a changed condition; :4.5 and :5.3 keep that actual resource question. | Copyable knowledge, scarce mentor time and physical resources have different constraints; a resource rule alone creates no general governing right. |
