| [Leibmann et al., “Reddit Rules and Rulers” (2025, v2)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.14163v2), discussion and limitations. | Keep stated rules, actual governance and expressed reactions distinct when following :4.7's results. | The study does not select a constitutional form or show that adopting one produces trust. |

Decider with Resolution offers a specified proposal-and-amendment procedure for a small team that accepts its commitments. The more general construction in :4.5 remains preferable where participants need different deliberation or withdrawal conditions. Neither option gains authority from its procedure alone.

An existing adequate owner with consultation is a serious alternative to a new representative body. Independent practice without binding common decisions is another. A more elaborate form earns its cost only by supplying a needed shared decision and workable correction.

Reopen when a mandate expires, a common subject or affected constituency changes, an external permission fails, or actual use exposes an unworkable decision or challenge route.

### CB.13:12 - Relations

- **CB.1** identifies the useful common activity or independent-practice alternative. **CB.7** prepares organisers to work within the resulting mandate; **CB.8** handles rules and incidents under it.
- **CB.4/5** supplies discussion and subject contributions for a decision. Receiving those contributions and authorising the decision remain different acts. The Core Protocols option in :4.5.1 supplies a particular decision procedure only within an adopted arrangement.
- [OCE.2:4](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/ORGANIZATION-CHANGE-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#oce24---solution) recovers actual work and authority conditions. [OCE.6](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/ORGANIZATION-CHANGE-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#oce6---establish-holder-assignments-and-enabling-relations-for-organization-change) obtains assignments and enabling relations.
- [LDR.1:4.4](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/LEADERSHIP-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#ldr144---perform-the-smallest-sufficient-working-conversation) supplies a working conversation; [OCE.14:4](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/ORGANIZATION-CHANGE-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#oce144---solution) revises an existing organisation under its current decision basis.
- [ECO.7:4](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/ECONOMIC-REASONING-AND-COORDINATION-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#eco74---solution) supplies common-resource rule construction. Corporate or regulated questions return to their applicable governance and professional authorities.

### CB.13:End

# Part D - Develop practice and connect its contributions

## CB.14 - Develop and Maintain a Shared Repertoire of Practice

> **Type:** Community-building method pattern
> **Status:** Stable

### CB.14:1 - Problem frame

Use this pattern when practitioners repeatedly encounter a difficulty that their ordinary exchanges do not resolve, useful ways of working remain scattered among participants, or contrasting experience suggests a worthwhile improvement. Several repairers keep reconstructing the same diagnostic explanation. Volunteer organisers have different ways to welcome people with limited time. A profession needs a workable response to changed tools or conditions.

The governed object is the community's joint undertaking to obtain, qualify and maintain a useful repertoire: ways of working and the explanations, cases and support needed to use them. Community Management organises that undertaking and its return to practitioners. Core's Method-development patterns supply the construction and qualification of a reusable method; Research Method Practice supplies inquiry into an unresolved knowledge question when research is needed.

**First useful result:** a recurring problem has a specific next contribution, a willing and equipped provider, and an intended receiving use. That contribution may be a sufficient existing answer, a bounded comparison, a draft and trial, or a project whose returned result can be judged.

**What changes in practice:** people can obtain and improve reusable help instead of repeatedly discussing the same difficulty. Differences and failed attempts shape the result; later users can find its applicable conditions and request a correction.

Use ordinary help through CB.5 when a known answer resolves one person's case. Use HCD when the method is sufficiently known and the current need is for someone to learn it. A missing permission or unavailable resource may require its owner rather than development of a new method. Continue here when there is useful joint work on the repertoire itself.

### CB.14:2 - Problem

Shared experience is unevenly recoverable. A confident account may conceal the conditions that made it work. A useful local adaptation may be discarded because it differs from the usual practice, or copied into places where its support is absent. Repeated advice can leave the common difficulty intact.

A community can also agree to write a guide without providing anyone with time to write, resolve disagreements or try it. Conversely, a project team may complete a document that practitioners cannot use and report the delivery as successful practice development.

The task is to connect the practical question, people with relevant experience, qualified development work and the receiving practice. Agreement can settle what the community wants to support; the claims about a method still need evidence appropriate to their use.

### CB.14:3 - Forces

| Force | Tension |
| --- | --- |
| Immediate help | A current case needs an answer while a recurring difficulty may need longer development. |
| Common language | Shared guidance makes experience reusable; excessive uniformity can hide important local conditions. |
| Comparison | Differences reveal useful questions but can become a ranking that encourages concealment. |
| Contribution | Practitioners supply experience; sustained writing, testing and maintenance consume additional time. |
| Authority | A community can sponsor work within its mandate while access, employment and professional decisions have other owners. |
| Reuse | A concise usable resource needs enough provenance and limits to avoid unsupported transfer. |

### CB.14:4 - Solution

Begin with the difficulty in practice. Recover the available answer and contrasting cases, choose the joint work that can improve it, obtain the necessary development and support, then return a qualified result to an actual use. Continue maintaining it only while that contribution remains wanted and supportable.

#### CB.14:4.1 - Turn repeated difficulty into a bounded development question

Ask practitioners to show the situation in which their present approach is insufficient. Recover what they were trying to achieve, what they did, the result, the difficulty and the conditions that may matter. Include a successful contrast or a failed attempt when it can distinguish an explanation. Obtain permission for sharing the material at the level the joint work needs.

State the question as a change someone could use. “Help a receiving repairer recognise which case needs specialist investigation” is more useful than “improve knowledge sharing”. Identify who needs that result and what they would do differently. A request from an organiser still needs comparison with the practitioners' work.

First look for a sufficient existing answer. Ask the person with relevant experience, recover the applicable guidance and compare its conditions with the case. C.39.RP and A.3.1.DR help recover the repertoire and its source meanings when the needed material is scattered or unclear. If an existing answer suffices, return it through CB.5 and stop the development proposal.

Separate the remaining difficulty. The instruction may be incomplete; the method may fail under a changed condition; a person may need practice; or an organisational condition may prevent competent work. These can coexist, but their recipients differ. Return a learning need to HCD, an assignment or support problem to its owner through OCE, and a reusable method question to ME.

If an explanation or effect remains unknown, use RMP.1 with the exact question and current source answer. It first tests whether existing material is sufficient. Research follows only when the unresolved question, non-routine choice, inspectable criticism and communicable result conditions hold. An unfamiliar question or an intended data collection exercise alone does not establish that need.

#### CB.14:4.2 - Use differences to find a useful exchange

When participants want to learn from contrasting practice, agree which dimensions matter to their work. Select several that can reveal distinct strengths or limitations, such as recoverability of a case account, effort spent obtaining missing information and usefulness of the returned explanation. Explain what each observation means and use comparable cases where possible.

Invite participants to describe their own practice against those dimensions, with examples and relevant conditions. Keep unknown observations visible. A large gap is a reason to investigate what differs; it may arise from case mix, tools, access, experience or the way the observation was made.

Discuss a contrast with the practitioners who produced it. Ask how the apparent strength was achieved, which conditions it required, what it cost and where it failed. Look for reciprocal contributions: one group may have an effective intake conversation while another makes its later reasoning easier to recover.

Choose a specific exchange from that comparison. Name what one participant will show, what the other will try, and what result they will bring back. CB.15 supplies a connection with another practice when the needed experience is outside the current group. A learning programme for a particular person then uses HCD as needed.

Use a stable scale only when its meanings and progression fit a sufficiently stable practice. For a changing practice, describe the differences and test promising alternatives. Preserve dimensions separately unless a justified decision rule needs to combine them. The purpose here is to find useful work and partners; an overall league table would answer a different question and can make candid comparison harder.

#### CB.14:4.3 - Choose work that fits the size of the contribution

Select the smallest undertaking that can obtain the wanted result.

| Situation | Useful undertaking | Return that makes it worthwhile |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Participants can explain a sufficiently known way, but its usable account is missing. | Jointly develop an explanation, with an author or editor where helpful. | An instruction another intended user can follow, with conditions and remaining gaps. |
| Different approaches may help under different conditions. | Compare the cases and organise a bounded exchange or trial. | A qualified difference, supported choice or precise next question. |
| The contribution needs more sustained effort than ordinary participation can supply. | Establish a time-bounded practice-development project. | A usable product, evidence, limits and any maintenance or adoption work still needed. |
| The proposed explanation requires a new knowledge result. | Obtain the bounded RMP contribution. | An answer or retained uncertainty that changes the method decision. |

Writing can be shared, or one person can prepare drafts from practitioners' accounts. Choose from the available time and skills. A commissioned writer can make progress while practitioners retain responsibility for checking whether the account describes their work and answers their question.

For a project, establish its purpose, scope, participants, resource provision, intermediate returns and completion condition through the community's actual decision arrangement in CB.13. Include the people who will judge and use the result. A group may propose the work from below; the needed mandate and commitments still have to be obtained.

Obtain the time and access from their real owners. Use OCE.6 for assignments and enabling conditions, OCE.11 when this work overlaps with continuing commitments, and OPS.5 when a provider must decide whether to admit the work. An interested employee cannot commit an employer's time merely by volunteering in the community.

Agree useful intermediate contact. A draft, unresolved case or trial observation should reach practitioners early enough to alter development. Choose contact frequency from that purpose and the project's uncertainty. Regular reports of activity add little if the community cannot influence the result or obtain missing inputs.

#### CB.14:4.4 - Obtain a usable method contribution

Bring the cases, sources and disagreements to the people doing the method work. Preserve which claims are observations, participant interpretations, proposed explanations or established guidance. Recover the contribution and conditions of each serious option through A.3.1.QM before presenting it as suitable for a new use.

For a guide, work through the intended user's action: what makes the situation recognisable, what input is needed, what the person does with it, what result they inspect and when they continue or stop. A.3.2.DE supplies that construction and a trial of the description. Invite practitioners to identify a missing operation or a place where the author has relied on private knowledge.

Keep disagreements attached to the claim they change. One participant may object to an instruction because it needs access unavailable at their site. Another may dispute the proposed explanation despite agreeing that the local workaround is useful. Obtain the missing condition, retain separate variants, narrow a claim or request the relevant investigation. A majority preference cannot resolve an empirical uncertainty.

When no available method supplies the needed result, give a capable method developer the gap, relevant cases, required result and conditions. Obtain a proposal explaining the actions, inputs, dependencies, alternatives and stopping conditions. C.32.MWA.PP compares arrangements whose combined conditions matter; B.1.5.CM develops the proposed whole while preserving what is established and what remains a candidate. Practitioners supply the work that tests those choices, and A.3.2.DE makes the resulting explanation usable.

Obtain the qualification needed for the proposed use from A.3.1.QM and the relevant professional practice. A candidate can remain useful for further development while an unresolved condition prevents ordinary reliance. The community needs the developer's result and limits before deciding whether to adopt it.

If the proposed improvement requires a change outside practitioners' control, state the particular change and its owner. CB.16 can obtain and carry a collective request; OCE can develop a change to an organisation. The community's guide should state the resulting condition that users actually have, including a still-unmet one.

#### CB.14:4.5 - Try the contribution in the uses that matter

Choose a first use that can answer a live question about the contribution. It may test whether a reader can recover the instruction, whether the approach fits the case, or whether its benefit justifies the burden. These are different questions; one successful reading does not answer all three.

Use A.3.1.WT to arrange and observe actual trials when method evidence is needed. Select relevant conditions and, where it can change the decision, a contrasting situation that could defeat the claim. Obtain capable participants, permitted material, necessary support and the applicable stop before relying on the trial.

Observe the work as well as the verdict. Record what was tried, the result, effort, assistance, departures and unresolved difficulty. An expert's intervention may reveal missing instruction or a needed capability condition. A participant's approval may explain their preference while leaving effectiveness uncertain.

Return observations to the needed Core's Method-development family judgement: A.3.1.CC checks coherence, A.3.1.FT judges fit or transfer, and C.11.MV compares practical worth against current alternatives. For causal explanations, keep the RMP question and evidence requirements in view. The community can coordinate these returns without turning every exchange into a research project.

Revise the affected explanation or conditions and try the changed use when its earlier result no longer applies. Preserve valid observations from unchanged uses. If the contribution remains unusable, return that result and the exact missing work; do not require the receiving practitioners to adopt it to complete the project.

#### CB.14:4.6 - Return the result to the repertoire and to practice

Have the intended recipients inspect the result against the agreed question. Return the useful guidance or product, its supported situations, evidence limits, remaining work and the means of obtaining help or reporting a problem. A project closes with an intelligible receiving result, including a justified stop when the proposed improvement failed.

Choose how later users will find it. Put a recurring answer beside the question people actually ask, link the fuller explanation and preserve its sources. Demonstrate the first use when explanation alone is insufficient. Keep access possible for someone who did not attend the development meetings.

Agree who can correct the material and what maintenance they can sustain. A.3.1.MV supplies the distinction between a changed method, a changed description, a local departure and a support change. Preserve the relevant applicability and evidence instead of treating every new file edition as a newly validated method.

For adoption, obtain the receiving practice's decision and conditions. Learning a new way may require HCD; a changed assignment, tool or permission needs its owner. The returned repertoire offers a qualified option. Actual adoption and successful use remain observable later results.

The same resource can serve independent carriers who never join the community. Provide a usable explanation and conditions through an accessible publication or provider, and a feasible correction route where one can be maintained. Their use need not include a meeting, chat or public account of participation.

#### CB.14:4.7 - Maintain the contribution from later use

Invite reports that can change the advice: an unlike case, missing input, successful adaptation, failed result or excessive burden. Ask for enough context to decide which claim is affected. Credit useful corrections through CB.6 without rewarding only favourable outcomes.

Revisit the affected entry when evidence, tools, practice conditions or available support change. Preserve an older variant if another use still needs it. Retire or qualify misleading guidance and inform the people known to rely on the affected claim.

If no one can maintain a resource that requires continued support, reduce its promise, obtain a successor or withdraw that use through CB.11. A small resource with a clear limit may remain useful; an unsupported promise of ongoing advice will not.

### CB.14:5 - Archetypal Grounding

#### CB.14:5.1 - From repeated case repair to a usable shared guide

In a constructed network of repair workshops, technicians repeatedly ask which information should accompany a request for a second opinion. CB.5 resolves several cases, but the same missing context recurs. The members select a bounded question: help a competent recipient decide what to inspect next without repeatedly requesting basic information.

They recover existing instructions and compare permitted, anonymised examples. One workshop obtains clear accounts of the symptom; another supplies a concise record of what has already been tried. Faster replies at the second workshop also reflect its easier case mix. The group treats that difference as a question, not proof that its approach is superior.

None of the existing instructions explains how to request help when the original configuration is partly unknown. Two practitioners, a method developer and a writer accept a four-week project to supply that procedure and its usable explanation. Their employers provide the agreed time; the community supplies an editor and intended trial recipients. The completion condition is a procedure that lets a recipient identify a useful next inquiry or state the information that prevents advice.

With the practitioners, the developer compares two arrangements: have a specialist reconstruct every configuration before a request, or let a competent recipient first inspect the known account and decide which missing fact is needed. The first is feasible through a paid service but would consume specialist effort even when the unknown detail does not affect the question. They select the second for a bounded trial, accepting that some cases will need an additional exchange. Using B.1.5.CM and A.3.2.DE, the developer prepares the proposed procedure and explanation: state the question, distinguish known from unknown configuration, report prior actions, and obtain the recipient's next inquiry or stop.

The first reading exposes wording that could lead the sender to guess an absent configuration; the team corrects it before the trial. In the complete-information case, the recipient chooses a next inquiry directly. In the incomplete case, the recipient identifies the missing detail, the sender obtains it, and the recipient can then continue. A third case lacks retrievable information and receives an explicit stop. The developer and receiving practitioners check these results against the procedure's stated conditions and return its bounded fit, the additional-exchange burden and the retained limit.

The community accepts the developed procedure and guide for those communication uses, with the trial observations and limitation. It has not established a better diagnostic method or a general reduction in repair time. An editor maintains the guide; receiving workshops decide how to introduce it. A workshop outside the network can use the published instruction without joining its meetings.

#### CB.14:5.2 - Reciprocal comparison without a single winner

Volunteer garden groups compare welcoming a new helper and handing over an unfinished task. One group provides a useful first explanation but loses task context when the host changes. Another keeps that context well but expects newcomers to infer where help is needed.

They agree two dimensions with observable examples, inspect a recent arrival and handover in each group, and exchange the relevant practices. The first group tries a brief end-of-session handover; the second asks a newcomer to recover the first task from its invitation and welcome.

At the first garden, the departing host leaves a short account of the bed still to weed, the unlabelled seedlings to leave alone and the location of the tools. At the next session, a replacement continues the named bed without contacting the absent host and stops at the uncertain seedlings. Preparing and checking the account takes five minutes at the end of the session. The group keeps that effort for work that will pass to another host; the uncertain seedlings still need an experienced gardener's decision.

At the second garden, the newcomer initially cannot identify a first task from the general invitation. The host adds the meeting place, the person to meet and a starter task, then demonstrates potting one tray. The newcomer finds the work and completes the next tray with help available. Preparing the materials and giving a ten-minute welcome are now explicit host work. The group arranges a willing host for the next arrival and retains supervision for tasks that need plant judgement; the clearer invitation alone would not supply that support.

Each group returns the changed practice, observed use and burden to its counterpart. One keeps a handover for unfinished work; the other provides a prepared first task and host. They remain independent and can adapt or stop either arrangement. If the accounts concern different work, they first revise the comparison rather than treating the apparent gap as a competence deficit.

#### CB.14:5.3 - A method project cannot promise an unknown cause

A group observes repeated equipment failures and wants to publish a preventive rule. The available cases do not distinguish a maintenance effect from differences in equipment and use. A qualified existing source can answer one configuration's question, so that answer is returned immediately.

The wider causal question remains. RMP.1 is used to state who needs the answer, the competing explanation and the evidence that could change the rule. If a research contribution is justified and can be provided, the community arranges that work and receives its result. If the needed records cannot be obtained, the guide retains the uncertainty and omits the unsupported preventive claim. Useful case-sharing continues within that limit.

#### CB.14:5.4 - Known practice and absent provision

A volunteer already has a usable, appropriate instruction but cannot access the tool it requires. The organiser obtains the access decision from the owner or helps choose a feasible alternative. No new guide or practice-development team is needed. If the tool is available and the difficulty is learning to use it, the next contribution is HCD support.

### CB.14:6 - Bias-Annotation

Frequent speakers, well-resourced sites and successful cases are easier to collect than quiet corrections, failed attempts or work under scarce provision. Invite relevant contrasts and let contributors restrict sensitive material. Explain how comparisons will be used before requesting them; a learning exchange coupled to undisclosed personnel ranking will distort both trust and evidence.

### CB.14:7 - Conformance Checklist

For reliance on the joint development result, establish:

- The recurring difficulty and receiving use are specific enough to select a contribution.
- Existing answers have been considered; learning, support, method and research questions have appropriate recipients.
- Comparisons retain consequential conditions and separate dimensions; a difference is investigated before becoming a superiority claim.
- Sustained work has willing participants, effective time and access, intermediate returns and a completion condition.
- The method contribution has the description, qualification and evidence needed for its intended use.
- The receiving practitioners obtain a usable result with limits and an available correction or maintenance arrangement.
- Changed conditions can reopen the affected advice, and independent use does not silently require community membership.

These questions justify reliance on the result. A few recurring difficulties are sufficient to recognise the opportunity and begin the bounded inquiry.

### CB.14:8 - Common Anti-Patterns and How to Avoid Them

| Failure in practice | Repair |
| --- | --- |
| Repeat a helpful clinic while the shared instruction remains missing. | Name the recurring gap and obtain a reusable contribution with an intended recipient. |
| Publish the group's consensus as a proven method. | Preserve the proposed claim and obtain qualification or evidence for the use that depends on it. |
| Rank unlike sites by one convenient total. | Compare meaningful dimensions and conditions; use the contrast to select an exchange or inquiry. |
| Ask volunteers to produce a substantial resource in their spare time. | Obtain the actual commitment or reduce the work to what can be sustained. |
| Commission a polished document without practitioner involvement. | Have practitioners shape the question and inspect drafts against their work. |
| Count a scheduled trial or favourable opinion as successful application. | Recover the performed use, result, support, departures and evidential reach. |
| Keep an obsolete guide visible because no replacement is complete. | Qualify or withdraw the affected claim while preserving still-useful guidance. |

### CB.14:9 - Consequences

The community gains a way to turn distributed experience into resources people can use and improve. A temporary project can obtain sustained work without making all participation project work. Differences remain visible enough to support adaptation and reciprocal help.

The method consumes time for recovery, disagreement, testing and maintenance. That effort is justified by the recurring use it serves. A sufficient existing answer or a small support repair can be the better result.

### CB.14:10 - Rationale

Repeated practical trouble is a useful entry because it links development to a receiving task. Comparing contexts then helps distinguish a missing explanation, a transferable contribution and a condition that belongs to another owner. This avoids asking one document to solve every difficulty.

Community judgement and method evidence have different jobs. Participants can establish priorities and sponsor a contribution. Qualified development and observation establish what users may reasonably expect from the resulting way of working. Connecting those returns preserves both practitioner influence and warranted claims.

### CB.14:11 - SoTA-Echoing

The practice question is how a community can improve a reusable repertoire without losing its practical context or claiming more than its evidence supports. The selected line combines practitioner-led documentation and reciprocal comparison with explicit development, trial and maintenance contributions. Informal help remains the lower-burden alternative when it already resolves the use.

| Source or developed treatment | Choice and effect in this pattern | Limit and accepted cost |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Etienne Wenger-Trayner, Beverly Wenger-Trayner, Phil Reid and Claude Bruderlein, *Communities of practice within and across organizations*, second edition (2023), pp.112–114 and 157–162. | **Adapt** joint documentation, mutual benchmarking and practice-development projects in :4.2–4.6. Keep practitioners involved while allowing dedicated writing and a temporary team. | This practical repertoire does not establish causal effectiveness or a universal maturity ladder. Stable scales suit stable practices; qualification and contrasting uses add effort. |
| C.39.RP, A.3.1.DR, A.3.1.QM, C.32.MWA.PP, B.1.5.CM and A.3.2.DE, A.3.1.WT, A.3.1.CC, A.3.1.FT and C.11.MV and A.3.1.MV:4.1–4.3. | **Adopt** recovered source contributions, explicit method proposals, claims qualified for their uses, actionable descriptions, observed trials and bounded maintenance. These repair the gap between agreement to publish and a contribution fit for use. | Invoke the needed contribution; an ordinary advice exchange does not need a complete method-development programme. A candidate retains its unresolved status. |
| RMP.1:4 and OCE.11:4, with OPS.5:4. | **Adopt** a sufficient-source stop and the explicit provision of sustained work. **Reject** starting a study merely because the question is unfamiliar or treating volunteered interest as available capacity. | The community accepts a missing-input or uncertainty return when the required knowledge or provision cannot be obtained. |

The Guidebook contribution above is an analytical adaptation with attribution to its authors, under its [CC BY-NC 4.0 licence](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc/4.0/). The [source text](https://www.wenger-trayner.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/03/24-02-21-CoP-guidebook-second-edition-final-2.pdf) supplies the practical forms; this pattern adds the constructed cases and supplier connections.

Reopen this selection when the recurring use changes, new evidence defeats a method claim, comparisons cannot retain relevant conditions, or the work and maintenance burden exceed the value people obtain.

### CB.14:12 - Relations

- **CB.5** supplies immediate peer help and cases that may expose a recurring gap; **CB.7** prepares organisers who support the development work.
- **CB.13** supplies its collective mandate. **CB.15** connects relevant practices; **CB.16** carries a needed collective request to an external decision.
- [C.39.RP (FPF C.39.RP:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/FPF-Spec.md#c39rp4---solution) and [A.3.1.DR (FPF A.3.1.DR:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/FPF-Spec.md#a31dr4---solution) recover source contributions; [A.3.1.QM (FPF A.3.1.QM:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/FPF-Spec.md#a31qm4---solution) qualifies each contribution for its use.
- [C.32.MWA.PP (FPF C.32.MWA.PP:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/FPF-Spec.md#c32mwapp4---solution) compares arrangements; [B.1.5.CM (FPF B.1.5.CM:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/FPF-Spec.md#b15cm4---solution) develops the proposed method whole; [A.3.2.DE (FPF A.3.2.DE:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/FPF-Spec.md#a32de4---solution) makes its account usable.
- [A.3.1.WT (FPF A.3.1.WT:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/FPF-Spec.md#a31wt4---solution) supplies trials. [A.3.1.CC (FPF A.3.1.CC:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/FPF-Spec.md#a31cc4---solution), [A.3.1.FT (FPF A.3.1.FT:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/FPF-Spec.md#a31ft4---solution) and [C.11.MV (FPF C.11.MV:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/FPF-Spec.md#c11mv4---solution) supply coherence, fit or transfer, and practical-worth judgements respectively; [A.3.1.MV (FPF A.3.1.MV:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/FPF-Spec.md#a31mv4---solution) maintains the repertoire.
- [RMP.1 (RMP.1:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/RESEARCH-METHOD-PRACTICE-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#rmp14---solution) distinguishes a sufficient answer from a research contribution and returns the remaining question.
- [OCE.11 (OCE.11:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/ORGANIZATION-CHANGE-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#oce114---solution) supplies workable overlap with continuing commitments; [OPS.5 (OPS.5:4)](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/OPERATIONS-MANAGEMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#ops54---solution) supplies admission when an operating provider must accept the work.
- **HCD** supplies the particular learning contribution needed to use a known method; **CB.10/11** connects observed value with continuation, transfer or ending.

### CB.14:End

## CB.15 - Connect Practices through a Useful Joint Contribution

> **Type:** Community-building method pattern
> **Status:** Stable

### CB.15:1 - Problem frame

Use this pattern when a practical result depends on people from different communities understanding and using one another's contribution. A repair workshop and a reuse group repeatedly return incomplete referrals. Two professional groups use the same word for different decisions. Practitioners want to visit another site because its work may help with their own difficulty.

The governed object is a bounded connection between practices: the useful joint work, its participants and conditions, and the return each side receives. This is one branch of building relations across communities. It can begin with a single exchange and end there; a durable partnership is a further choice.

**First useful result:** the participants agree one useful question or task, each side's contribution, the permitted material and access, and how they will recognise a worthwhile return. A precise missing contribution or decision can also be the result.

**What changes in practice:** contact develops into work that each side can use. People can discover differences through a concrete case, correct a misunderstanding and choose a proportionate continuation.

A friendly introduction or shared event may already meet the purpose through CB.2/4. A sufficient published answer may need only source use through CB.5. Use this pattern when different practices must contribute to the result and the connection itself needs construction. A joint decision mandate belongs to CB.13; a collective external position belongs to CB.16.

### CB.15:2 - Problem

People can meet across a professional or community boundary without obtaining anything they can use. Presentations describe the other group but leave its work obscure. A common vocabulary conceals different purposes, standards or authority. One group may regard the other's caution as obstruction while the other sees an incomplete or inappropriate request.

An ambitious partnership can make this worse by demanding broad agreement before participants have worked together. The opposite failure is an attractive pilot whose useful result depends on unpaid effort, unavailable access or a person who cannot continue.

The task is to discover a concrete contribution worth obtaining together, make its conditions workable and learn from its return. Understanding each other's work matters where it changes that contribution.

### CB.15:3 - Forces

| Force | Tension |
| --- | --- |
| Different purposes | Each practice has its own users, obligations and reasons for acting. |
| Small commitment | A bounded first task makes entry possible but must still return something useful. |
| Translation | Shared words can help discussion while hiding differences in action or evidence. |
| Access | Seeing real work can reveal its conditions; material and participants may have legitimate access limits. |
| Reciprocity | Both sides need a reason to contribute, which need not be the same kind of benefit. |
| Continuation | A successful encounter can justify more work, but its support and mandate may not extend to that work. |

### CB.15:4 - Solution

Find a concrete problem whose answer needs both practices. Bring capable participants to a small useful action, make differences and permissions explicit at that action, then return the result to both sides before deciding whether to expand the relationship.

#### CB.15:4.1 - Locate the difficulty where the practices meet

Start with an actual request, referral, shared material, decision or outcome. Ask each side what it was trying to achieve, what it needed from the other, what it supplied and what happened. Recover a case where the connection worked as well as the difficulty when that contrast could change the explanation.

Let practitioners describe their own work and constraints. An organiser's account can identify a lead, but someone who performs or receives the contribution may expose a different problem. Use CB.1 and OCE.2 when the relevant people, affected interests or actual relations are still unknown.

State the common question in terms both sides can test against their work. They need not agree on every explanation. “Make a referral that the receiving workshop can accept or return with a specific reason” gives the groups a joint subject while allowing disagreement about their wider priorities.

Identify what would make the connection worth each side's effort. One may obtain a usable result, another a clearer incoming request, payment or experience relevant to its practice. Make an asymmetry visible. If the proposed provider has no reason to take part, ask whether a paid service or a smaller contribution would be worthwhile.

#### CB.15:4.2 - Choose a small action that needs both contributions

Look for an action that is feasible with limited preparation and useful even if the relationship goes no further. Good candidates expose the practical difference: work through one referral, compare two interpretations of the same case, inspect a relevant operation on a visit, or prepare one joint response.

| Needed result | Possible form | What must come back |
| --- | --- | --- |
| An answer to a bounded question. | Consultation or case discussion with qualified contributors. | An answer, applicability limit or precise missing inquiry for its receiver. |
| A concrete understanding of how another practice works. | A prepared visit, demonstration or learning expedition. | An account of the observed work and conditions, with what the visitor could try or still needs. |
| A working connection between recurring contributions. | A trial of one handover or jointly produced item. | The received result and an identified correction or condition for repeat use. |
| A larger new contribution. | A bounded joint project. | The agreed product and its use conditions; CB.14 governs a shared repertoire contribution. |
| A position to carry into an external decision. | Joint preparation with an explicit mandate through CB.16. | The authorised position and the decision-maker's response. |

Choose from the result rather than the attraction of a format. A visit is useful when situated observation can change understanding; a question that can be answered from an existing account may need no visit. A project is useful when the contribution needs sustained work; an informal encounter cannot silently promise its delivery.

Ask both sides to refine the first task. Keep it small enough for their actual commitments, but large enough to reveal whether the proposed connection works. If no task offers value under available conditions, end with that conclusion or a specific missing condition.

#### CB.15:4.3 - Obtain participants, access and the working agreement

Invite people who can supply the required experience or result. Distinguish the practitioner who explains the work, the facilitator who helps the exchange and the owner who can change an arrangement. LDR.1 supplies the working conversation; OCE.6 supplies a needed assignment or enabling relation.

Agree the question, preparations, time, location or channel, usable material and expected return. State who may see, retain or reuse the material and what needs permission. If a confidential case is essential and cannot be shared, choose a permitted alternative or stop that part of the exchange.

For a visit, obtain a local practitioner who can reveal the important work and answer questions about decisions. Arrange access to relevant activity, people and context. A room of general presentations can explain the organisation while leaving the intended practice unseen.

For an online visit, choose the work that can actually be shown through that medium. A guided screen demonstration and questions may reveal a sequence well; an aspect requiring physical observation remains outside the result. Distinguish live work, a reconstructed demonstration and an illustrative case when interpreting what was seen.

Keep the commitment and authority bounded. Participants may offer their own time but need an employer's permission for protected work time or data. A person who attends on behalf of a group needs the scope of that representation. If the first action needs no collective commitment, willing individuals can agree it within their own authority.

#### CB.15:4.4 - Clarify meanings through the shared work

Have each side explain the relevant action through a concrete case. Ask what starts it, what material it needs, what decision is made, what result leaves and who uses that result. Where the accounts conflict, locate the difference before proposing a shared term.

Compare consequential expressions in use. If one group calls a request “ready” when all fields are filled and another means a qualified practitioner has accepted it, preserve both meanings and say which condition the joint work needs. A short explanation or distinct wording may suffice.

When a recurring comparison or translation depends on an exact relation between local meanings, use F.9 to test the semantic correspondence or difference and its suitability for the proposed use. A mapping of terms does not itself provide permission to share material, authority to decide or a working referral arrangement.

Use the difference to change the joint action. Revise the question, add necessary context, obtain an unavailable contributor or keep two outputs for different recipients. Let each side recover the revised arrangement in its own words and apply it to the case.

Separate a misunderstanding from a substantive conflict. One group may understand a request precisely and still be unable to fulfil it because of cost, priorities or professional obligations. [LDR.2](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/LEADERSHIP-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#ldr2---agree-feasible-joint-contributions) helps make those conflicting contributions and choices explicit; the actual owner decides what can change.

#### CB.15:4.5 - Perform the action and inspect what each side receives

Brief the participants on the intended result and conditions, then do the selected work. Allow questions at the points where a decision becomes visible. On a visit, ask the practitioner why a particular move was selected, which alternatives were possible and what would change it.

Give each side a chance to correct the other's account. A participant may have understood the visible sequence while missing an input supplied elsewhere. Recover that dependency before proposing to use the approach in a new setting.

Inspect the receiving result. Can the next person act on the referral? Did the consultation answer the question? Can the visitor explain the observed practice and identify what their own situation lacks? Ask what remains uncertain and what additional work would resolve it.

Do not turn one observation into an unearned competence or effectiveness conclusion. A visitor may understand a practice without being able to perform it. HCD can supply the needed preparation, while A.3.1.FT can qualify a method's fit or transfer. Keep that further result separate from the successful visit.

When the action fails, return the failure to the relevant part of the arrangement. Repair an unclear request, obtain a missing decision or stop the unworkable promise. Use CB.8 if conduct prevents the exchange; a legitimate difference in professional judgement belongs to the subject question.

#### CB.15:4.6 - Return the contribution to both practices

Give the result to the people who need it on each side. Explain what was obtained, the conditions on which it depends and the next use or unresolved question. Check that the participants may share the intended account; protect material whose permission was limited to the encounter.

For a new shared instruction or practice contribution, use CB.14 to obtain qualification and maintenance. For a local adaptation, give the receiving owner the relevant observation and conditions. A helpful encounter may finish with one person changing a request and no new joint resource.

Where representatives took part, return the result to their groups and distinguish observations, proposals and agreed commitments. The account should allow those groups to correct a mistaken interpretation or decide whether to support further work.

Make the next contact usable when continued exchange is wanted: who can answer which question, by what route and within what realistic availability. Reduce the promise if it depends on a single overloaded person.

#### CB.15:4.7 - Choose a proportionate continuation

Compare the received value and burden with the reason for the exchange. The possible result is to stop after the useful episode, repeat a narrow action, revise the arrangement or establish a larger project. Make the choice with the participants and the owners of the additional commitments.

Use a joint project when sustained interdependent work is now worthwhile. Obtain its result, resource provision, assignments, intermediate returns and receiving decision. Use CB.13 for a common decision arrangement and OCE.11 when participants must protect continuing work while contributing.

Preserve each group's useful independence. A recurring referral, reciprocal visit or shared resource can connect practices without merging their membership or governance. If a common body is needed, name the decisions it must make and construct that mandate explicitly.

Reopen the connection when terms or practices change, a relied-on contact or resource disappears, one side bears an unaccepted burden, or the result no longer reaches its intended users.

### CB.15:5 - Archetypal Grounding

#### CB.15:5.1 - Repair a referral between a reuse group and a workshop

In a constructed local network, a volunteer reuse group sends requests to a professional repair workshop. The volunteers describe a request as ready once the intake fields are complete. The workshop reserves that word for a case a technician has accepted into its work. Repeated returns are interpreted as unwillingness to help.

One practitioner from each group and the intake organiser compare two permitted past referrals. Their common task is to make the next request easy to accept or return with a specific information gap. The volunteers gain a usable reply; the workshop expects fewer conversations to recover missing information. Each agrees time for this small trial.

They preserve two states: “information supplied” and “workshop accepted”. The request includes the owner's question and known context, with missing information stated. The workshop's intake owner retains the decision to accept work. Its existing service conditions and available capacity remain applicable.

For the next request, the technician can identify a missing model detail and returns that question through the agreed contact. The volunteer obtains the information, and the workshop then accepts the case. The two groups keep the distinction and contact route. They have improved that referral; they have not committed the workshop to accept every future request.

If demand grows beyond the contact's availability, they revisit provision before promising routine service.

#### CB.15:5.2 - A visit that reveals the work

A group of volunteer archivists wants to understand how another group recovers the history of an incompletely labelled photograph. A general talk could describe the collection, but the visitors need to see how practitioners combine clues and preserve uncertainty.

The hosts arrange a permitted case and a practitioner who can show the relevant sources. During an online visit, the practitioner reconstructs the investigation, identifies where an attribution remained uncertain and answers questions. The visitors distinguish the reconstructed account from live investigative work.

One visitor then explains how they would approach a different photograph and discovers that their own collection lacks the proposed catalogue link. The useful return is a clearer investigation question and that missing-source condition. A later local trial is needed before recommending the same approach there.

The visitors return a short account for the host to correct. The hosts identify a step that their demonstration left implicit and add it to the explanation they use for later visits.

#### CB.15:5.3 - From a small exchange to a supported project

Two walking groups use different descriptions of route accessibility. A first comparison reveals that one reports the surface while another describes whether its usual members completed the route. Neither account alone answers a newcomer with a different need.

They work through one route with the relevant participants and agree which observations and limits a joint account should expose. Producing and maintaining accounts for the whole area would require more work than the first encounter. They propose a bounded project through CB.14, obtain willing contributors and the groups' decisions on time and publication, and keep uncertainties about unobserved conditions visible.

The first route account remains useful even if the larger project is deferred.

#### CB.15:5.4 - An answer can finish the connection

An organiser asks a neighbouring club how to make its registration instructions clearer. The club supplies a permitted example and explains the relevant choice. The organiser adapts it and returns the useful result. The small connection has met its purpose and ends there.

### CB.15:6 - Bias-Annotation

Organisers may invite prestigious or familiar groups while missing the people who perform the relevant work. A host can also present only favourable conditions, and visitors can mistake a polished demonstration for ordinary practice. Ask for the specific work and meaningful contrasts, retain limits on access, and allow the group with less power to decline or renegotiate the proposed contribution.

### CB.15:7 - Conformance Checklist

For reliance on a joint contribution, establish:

- Each side can state the practical question and the value it expects from taking part.
- The first action is feasible and useful under the actual time, access and capability conditions.
- Participants can supply the contribution or obtain the missing owner decision.
- Consequential meanings and substantive conflicts are understood at the receiving use.
- The action yields an inspected result, including any unresolved input or failed condition.
- Both practices receive the useful return within the agreed permission and representation limits.
- Further commitments are chosen and provided explicitly; useful local independence remains available.

An unexplained referral failure or a promising contrast is enough to recognise the need. The conditions above support reliance on the constructed connection.

### CB.15:8 - Common Anti-Patterns and How to Avoid Them

| Failure in practice | Repair |
| --- | --- |
| Begin with a broad partnership agreement while the shared work is unclear. | Find one contribution both sides can use and try it under a small commitment. |
| Use a common word as evidence of common practice. | Compare its meaning and consequence in each side's actual case. |
| Resolve a resource conflict by rewriting terminology. | Return the real allocation or commitment question to its owner. |
| Fill a visit with presentations that could happen anywhere. | Arrange practitioner-guided access to the work and questions that need situated observation. |
| Treat visiting or understanding as being qualified to perform. | Obtain the separate learning, fit or professional result needed for later use. |
| Expand a successful pilot on the same unexamined volunteer effort. | Obtain provision for the new demand before promising the expanded service. |
| Let only the visitors receive the result. | Return useful observations, corrections and next decisions to the hosts too. |

### CB.15:9 - Consequences

Participants can overcome a specific disconnection while keeping their practices distinct. A concrete task exposes meanings, conditions and interests that general dialogue may miss. The returned result can justify a larger contribution or a useful stop.

Preparation, access and interpretation take effort. Some connections remain inappropriate under current conditions. A bounded result with an explicit gap may be more useful than a broad but unsupported partnership.

### CB.15:10 - Rationale

A small joint task gives differences a working consequence. People can ask why an action occurred, discover a missing dependency and judge whether a change helps their receiving work. This makes mutual understanding actionable without requiring complete agreement in advance.

Returning the result to both sides prevents the encounter from becoming an isolated experience for its attendees. Separating that return from further commitment lets the participants keep a useful narrow connection and obtain stronger governance or provision only when the work needs it.

### CB.15:11 - SoTA-Echoing

The practice question is how to obtain a useful contribution across communities whose meanings, interests and working conditions differ. The selected line begins with a small joint task and practitioner access, then uses its received result to choose continuation. General networking remains useful for contact; it is insufficient when the result depends on understanding or combining actual work.

| Source or developed treatment | Choice and effect in this pattern | Limit and accepted cost |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Etienne Wenger-Trayner, Beverly Wenger-Trayner, Phil Reid and Claude Bruderlein, *Communities of practice within and across organizations*, second edition (2023), pp.180–183. | **Adapt** useful initial activities, boundary projects and practitioner-guided visits in :4.1–4.3/:4.5. **Reject** substituting general host presentations for the work the visit needs to reveal. | The guide supplies practical constructions and experience, not a guaranteed trust or performance effect. Obtaining relevant access and returning the result adds preparation. |
| LDR.1:4.2–4.4 and OCE.11:4. | **Adopt** capable participants, a working brief, handling a changed situation and explicit coexistence with continuing work. :4.3/:4.7 obtains the conditions for the promised contribution. | Obtain the necessary authority and capacity from their actual owners. |
| F.9:4 and A.3.1.FT:4. | **Adopt** separate local meanings and a bounded translation use when recurring semantic correspondence matters. :4.4/:4.5 preserves the further work needed to use an observed practice. | Formal semantic comparison is needed only for the claim that depends on it; it does not create the social connection or establish professional transfer. |

The Guidebook contribution is an analytical adaptation with attribution to its authors, under [CC BY-NC 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc/4.0/); see the [source text](https://www.wenger-trayner.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/03/24-02-21-CoP-guidebook-second-edition-final-2.pdf). The constructed cases and supplier connections are this pattern's development.

Reopen the selected approach when the useful task cannot be made small enough, a changed practice defeats the translation, the exchange transfers unaccepted burden, or different evidence shows that the chosen form cannot supply the needed result.

### CB.15:12 - Relations

- **CB.1 and CB.2** help find the relevant need and people; **CB.4 and CB.5** provide shared activity and a usable contribution when the cross-practice connection is already sufficient.
- **CB.14** organises development and maintenance of a shared repertoire. **CB.13** supplies a common mandate; **CB.16** carries a collective external voice when needed.
- [LDR.1:4.4](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/LEADERSHIP-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#ldr144---perform-the-smallest-sufficient-working-conversation) supplies working conversations; [OCE.11:4](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/ORGANIZATION-CHANGE-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#oce114---solution) supplies coexistence with continuing commitments.
- [FPF F.9:4](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/FPF-Spec.md#f94---solution) supplies exact semantic comparison and the separate bounded-use question. Its relation is distinct from the connection between the communities.
- [FPF A.3.1.FT](https://github.com/ailev/FPF/blob/main/FPF-Spec.md#a31ft---qualify-method-fit-and-transfer) supplies method fit and transfer; **HCD** supplies the learning needed for a later performance.
- **CB.8** addresses conduct that prevents the exchange; **CB.10** helps judge the relationship's value and **CB.11** helps decide its continuation.

### CB.15:End

## CB.16 - Form and Carry a Collective Voice into a Decision

> **Type:** Community-building method pattern
> **Status:** Stable

### CB.16:1 - Problem frame

Use this pattern when a community needs to contribute to a decision that affects its practice, but individual comments do not adequately carry the shared concern. A profession is invited only after consequential design choices have been made. A provider asks a network for its view on a proposed change. Several local groups want to make one request while preserving different interests.

The governed object is a collective contribution to a specified receiving decision: the position, whom it represents, the authority to carry it, and the returned response. This is one branch of collective action. CB.13 constructs the decision mandate when needed; this pattern develops and carries the substantive position within it.

**First useful result:** participants can identify the receiving decision, the useful contribution they seek, the positions and uncertainties that matter, and who may present which position on whose behalf.

**What changes in practice:** a spokesperson can offer a grounded, authorised contribution and bring back an actionable answer. Members can distinguish what they asked for, what was agreed and what remains unresolved.

An individual's sufficiently useful expert answer can remain an individual contribution through CB.5. A community's internal allocation belongs to its existing decision arrangement. Use this pattern when representation of a shared position and its return from another decision are material to the result.

### CB.16:2 - Problem

An organiser may speak confidently for a community without knowing whether its members support the position. A popular statement may omit the practitioners most affected. Conversely, waiting for complete agreement can prevent any useful contribution before the decision closes.

Even a properly authorised message can fail because it asks only for recognition, gives the recipient no feasible action or reaches someone who cannot decide. A meeting may then be reported as influence although the receiving decision and working conditions remain unchanged.

The task is to build a contribution that both the represented participants and the decision-maker can use, carry it under a valid mandate, and return its actual effect and remaining work.

### CB.16:3 - Forces

| Force | Tension |
| --- | --- |
| Shared voice | A joint position can make a common concern intelligible while concealing real differences. |
| Expertise and authority | Knowledge supports the content; representation depends on the group's decision arrangement. |
| Timing | A decision window can be short while affected people need a usable opportunity to contribute. |
| Feasibility | An ambitious request may matter but require contributions that the community cannot provide or promise. |
| Negotiation | A representative needs enough discretion to respond while preserving the limits members accepted. |
| Influence | A received or accepted proposal still needs effective decisions and later observation of the practical result. |

### CB.16:4 - Solution

Locate the decision that can change the practice, develop the useful contribution with the people concerned, obtain the mandate to carry it and engage the actual recipient. Return the response and its consequences to the represented participants before treating the contribution as complete.

#### CB.16:4.1 - Locate the receiving decision and the practical gain

Recover the proposed change or recurring exclusion from an actual source: a decision proposal, current arrangement, invitation or account of the work. Identify who can decide, what is still open, the constraints and the time by which a contribution can matter. Confirm uncertainty with the recipient when access permits.

Ask practitioners what difference they need. Describe the receiving task or result and how the current arrangement helps or defeats it. “Include the technical writer before the interface is fixed so that operating instructions can influence its design” gives a recipient more to assess than “recognise the importance of writers”.

Use STR.5 when the contribution itself is unclear. It connects a recipient's need with a worthwhile result and the capability and resources that could sustain provision. Compare a serious alternative, such as an earlier bounded review, a later specialist service or a different provider. Keep the conditions each proposal needs.

Separate the community's aspiration from the facts on which it relies. Cases may demonstrate a recurring missing input without proving the effect of a proposed new arrangement. Obtain a sufficient source answer or the needed inquiry through CB.14/RMP where that stronger claim determines the choice.

If the decision is already closed, establish what can still change: implementation, correction, a later review or a new decision. Do not offer participants a choice that the recipient has no authority or opportunity to make.

#### CB.16:4.2 - Recover positions and affected differences

Define whose position is needed for this contribution. Use actual membership and constituency arrangements where they exist. Include people affected by the proposed action even when they do not attend ordinary meetings. CB.1 and OCE.2 help recover missing participants and different interests.

Provide enough of the proposal and its consequences for people to respond. Ask for the change they want, the case or reason behind it, what they can accept and what would make the proposal unworkable. Choose accessible routes and timing proportionate to the decision; one open meeting may not reach dispersed or less confident contributors.

Separate agreement, disagreement and absence of a response. An unanswered invitation supplies no assent. A well-attended discussion supplies useful positions, but its participants do not automatically represent every practitioner in the field.

Investigate differences that could change the request. Some may concern the same aim under different conditions; others involve competing priorities or a cost one group is asked to bear. Preserve the consequential difference in the proposal or in a clearly attributed dissent.

Agree what may be quoted, summarised or disclosed. A confidential case can inform the question without being identifiable in the public position. Obtain permitted evidence sufficient for the claim, or narrow it to what the participants can support.

#### CB.16:4.3 - Develop a position the recipient can act on

Construct the contribution around the actual decision. Explain the current difficulty, the proposed change, why it helps the receiving work, the evidence and limits, and the support needed to make it feasible. State what response is requested: a decision, a change to a proposal, a trial, information or a further conversation.

Compare materially different options when the choice remains open. Include the useful part of the current arrangement and the loss each change introduces. A narrower feasible request may obtain a needed contribution sooner; a broader request may be necessary if the narrow one leaves the real problem intact. Explain the choice to the represented participants.

Make the resource implications visible. State what the community can offer within its actual commitments and what must come from the recipient or another owner. A group of practitioners can propose protected review time; it cannot create that allocation by promising enthusiasm.

Prepare a joint position that can carry meaningful disagreement. Depending on the group's rules, it may be a supported majority proposal with a minority account, a common minimum with stated unresolved questions, or several attributed alternatives. Use a joint position only at the scope the participants have authorised.

If the contribution is advice, say what it can establish and which decision remains with its receiver. A request to be consulted may be justified by a useful professional contribution; it does not by itself establish an exclusive right to represent the domain.

#### CB.16:4.4 - Obtain and bound the representation mandate

Use the existing decision arrangement or obtain the needed one through CB.13. Establish who may approve the position, which participants it represents, the authorised spokesperson, the subject and duration, and how members can correct or withdraw the mandate.

Give the representative workable discretion. State what they may clarify or negotiate, the conditions they must preserve and which changes must return for a further decision. A mandate that only says “speak for us” leaves both the speaker and members exposed to incompatible expectations.

Distinguish approval of the position from approval of commitments. The community may authorise a request while each employer, site or individual retains the right to provide its own time or resources. Obtain those decisions when the negotiated action depends on them.

Prepare the spokesperson to use the mandate. They need the reasons, evidence limits, different positions, likely questions and a way to return an unexpected proposal. LDR.1 supplies the working brief and explanation. Where the task needs more than one kind of knowledge, obtain a supporting contributor.

When a collective mandate cannot be obtained within the decision window, choose a truthful narrower contribution. Named willing contributors may offer their own joint view under their own authority. State whom it represents; a community name or large mailing list does not enlarge that scope.

#### CB.16:4.5 - Engage the recipient and handle a changed proposal

Confirm that the recipient can receive the contribution in time and through a usable route. Present the request with its practical reason, represented constituency and mandate. Provide the evidence needed for the decision at a level the recipient can inspect.

If the first contact cannot decide, ask for the appropriate receiving route. Where no accessible route or timely decision remains, return that limit to the participants so they can choose a later opportunity or end this episode.

Use the exchange to test understanding. Ask the recipient to identify the question they can decide and any missing condition. Recover objections from the work: an unavailable resource, another affected interest, an unsupported claim or a different decision boundary may each require a different response.

Work within the authorised discretion. A representative can clarify the case or compare an allowed option. A materially different commitment or claim returns to the people who can approve it. State the pending condition to the recipient so that a provisional discussion is not mistaken for an agreement.

Keep disagreement intelligible. The recipient may decline the request for a reason members contest. Return that reason and the remaining possible action; do not replace it with an unsupported account of the recipient's motives.

If the recipient offers a trial or accepts a change, identify the actual decision, owner, scope and next contribution. Obtain the assignments, access and resources through their owners. An invitation to discuss, a favourable comment and an effective decision remain different results.

#### CB.16:4.6 - Return the answer and make the next action usable

Report the contribution and response to the represented participants. Explain what the recipient accepted, rejected, changed or left open; the reason and evidence supplied; any commitment made within the mandate; and any new question requiring the group's decision.

Give dissenting or previously absent participants a usable opportunity to correct a misrepresentation. Correct a materially inaccurate account with the recipient as well as within the community. CB.13 supplies reconsideration or replacement when the representative exceeded the mandate.

Return implementation work to its actual owners. Use OCE.14 for a bounded revision of an existing arrangement and OCE.6 for needed assignments and enabling conditions. CB.14 supplies a new practice contribution; HCD supplies a needed learning result. If the community has only obtained an invitation to further work, preserve that bounded gain and the unfinished result.

Agree an observation that can tell whether the expected practical change occurred. For example, check whether writers were actually included before the specified decision and whether their contribution was usable. CB.10 can connect those observations with the community's value question. A causal claim about the whole intervention still requires suitable evidence.

Close the representation episode when the answer, effective commitments and remaining work are understood by their recipients. A refusal or a bounded agreement may complete this episode even though the community's wider aim remains open.

#### CB.16:4.7 - Reconsider the position and its continuing mandate

Reopen the affected position when the external proposal changes, new evidence defeats a reason, a previously missed constituency matters, the required provision is absent or the mandate expires. Recover which part of the earlier contribution remains useful.

For continuing representation, agree how the community will keep the representative informed, obtain returns and replace them. The burden may justify a standing arrangement through CB.13; occasional external requests may need only bounded assignments.

Preserve independence among the represented groups. They may support one shared request while retaining different practices and other positions. A cross-community response through CB.15 needs each side's actual mandate at the scope attributed to it.

Independent carriers of a practice can use a published argument or contribute an attributed response without joining a common organisation. Their use or silence supplies no authority for a spokesperson to claim that they endorse the collective position.

### CB.16:5 - Archetypal Grounding

#### CB.16:5.1 - Technical writers ask to enter a design decision earlier

In a constructed engineering firm, a writers' community finds three cases where documentation began after the operating interface had been fixed. The writers had to describe confusing actions that an earlier review might have changed. They recover the cases with the relevant project staff and obtain permission to use the examples internally.

The community compares two proposals. A stronger final documentation service preserves the current development schedule but leaves interface changes late. A bounded writer review before the interface decision could expose explanation problems earlier; it requires time from both a writer and the design team.

Members support trying the early review on two projects. Some prefer a permanent writer on every team, while others cannot provide additional time under current allocations. The position retains those differences and requests a limited trial with protected time, a defined review question and a return after the two project decisions.

Under its member decision arrangement, the community authorises a spokesperson to request that trial. The mandate permits clarification of timing and review material; it does not permit promising staff from every department. The spokesperson brings the two alternatives and the cases to the engineering manager who owns the relevant process decision.

The manager proposes involving all writers in a weekly meeting. That exceeds the offered contribution and lacks time provision. The spokesperson returns the proposal for discussion. The members retain the bounded review, and the manager instead approves two project-specific sessions. The participating managers allocate the agreed time and the two writers accept the work.

The community receives the decision, conditions and named project returns. In the first session, the writer's explanation of an operating sequence prompts the team to clarify an interface label before its decision. Before the second session, its assigned writer becomes unavailable. The project reaches the interface decision before a replacement is obtained and reports that the early review did not occur. The community returns that provision failure to the participating manager for the next proposed use, retaining the useful first contribution. The two returns do not establish the programme's overall effect.

#### CB.16:5.2 - A shared response can preserve a minority concern

A provider proposes moving a network's support sessions to a new time. Most participating groups prefer the new slot; a smaller group would lose its only accessible session. The network gathers each group's reason and confirms which service decisions the provider can make.

Its authorised position supports the new slot only if a usable alternative remains for the affected group. The spokesperson is permitted to compare equivalent alternatives but must return any proposal that removes access. The provider offers a recorded presentation.

The smaller group explains that it needs live questions about its cases, so a recording does not supply the missing contribution. The spokesperson returns that answer within the mandate. The provider agrees to test a second live session if an appropriate host can be assigned. The network reports the conditional result and the unfilled host requirement; access is not described as secured until that condition is met.

#### CB.16:5.3 - Named contributors speak without claiming the whole field

A platform invites comments from repair practitioners on a proposed information form. Several members of a loose network have useful experience, but the network has no authorised common position and many independent repairers are outside it.

The willing contributors submit a joint technical account in their own names, stating the settings their examples cover and the limits of their agreement. They make the permitted account available for others to use or challenge. The response is a real contribution; it is not presented as the view of all repairers.

A later proposal to maintain an ongoing representative relationship would require a mandate and provision through CB.13. The earlier signatures do not settle that new question.

#### CB.16:5.4 - A request already within the organiser's assignment

A club needs its existing room booking corrected. The authorised organiser contacts the venue, obtains the correction and informs participants. The task is complete under the existing assignment. It does not need a new collective position or representation process.

### CB.16:6 - Bias-Annotation

A spokesperson may overrepresent confident contributors, people available at the consultation time or the position most convenient to a sponsor. Invitations from a prestigious recipient can also encourage overclaiming the community's mandate. Recover affected differences, show the scope of support and preserve useful dissent. Keep represented people able to challenge the speaker's account and the claims open to contrary evidence.

### CB.16:7 - Conformance Checklist

For reliance on a collective contribution, establish:

- The receiving decision, authorised recipient and opportunity to affect it are known or their uncertainty is explicit.
- The practical gain and proposed action are supported at the claimed scope, with a serious alternative where it changes the choice.
- Affected positions, meaningful dissent and missing responses remain distinguishable.
- The position is approved and the spokesperson has a valid mandate with usable discretion and return conditions.
- Promised resources and commitments have their actual owners' decisions.
- The response is returned accurately, with the effective result and unfinished work.
- Misrepresentation, new conditions and expired authority can reopen the affected contribution.

A recurring exclusion or an external invitation is enough to recognise the opportunity. It does not supply the authority or evidence required for reliance.

### CB.16:8 - Common Anti-Patterns and How to Avoid Them

| Failure in practice | Repair |
| --- | --- |
| Ask for recognition without a contribution the recipient can assess. | Explain the receiving work, proposed change, benefit, cost and needed support. |
| Treat the most visible members as the whole constituency. | Recover affected positions and state the actual representation scope. |
